**Revisit when.** Sessions gain their own needs-attention states beyond the lane's (D-083 declined this), or the lens is asked to show more than one blocked lane per mission — the projection names the first in creation order, which is the precedence rule, not a ranking.

## D-094 — Every chat answers "what are you doing?" without being opened

**Context.** D-083 gave a lane parallel conversations and D-084–D-090 gave them rows and tabs, but a background chat said nothing about itself unless it was blocked ("· needs you"). Choosing where to type in a three-chat approach meant opening each one. The owner's Phase 2 direction names the felt problem: chats should read as a shared workspace, not as queued conversations — which begins with each one saying what it is doing, what it has touched, and when two of them are on the same ground.

**Decision.**

- **Four states, one word each, precedence by what a person must act on:** `needs you` (its turn is blocked — the existing warn-toned words, unchanged), `working` (its turn is live), `queued · {n}` (direction waiting for the shared workspace), and idle — which renders nothing, because ten quiet chats each announcing "idle" is apparatus. The words appear on the tree row, the session tab, and the overview link; the *selected* chat's row says nothing, its state being the room's state line. Warn tone only for needs-you; working and queued recede to `--text-3`.
- **A chat's footprint is what its turns committed.** Per-chat changed files are derived from checkpoints through the executions that belong to the chat — git's account, latest checkpoint winning per path — and rendered as `{n} files` on the overview link, with `last heard {time}` beside a working chat so "working" carries its freshness.
- **Overlap is warned from evidence, never predicted.** When two chats' checkpoint histories have changed the same file, the approach overview says so in one warn-toned sentence — the chats and the path, further overlapping files counted quietly. Nothing guesses what a direction *might* touch: a warning that can cry wolf teaches people to ignore the one that matters.

**Alternatives.** Predictive conflict detection from direction text or live tool activity (rejected: unverifiable claims in a product whose whole culture is evidence); a per-chat state dot (prohibited — status is words, DESIGN.md#status-semantics); announcing idle (rejected as noise); filtering the evidence panel's file tree per chat (rejected: the worktree is genuinely shared, and a filtered tree would say otherwise).

**Consequences.** `sessionActivity`, `sessionChangedFiles`, and `contestedAcrossSessions` are renderer derivations over the detail payload the room already polls — no contract change, no new fetch. The overlap sentence is also the groundwork for the concurrency slice: "serialize or confirm when edits conflict" needs exactly these facts.

**Revisit when.** Simultaneous execution inside one approach is attempted — that is its own slice, it reverses D-083's deliberate "one live turn per lane," and it must answer the checkpoint-attribution problem (a second writer in one worktree makes `captureCheckpoint`'s whole-tree commit attribute one chat's half-done edits to another) before any scheduler work begins.

## D-095 — A chat can run alongside the workspace's turn, read-only

**Context.** D-083 made the workspace take turns: one live execution per lane, every sibling chat's direction queueing behind it. Correct for writes — one worktree has one git index, and checkpoint capture commits the whole tree, so a second simultaneous writer would record one chat's half-done edits as another's evidence — but it made every conversation feel like a queue, which the owner's Phase 2 direction named as the thing to fix. The requirements as stated ("same worktree" and "simultaneous execution") are contradictory for writers; this decision resolves the tension at the only honest line there is.

**Decision.**

- **Writes stay exclusive; reads run alongside.** An execution carries `access: write | read`. The lane's partial unique index now guards write executions only, and a new per-session index guards one turn per conversation whatever its access — a session's turns share one harness transcript, and two at once would fork it.
- **Alongside is the baton holder's act.** Sending in a chat while another chat's write turn runs asks the controller once, inline in the composer: Queue (the default), or Run alongside · read-only. It spends the host machine's quota exactly as immediate dispatch does, and quota is what the baton already gates; anyone else's direction queues exactly as before. A question gone moot — the running turn ended while it was on screen — submits the ordinary way rather than swallowing the click (the sessions spec caught the swallow through the poll's two-second lag).
- **A read turn may look and speak, never act.** Every permission request it raises is denied at the runner the moment it arrives, with the reason on the harness's own channel — no card, no waiting, no baton involvement. It captures no checkpoint: committing the worktree mid-write-turn would be the exact fabrication that keeps writes exclusive. It declares no boundary from any source, and the control plane refuses one that arrives anyway — a waiting handoff must never complete on a read turn's word while the write turn is mid-tool-call.
- **The workspace's story stays the write turn's.** Lane and list state, the stall overlay, turn-taking dispatch, and idle-transfer checks all read write executions only; a read turn's liveness is its own chat's `working` word (D-094). Its completion still nudges the dispatcher, because a direction deferred behind its own chat's answer is waiting on exactly that moment — and the dispatcher re-checks everything, so the nudge can never start a second writer.
- **Stop names the conversation.** With two live turns in a lane, the wire carries the session: the state line's Stop still means the workspace's turn, and the chat answering read-only carries its own quiet Stop while on screen. The runner's active-turn map is keyed by execution now — workstream keying would let the second turn overwrite the first, and a stop or an approval answer would find the wrong turn.

**Alternatives.** Per-chat worktrees, Conductor's shape (rejected: the owner's constraint is one branch, one worktree per approach — that identity is the product); two writers with tool-event-attributed checkpoints (rejected: bash writes files no tool event names; unattributable evidence is fabricated evidence); session kinds chosen at creation ("review chat") (rejected: sessions are words-first, and the read/write question belongs to the moment of sending, not the chat's identity); auto-running alongside without asking (rejected: it spends quota and changes what "send" means — the person chooses).

**Consequences.** `ExecutionSchema.access` is required, so every fixture states it. A read turn advances its chat's harness session — the next write turn of that chat remembers the conversation, which is the point. The fake harness honors the deny path, so the whole story is provable deterministically: `alongside.test.ts` (dispatch, refusals, state, stop, boundary), `execution.test.ts` (deny-at-machine, no checkpoint, no boundary), and the approval spec's in-window run (a read turn answered beside a blocked write turn, one card, the file only after approval).

**Revisit when.** A non-controller wants a read turn (the baton gate is a quota judgment, not a safety one — the safety is the deny policy); or read turns want tools beyond the CLI's default-allowed reads, at which point the deny list becomes a policy surface rather than a constant.

## D-096 — A check is attributed to the chat whose checkpoint it ran at

**Context.** Checks are lane evidence: they run in the approach's one worktree and bind to the revision they proved (D-043, D-082). With several chats sharing that worktree, the owner asked for per-chat verification — "which chat produced which evidence." The naïve reading is a lie waiting to be recorded: Novus does not know which tests cover which files, and the worktree at any revision holds **every** chat's work so far, so "chat A's tests passed" would claim a scope no run ever had. D-083 declined per-session verification outright; this decision does the honest half of it.

**Decision.**

- **Attribution by trigger, never by content.** Every check already pins the revision it proved; every checkpoint belongs to one turn and every turn to one conversation. The join — check → `checkpointSha` → checkpoint → execution → session — names the chat *at whose checkpoint* the check ran. That sentence is the ceiling of what is knowable, and the words on screen say exactly it.
- **A renderer derivation, no wire change.** `sessionOfCheck` / `sessionChecks` compute the join from the payload the room already holds. Checks still *run* as lane evidence against the shared worktree — readiness, staleness, and re-running are untouched.
- **Two surfaces.** The approach overview's chat rows gain `{p}/{n} checks at its checkpoints`; the evidence ledger's provenance line gains `· "{title}"'s checkpoint` while the lane holds more than one conversation. A check with no recorded revision, or one whose revision no checkpoint claims, is attributed to nobody rather than to a guess.

**Alternatives.** Content-based attribution — mapping test files to changed files (rejected: Novus cannot know a project's coverage topology, and a wrong attribution is fabricated evidence); running checks in per-chat sandboxes at the chat's own revision (rejected: that is per-chat worktrees through the back door, and D-095 just drew that line); tagging checks with a session id on the wire (rejected for now: the revision join derives it losslessly, and a stored duplicate can drift).

**Consequences.** An automatic check (D-082) lands at the checkpoint that triggered it, so it reads as that chat's row — which is the intuition the owner named, made true by construction. A participant-run check attributes to whichever chat's checkpoint the worktree stood at, which is honest even when surprising. "Screenshot: captured"-style visual evidence stays out of scope until the artifact store (D-022) exists.

**Revisit when.** CI ingestion arrives (external checks bind to commits, so the same join works — but their revisions may be merge commits no checkpoint claims), or a project declares which checks cover which paths, at which point content-scoped attribution stops being a guess.

## D-097 — Chats own files, and provably disjoint owners write in parallel

**Context.** D-095 drew the line at one writer per worktree because a second writer made checkpoint evidence unattributable. The owner's ownership proposal changes the math: if each chat declares which files it owns, attribution stops being a guess — inside the declaration. This decision builds that layer end to end: declaration, approval, enforcement, parallel dispatch, per-scope evidence, drift, and the integration pass.

**Decision.**

- **A scope is a session's declared file set** — repository-relative glob patterns (`server/**`, `src/*.ts`), bounded, refusing absolutes and `..`. Setting or clearing it is the **baton holder's act**, event-recorded: a scope is standing write authority inside its patterns, and standing authority is the baton's to grant. The dialog's proposal is derived from the chat's own touched files — evidence, never a guess from task text — and the running turn keeps the scope pinned at its dispatch (D-043's pattern).
- **Provably disjoint scopes write in parallel.** One matcher, exported from the contracts package, is every judge — the runner enforcing, the server admitting, the composer predicting — because a drifted copy would be a security judgment made twice. Disjointness is deliberately conservative: literal path prefixes must diverge, and any pattern with no literal prefix overlaps everything; a wrong "overlap" costs a queue wait, a wrong "disjoint" costs the evidence. Any unscoped party, running or arriving, means exclusivity exactly as before scopes existed. The write-exclusivity index is replaced by the dispatch transaction under the mission's advisory lock, since the judgment reads scopes no index can.
- **Enforcement is the scope answering instead of the person.** A scoped turn's path-naming tools (Write, Edit, NotebookEdit) are decided at the runner: inside the scope auto-allowed — approving the scope *was* the approval — outside it auto-denied with the reason on the harness's own channel; no card either way, and the stream's prompt boundary is suppressed with it. **Bash still asks a human**, always: a shell command declares its targets nowhere, and pretending otherwise would be enforcement theater.
- **Evidence stays per chat.** A scoped checkpoint stages and commits only its own paths, under a per-worktree capture lock so parallel captures cannot interleave. Dirty paths in a running sibling's declared scope are that sibling's work mid-flight and stay silent; dirty paths in *nobody's* scope are **drift** — named on the checkpoint, warn-toned in the trace, never committed by the turn that observed them.
- **The lane transfers on its last writer.** With two turns live, one writer's boundary is not the lane's: a waiting handoff completes only when every other write turn is finished — a blocked one counts as at-boundary, as PRODUCT.md always said.
- **Integration is the drain's act.** Scoped turns skip per-checkpoint auto-verification — a check against a tree a sibling is still mutating binds a verdict to a revision that does not describe it. When the lane's last turn ends: a clean worktree runs the declared checks once against the settled result; a dirty one reports `integration.blocked` with the paths, because a check against a tree no recorded revision describes is evidence about nothing. Settling drift is a person's act — an unscoped turn adopts it, deliberately, since running one *is* the manual integration.

**Alternatives.** Deriving scopes from task text (rejected: a guess wearing an approval); enforcing Bash by post-hoc revert of out-of-scope changes (rejected: with a parallel sibling in flight, "this turn's bash did it" and "the sibling is mid-edit" are indistinguishable, and reverting the wrong one destroys work); per-chat worktrees (rejected again — the shared folder is the constraint this whole layer exists to honor); storing a turn's scope on the execution row (not needed: the command payload pins it, the D-043 way).

**Consequences.** `Session.scope` and `Checkpoint.driftPaths` are on the wire; `integration.blocked` joins the runner event union. The composer's queue-or-alongside question disappears exactly where the server would dispatch in parallel. A participant-run check during parallel writers still exercises a moving tree — the staleness model marks it against head, and the ledger's honesty rules already cover it — named in PROGRESS rather than hidden.

**Revisit when.** A project can declare which checks cover which paths (per-scope verification stops being a lie); or real use shows the conservative disjointness refusing pairs people constantly want, at which point the matcher earns segments-aware overlap analysis rather than prefix comparison.

## D-098 — The preview is a surface inside Novus, not a hand-off to the browser

**Context.** D-045 built the loopback-only preview bridge and ended it at `shell.openExternal`: the validated address was handed to the operating system's browser. That made "open the app I just started" a context switch out of the product, and it made the room unable to say anything about what happened next — whether the page loaded, whether the server died under it, whether the thing on screen belongs to the process the room is talking about. The remaining runtime gap was a person importing a project, starting it, and *using* it without leaving the mission.

**Decision.** An embedded preview surface, as a `WebContentsView` owned entirely by the Electron main process.

- **Every D-045 gate stands, plus one.** The address must parse as loopback `http`/`https`, credential-free and control-character-free, and must be one a **live** run process of this workstream actually reported — stricter than the external bridge, which accepts any address the workstream ever reported: an embedded page whose process has ended would be Novus showing a page nothing is serving. Validation and the view both live in the main process; the renderer reserves a rectangle, reads a status, and can only name a URL the room already showed it. There is no address bar and no verb that takes one.
- **Navigation is confined to the approved origin.** The top frame may go nowhere but the exact origin the preview opened on — scheme, hostname, and port — enforced on `will-navigate`, `will-redirect`, and the window-open handler in the main process, through the same parser as the open gate. A same-origin `window.open` navigates in place; everything else opens nowhere. This is a view of one running application, not a browser.
- **The embedded page is treated as untrusted.** No preload, no Node, no `window.novus`, a sandboxed renderer, an isolated in-memory session per workstream, every OS permission request denied without a dialog, downloads refused. It is the person's own dev server, which is exactly as trustworthy as whatever the harness last wrote into it.
- **The page loading is not readiness.** The declared readiness signal stays the only thing that may say *ready* (D-045); the preview's own phases — loading, ready, unreachable, crashed, stopped — claim facts about one page, and its `ready` claims one HTTP response rendered, never that the application is correct. When the reporting process ends the preview says so in the process's own words and offers the honest next action; a native view sits above the renderer's DOM, so any state told in words takes the view down first.
- **Closing the preview closes a window onto the app, never the app.** Stop stays the Run surface's verb; reopening reuses the live process and its view state; a restart re-validates and discards the old view, whose state described a server that is gone.

**Alternatives.** Keeping `shell.openExternal` only (rejected: it is the gap this slice closes — no interaction surface, no honest state, no evidence of what was on screen); the `<webview>` tag (rejected: renderer-owned, deprecated posture, requires enabling `webviewTag` on the app window, and the harness cannot drive it); an in-renderer `<iframe>` (rejected: the app shell is `file://` with the bridge in scope, and framing an attacker-influencable page next to `window.novus` is the exposure D-048 exists to refuse); a second `BrowserWindow` (viable, rejected for the product reason: the point is the preview inside the room, beside the terminal, the trace, and the evidence).

**Consequences.** The bridge grows a `workspace.preview` block (open, setBounds, hide, reload, close, status) validated in the main process; DESIGN.md's Run entry and *App running* overlay now open the preview in the room; the external-browser bridge stays as a secondary "Open in browser". The renderer must hide the view whenever DOM would overlay its rectangle — dialogs, the narrow-width dock — because a native view cannot be covered by the page. A screenshot of the preview is not evidence of application correctness and is deliberately not captured in this slice.

**Revisit when.** Cloud workspaces make a preview something other than loopback; or the room needs more than one preview at a time, which is when the one-view model earns per-tab views.

## D-099 — A decision publishes as a tracked pull request, and Novus never merges

**Context.** D-075 ended at a prepared projection: title, body, branches, rendered read-only, with the tracked `PullRequest` row named as a later slice. Everything downstream of a decision — opening the request, getting it reviewed, watching it merge — happened outside the product with no record. And the projection hid a lie by omission: nothing has ever pushed a mission branch, so the head branch a request would name is one the repository host has never seen.

**Decision.**

- **The remote-head guarantee comes first.** A new runner command, `push_branch`, pushes the mission branch to the host up to the decided checkpoint, through the same per-operation credential pipe the clone path uses — a second, deliberately separate mint with `contents: write`, never a widening of the read-only clone credential, never stored, never logged. Success is reported as a `workspace.pushed` event carrying the branch and the exact SHA, recorded onto the workstream as `remote_head_sha`; failure settles the command with its reason and moves nothing. The create route refuses a pull request until the remote head **equals the decided checkpoint** — a request naming a revision the host does not serve would be the product's central lie.
- **The row starts at publication, exactly as D-075 promised.** `pull_requests` (prefix `pr_`): the decision it publishes, the host's number and URL, state (`draft`, `ready`, `merged`, `closed`), mergeability (`unknown`, `clean`, `conflict`), requested reviewers, ingested review threads, who created it, who merged it, and — per D-075's stored-draft rejection — a **snapshot of exactly the title and body that were sent**, while the prepared projection stays live for the un-opened case. At most one non-closed request per workstream, by partial unique index.
- **Novus opens drafts and never anything readier than a person asked for.** Creation always opens a *draft*; `mark ready` is a person's own act, as is requesting reviewers. All of it is `pr.manage` — Mission Admin and Operator, per PRODUCT.md's table, enforced server-side. The pull request is authored by the GitHub App installation; the human who clicked is attributed in the body and the event record (App = machine access, OAuth = identity — ARCHITECTURE.md).
- **There is no merge verb, structurally.** No route, no bridge call, no runner command, no provider method. Asserted the way D-063 asserts the absent delete: tests ask for a merge and require 404, and the vocabulary tests pin that the words do not parse. Merging happens on GitHub, by humans; Novus observes the result through ingestion and says who did it.
- **Ingestion is poll-first for the local-first deployment.** A desktop control plane has no public webhook endpoint, so a poller (control-plane sweep, same shape as the reliability sweep) refreshes open requests — state, draft flag, mergeability, review threads — recording changes as events with `actor.kind: external`. Review threads are ingested **read-only** and bounded: commenting from Novus needs user-attributed OAuth and stays unbuilt with `review.comment`; resolving happens on the host and is reflected. ARCHITECTURE.md's webhook path remains the destination for a deployed control plane.
- **Mission state.** `pull_request_open` becomes real and sits ahead of `decision_recorded` in the projection while a draft or ready request exists. A merged or closed request drops the mission back to `decision_recorded`, whose sentence then names how publication ended ("published as PR #4, merged by {who}") — because `completed` is a terminal lifecycle with receipt snapshotting this build has not earned, and pretending otherwise would be a second "done" lie. Recorded here as the slice's honest residue.
- **The fake provider grows the same seams.** In-memory pull requests with numbers, reviewers, threads, mergeability, and — under `NOVUS_FAKE_GITHUB` only — test routes that let a suite *be* GitHub: post a review comment, merge, close. Deterministic proof of the pipeline; the live adapter implements the identical interface against api.github.com (mark-ready is the codebase's first GraphQL call, because REST cannot un-draft), proven only by the opt-in live spec.

**Alternatives.** Opening non-draft requests directly (rejected: readiness is a human claim); merging from Novus behind a capability (rejected outright: PRODUCT.md's phase 7 — "Merging happens on GitHub, by humans, in V0 — Novus tracks the merge and never performs it"); webhooks now (rejected for the local-first deployment: no reachable endpoint, and a poller proves the ingestion semantics webhooks will inherit); storing review threads in their own table with threading (deferred with `review.comment`: bounded JSON on the row carries the read-only reflection until commenting exists); auto-pushing on every checkpoint (rejected: publication is an intentional act at a decision, not a side effect of work).

**Consequences.** `CapabilitySchema` gains `pr.manage`; `MissionStateSchema` gains `pull_request_open`; the runner vocabulary gains `push_branch` (widened in schema.sql's one place); `RunnerEventSchema` gains `workspace.pushed`; `workstreams` gains `remote_head_sha`; the wire gains `pullRequest` and `branchPush`; the GitHub App needs `pull_requests: write`, so an existing installation needs re-approval. The provider `kind` union stops lying: `"github"` joins it and the live adapter says so.

**Revisit when.** A deployed control plane exists (webhooks replace the poll as primary); `review.comment` is built (threads earn a table and two-way flow); or the merged state earns the real `completed` lifecycle with a snapshotted receipt.

## D-100 — Completion moves in-house: Novus operates the pull request, and a person merges from inside the room

**Context.** D-099 made the absence of a merge verb structural, reading PRODUCT.md's "merging happens on GitHub, by humans, in V0" as "not from Novus." On sight of the working draft flow, the owner reversed that reading and named the model: Conductor merges from inside the app once checks, reviews, and comments are handled, with GitHub performing the act underneath; Codex reviews pull requests in its own panel. The product line that survives intact is the original one — Novus must never **silently** merge code. What falls is the absence of the verb: an explicit, gated, recorded human act is not a silent one, and sending a person to a browser tab to perform the one act the whole flow led to was ceremony, not safety.

**Decision.** Novus becomes the complete interface for operating the pull request; GitHub remains the source of truth.

- **Merge readiness is one aggregated projection** on the pull-request row, the Conductor-shaped gate: the host's required and non-required checks with their runs, the review decision (approvals, changes requested), mergeability and conflicts, how far the branch is behind or ahead of its base, unresolved review threads — and beside them Novus's own facts: the verification tallies at the decided revision and the decision's accepted risks. Rendered as the readiness ledger in the evidence style: every unmet item is a named blocker, never a summary color.
- **Completion verbs, all `pr.manage`, all host-performed, all event-recorded as the person's act with the host's result recorded as external**: **Merge**, offering only the repository's own allowed methods (merge, squash, rebase — read from the host, never assumed); **Update branch** for behind-base; **Close without merging**; **Delete remote branch**, offered only after merge or close and never automatic; and the existing **Archive** (D-063) offered as the post-merge act. The runner is uninvolved — these are host-API acts, not workspace acts.
- **Two-tier gating, and the tier is the honesty.** A merge the host itself cannot perform — conflicts, a failing *required* check — is refused server-side in words. Every other blocker — changes requested, unresolved threads, Novus's own unverified checks, non-required failures — is stated on the confirmation, and a person may proceed deliberately: the confirm restates the outstanding blockers and the recorded event carries exactly what was accepted, so "merged with two open comments and one unverified check" is a durable sentence, not a hidden one. Silent is the enemy; sovereign is the point.
- **Review operates in-house too**: the pull request's commits and changed files with unified and split diffs; inline comments written from Novus — authored by the App installation and attributed in the body as "{login} via Novus" until user-token attribution exists; GitHub review threads listed and **resolved** from Novus (the GraphQL threads API, which also fixes D-099's read-only threads showing as open); and **Send to chat**, which turns a review comment into a prefilled direction for a chosen conversation — Conductor's send-feedback-to-agent, with Novus attribution.
- **Metadata is editable**: title, description, labels — host-patched, event-recorded.

**Alternatives.** Keeping D-099's structural absence (rejected by the owner on sight, with the Conductor/Codex parity named); auto-deleting the branch on merge (rejected: deletion stays its own explicitly approved act); blocking merge on every blocker (rejected: a person may accept named risks deliberately — the guarantee is that nothing is silent, not that nothing is theirs to decide); commenting via user OAuth tokens now (deferred: App-authored with in-body attribution ships the flow; identity-attributed comments arrive with `review.comment`).

**Consequences.** PRODUCT.md's phase 7 is rewritten to completion-in-house with the never-silent rule stated; DESIGN.md's references carry the 2026-08-09 re-observation of Conductor's checks tab. The D-099 tests that pinned the *absence* of a merge verb are rewritten deliberately under this entry into tests of the *gated* verb — the refusals (not ready, host-refused, wrong capability) and the recorded acceptance of named blockers. `pull_requests: write` covers merge, update-branch, close, comments, and labels, so no new App scope is needed; branch deletion needs `contents: write`, already held.

**Revisit when.** `review.comment` lands user-attributed identity for comments; or branch-protection rules Novus cannot read make the two-tier gate misjudge what the host will refuse.

## D-101 — Comments wear the person's own name, and the host may knock

**Context.** Two residues D-100 named. A comment from Novus reached GitHub as `novus-kartik[bot]` with the person's name folded into the body — attribution as prose, not identity. And the host's story arrived only by poll: fine for a local desktop, fifteen seconds of blindness for anything deployed, and ARCHITECTURE.md had always said webhooks were the destination.

**Decision.**

- **The person's own OAuth token authors their comments.** Sign-in already runs the person through GitHub OAuth for identity; the flow now requests `read:user repo` and the exchange's access token is **stored on the user row** — held by the control plane alone, never served to a client, a runner, or an event, refreshed at each sign-in, and never cleared by a flow that carried none, so a re-auth under the old scope cannot delete a grant already made. The comment route reads it at the moment of the call: token held → the person's token performs the REST call and GitHub shows *them* as the author, body unprefixed; no token — a row from before the scope existed — the App authors it with the "{login} via Novus" prefix, exactly the D-100 fallback, and the recorded event says which (`authoredAs: user | app`). The deterministic flow stores `gho_fake_{login}` and the fake host reads the author off the token's identity, so attribution is testable end to end. The scope widening is a consent change: existing sessions keep working, and attribution begins at each person's next sign-in.
- **A webhook receiver, signature-first.** `POST /webhooks/github` exists only when `NOVUS_GITHUB_WEBHOOK_SECRET` is set — a local-first control plane has nothing GitHub could reach, and without the secret the endpoint answers 404 rather than existing unverifiable. The signature is HMAC-SHA256 **over the raw bytes** (the route's own scope parses to buffer; a re-serialized body would verify nothing), compared in constant time; a forged knock is 401 and syncs nothing. A verified knock for `pull_request`, `pull_request_review`, `pull_request_review_comment`, or `issue_comment` syncs **exactly the named request** through the same per-row path the poll uses — the webhook is a poke, never a second ingestion truth — an untracked request is 204, not news. The App manifest declares the hook and those events only when `publicBaseUrl` is not loopback. The poll remains, demoted to what ARCHITECTURE.md always called it: the fallback.

**Alternatives.** A separate "connect comments" OAuth flow with incremental scope (rejected for V0: a second consent ceremony for one capability; the sign-in scope says what Novus does); encrypting the stored token at rest (deferred honestly: the control plane has no key store; the row is named in ARCHITECTURE.md's secret-placement table and the local-first database is the same trust domain as the .env beside it); applying webhook payloads directly as state (rejected: two writers of one truth drift — the payload only names what to re-read); accepting unsigned webhooks in dev (rejected: an unverified write path is a write path).

**Consequences.** `users.github_token` joins the schema and ARCHITECTURE.md's secret-placement table; the D-100 comment tests are rewritten under this entry (author = the person, raw body; the App fallback pinned separately); config gains `githubWebhookSecret`; the sweep's body is extracted so one request can sync alone. Live proof of user-authored comments waits on the owner's next real sign-in under the new scope — recorded in PROGRESS, not pretended.

**Revisit when.** A deployed control plane runs the webhook in anger (delivery retries, out-of-order knocks); or a key store arrives and the stored token earns encryption at rest.

## D-102 — Settings after first run: the rail's gear, ⌘,, and the theme choice made reachable

**Context.** D-029 built the whole theme system — token-for-token light override, Light/Dark/System persisted, resolved before first paint — and put its only control on the first-run setup room. Once a person finished setup, no surface could change the theme again, and the light palette stayed "provisional until screen-proven" for lack of any way to reach it. The owner asked for the toggle, placed where desktop apps put it. The survey is unanimous — Linear, Notion, Slack, VS Code, GitHub Desktop, Conductor all keep appearance in a Settings/Preferences surface reached from the sidebar's account corner or the platform's `⌘,`, offering the Light/Dark/System triple; none put a sun/moon button in the working chrome.

**Decision.** A **Settings dialog** on the house dialog anatomy, holding the setup room's Theme row verbatim — same label, same description, same three-segment control, applied immediately, persisted in the same preference. It opens from two places and no more: a quiet **gear glyph in the rail's identity row** (the account corner DESIGN.md already owns — beside sign-out, where Conductor keeps it) and **`⌘,`**, the platform's own chord. The choice-set is one exported list consumed by both surfaces, so setup and Settings can never drift. A theme flip also repaints **live terminal panes**: the emulator copied token values out of CSS at pane creation (D-046), so `applyTheme` announces itself on the window and the dock hands every open pane the newly resolved palette — no stranded dark terminal in a light product.

**Alternatives.** A sun/moon toggle in the top bar (rejected: no surveyed desktop tool does this; the top-right corner belongs to the mission, and a two-state toggle cannot say "System"); a full Settings *window* (rejected for now: one row does not justify a window; the dialog grows rows when real preferences exist); reopening the setup room to change theme (rejected: setup is a first-run surface, and reaching it again would mean faking signed-out state).

**Consequences.** The light palette is finally screen-proven — populated room, rail, dialog, and a relaunch resolving light before first paint (evidence 115–118, `theme.spec.ts` driving the gear, `⌘,`, System-follows-OS, and persistence across a real relaunch) — and DESIGN.md's "provisional" wording retires. `.settings-row` reuses the setup row's classes inside the dialog. Future preferences land in this dialog, not in new corners.

**Revisit when.** Settings holds more than presentation preferences (it then earns sections and perhaps a window), or a second control tries to move into the identity row.

## D-103 — The theme control is a block at the rail's foot, and the glyph is the resolved theme

**Context.** D-102 put the theme choice in a Settings dialog. On the first rendered screen the owner reversed it: a centred modal for one row reads as a page, and they wanted the choice to appear *where the control is* — a small block at the rail's foot — with the trigger's icon telling the truth about the current mode instead of wearing a generic mark.

**Decision.** No dialog. The trigger in the rail's identity row **is the resolved theme**: a crescent while the product is dark, a sun while it is light — System wearing whichever it currently resolves to, kept live off the same event the terminal repaints on. Clicking it opens a **block anchored to the identity row** — the setup room's three-option segment spanning the row's width, one plane above the rail (surface-2, one edge, elev-2) — applied immediately, closed by a click outside or `Esc`, with no scrim and no focus ceremony. `⌘,` toggles the same block, revealing the rail first when it was hidden so the block has somewhere to open. D-102's placement survey, shared choice-set, terminal repaint, and screen-proof all stand; only the dialog is reversed.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the dialog and restyling it (rejected: the owner's objection was the page-ness, not the styling); a bare two-state sun/moon toggle (rejected: it cannot say "System", which is the default most people should keep); a menu-style vertical list (rejected: the segment is the anatomy setup already taught).

**Consequences.** `SettingsDialog` becomes `ThemeControl` (trigger + anchored block in one component); the settings-dialog evidence is re-shot as the popover (115–116); a future real Settings surface is a new decision, not a resurrection of this dialog.

**Revisit when.** Novus accrues genuine preferences beyond presentation — that surface will not be this popover.

## D-104 — The theme block is icons, not words

**Context.** D-103's block carried the setup room's worded segment — Light / Dark / System as text buttons. The owner, on sight, asked for the compact icon form instead: sun, crescent, display, the pattern the reference popover used.

**Decision.** The popover's three segments are glyphs alone — sun for light, crescent for dark, a display for system — each an icon button with its label on `aria-label` and `title`, the active one washed `--selected` exactly as active icon buttons are everywhere. The block hugs its content instead of spanning the row. The **setup room keeps its worded row**: a first-run surface explains itself; the popover is for a person who already chose once.

**Alternatives.** Icons with visible labels beneath (rejected: the block outgrows the corner it lives in); replacing setup's words with icons too (rejected: setup is where the choice is taught).

**Consequences.** The popover evidence is re-shot (115–116); the choice-set stays one list, the glyphs living beside the control that draws them.

**Revisit when.** A fourth appearance choice ever exists (it will not fit a glyph row quietly).

## D-105 — A person wears their GitHub picture, and leaving is a glyph

**Context.** The rail's account corner read `(KJ) KJ14G… ☾ Sign out`: an initials disc for a person who has a real profile picture, a login truncated beside it, and the only worded control in a row that had otherwise become icons (D-104). The owner named both on sight — the mark is a GitHub account's face, so show the face; and the word beside two glyphs should be the third glyph.

**Decision.** Every human identity mark wears **the person's own GitHub avatar**, initials remaining the fallback whenever there is no picture. The address is the login the person already signed in with: GitHub's own `https://github.com/{login}.png` redirect, so **nothing is added to the user row, the contract, or any API response** — no `avatar_url` column, no new field to keep fresh, no picture stored by Novus. The fetch happens **in the main process**, which answers the renderer with a `data:` URI, so the window's content policy stays `default-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:` — no page in this product reaches the network on its own and one picture is not the reason to start. The result is cached per login for the life of the process, on both sides of the bridge, and a miss is remembered as a miss. **Sign out becomes an icon button** — a door with an arrow leaving it — with `Sign out` on `aria-label` and `title`, beside the theme glyph in the same corner.

**Alternatives.** Reading `avatar_url` from GitHub's `/user` at sign-in and storing it (rejected: a column, a migration, a contract field and a staleness problem, for a URL derivable from the login — and it would still have to reach the renderer as a fetched image); widening `img-src` to `avatars.githubusercontent.com` and letting the renderer load the picture directly (rejected: it makes the renderer a network client for the first time, which is a security posture change, not a portrait); keeping initials (rejected on the owner's direct call); a worded Sign out (rejected: it is the last word in a row of glyphs).

**Amended the same day, on the owner's sight of the built result: the face is never late.** The first build fetched on every launch, so the account corner showed initials for the first beat of every session and then turned into a photograph — a flicker in the corner of the window, every time. Two changes, no new decision: the picture is **kept on this machine** under `userData` and answered from there without awaiting the network, refreshed behind the caller so a changed picture lands on the next launch; and the signed-in person's face is **resolved before the main process says `signed_in`**, riding on the auth status itself and seeded into the renderer's identity cache before anything renders it. Their mark is a face on the first frame the shell paints — at first sign-in and at every relaunch. A miss never discards a picture already held, so being offline is not "no face". Everyone *else* still resolves asynchronously on first sight in a session: it is one bridge call against a local file, and their mark is not the one a person watches at launch.

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's "no photos in V0" is reversed for identity marks only — the mark's size, shape, and 1px edge are untouched, and a photo is cropped by the circle it fills. Every mark in the product — rail, participants, trace, handoffs — takes the picture from the same component, so a participant is recognisable wherever they acted. Offline, the product looks exactly as it did before. `people.avatar` is the one bridge verb added; it grants nothing and reads nothing local.

**Revisit when.** Novus ever hosts an identity that is not a GitHub account, or a person asks to show no picture at all — the second is a preference this decision does not invent.

## D-106 — Focus handed back by a closing dialog wears no ring

**Context.** Pressing `+` on a project row opens the new-mission dialog; `Esc` closes it and the Dialog primitive hands focus back to the button it came from (D-076). The owner saw the result: a white box around the `+`, clipped by the rail's edge into an L. `Esc` is a key press, so the browser treats the restored focus as keyboard navigation and draws the `:focus-visible` ring — on a control that only exists on hover, at the rail's edge, aimed at by nobody.

**Decision.** The restored element keeps the focus and **does not wear the mark**: the Dialog stamps `data-focus-quiet` on the opener as it hands focus back, one rule suppresses the outline while that attribute is present, and the **next key press removes it**, restoring the ring in place. Blur removes it too. Nothing is blurred, no `tabindex` is moved, and focus order is untouched — a person who Escapes a dialog and then presses Tab or Enter sees exactly what they saw before.

**Alternatives.** Dropping the ring on `.side-new-mission` (rejected: it removes the mark for the person who *did* Tab there — DESIGN.md's ring is never removed); not restoring focus at all (rejected: it dumps the keyboard at the top of the document, which is the failure D-076's primitive exists to prevent); `focus({ focusVisible: false })` alone (rejected: not dependable across the engines this product will meet, and it says nothing about what happens next — the attribute is also what makes the next key press restore the ring); making the ring inset so it stops being clipped (rejected: it fixes the shape of a mark that should not be there, and would change focus everywhere).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's Focus section gains the one sanctioned suppression, named and bounded. Every dialog in the product inherits it, because they all come through the one primitive.

**Revisit when.** A popover or drawer that is not a Dialog starts restoring focus of its own — it should be handed the same behaviour rather than a second copy of it.

## D-107 — The harness's workers become legible: the join recorded, the end recorded, and a Workers view inside the turn

**Context.** D-071 made subagent activity visible but deliberately flat: text and tool events carry `parent_tool_use_id`, the trace indents them inside the turn's technical disclosure, and the renderer reads the parent id as a boolean. PROGRESS.md's known-gaps entry recorded exactly what that left unknowable — the spawning Task call's own id was discarded at the parser, so a worker's tag pointed at an id appearing nowhere else in the log; a Task's tool result emitted no event, so a worker's end, success, failure, and final report were parsed and thrown away; and two concurrent workers' steps interleaved in one indistinguishable list. The owner asked for first-class worker visibility without turning workers into Sessions, Approaches, or worktrees.

**Decision.** Three moves, all bounded by what the stream actually states.

*Protocol.* `harness.tool` gains the tool call's **own** `toolUseId` — the missing half of the join — and a Task's tool result becomes `harness.worker.ended` `{ toolUseId, failed, report }`: the result's own error flag and the worker's handed-back text, bounded like all harness text. Both are recorded-and-nothing-else at the control plane; no worker table, no worker id prefix, no side effects. A result whose Task call was never seen emits nothing — an end without a recorded start is not a worker.

*Presentation.* The trace groups a turn's tagged activity by the id it already carries. Inside the technical disclosure, a **Workers** rollup states the count and the states in words — `working` while the parent execution is live and no end has arrived, `done`, `failed`, and nothing at all when an execution ended without the result arriving, because an unstated end is not a state. Each worker row is its purpose (the Task call's own description), its state word, and its facts; choosing one opens a **worker inspector on the canvas** — purpose, parent chat, status, the timeline of its own tool activity, its report, its failure text — with *Back to chat* returning to the conversation at the scroll position it left. The inspector is a zoomed rendering of the same disclosure, updating as the room's ordinary poll delivers new events.

*Boundaries, unchanged.* A worker appears in no rail, no tab strip, no baton, no state line; it has no branch, worktree, checkpoint, controller, capability, or cost (the CLI reports usage per turn only, and a computed per-worker figure would be a number the vendor did not state — D-071's own rejection). Activity whose parent id joins to no recorded spawn stays what it was before this decision: indented grouped activity with no identity claimed. PRODUCT.md is untouched: a worker is not a domain object — it is the harness's business rendered legibly, under the existing Run/turn bullet, and it stays unaddressable outside the turn that spawned it.

**Alternatives.** Inferring worker completion from silence or from the next untagged text (rejected: invented lifecycle — the room warns from evidence, never prediction, D-094); a per-worker breathing indicator (rejected: the working indicator loops on the active execution row only, DESIGN.md#status-semantics); computing per-worker cost by summing tagged events' tokens (rejected: the CLI does not attribute usage below the turn, and Novus does not bill what the vendor did not state); worker tabs in the working row (rejected: the row holds what the person opened — approaches, conversations, files — and a worker is none of those, D-089); a `wkr_` identifier and worker rows in the database (rejected: ARCHITECTURE's event log already carries every fact, and a receipt that needed more would mean the *event* schema was incomplete, not the table set).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's "The harness's own workers" paragraph is rewritten: grouped-by-worker inside the disclosure with the rollup and inspector, still never the speech position, still no lane or mark of its own — the *identity* a worker now wears is the purpose text and state the harness itself stated, nothing more. The runner-event union grows one member and one field; older runners' reports still parse (both default). PROGRESS.md's known-gaps entry shrinks to what remains genuinely unknowable: per-worker cost, per-worker usage, a worker's own checks (a worker's shell command still reads as the turn's evidence), approval attribution below the turn, and nesting deeper than one level.

**Revisit when.** The CLI states per-worker usage or per-worker results on the stream — record them the day they exist; or a person asks to *direct* a worker, which is a product question about the harness boundary, not a presentation one.

## D-108 — Workers are the turn's own rows, entered with Enter, left with Esc

**Context.** D-107's first build put the Workers rollup *inside* the technical disclosure, where it sat among catch-all protocol rows ("requested {login}", "reached"), and its inspector carried a sentence of decision citations. On sight the owner called it cluttered and named the reference: terminal harnesses show subagents as clean lines you press Enter to step into — they wanted the best graphical form of that.

**Decision.** Workers leave the disclosure and become **the turn's own rows**, on the milestone anatomy CHECKPOINT already wears: a `workers` label, then one line per worker — purpose, the last thing it did in mono, the state word at the row's end. **Enter steps in, Esc steps out**; the click does the same. The disclosure returns to being the harness's own steps alone, and two catch-all rows that carried nothing the direction row and approval card don't already say — `execution.requested`, `boundary.reached` — are absorbed. The inspector sheds its lecture: title, `done · started · ended` facts line, timeline, files, report or failure — and **no decision identifiers in user-facing copy anywhere**, a rule this entry makes explicit.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the rollup in the disclosure and cleaning around it (rejected: a worker is something the turn *produced*, like a checkpoint — the disclosure is for how the harness worked, not what it made); a Workers section in the evidence panel (rejected: workers are not evidence, and the panel is not a menu, D-066); arrow-key roving selection across worker rows (deferred: buttons in tab order with Enter already give the keyboard path; a roving listbox earns its complexity only when turns hold many workers).

**Consequences.** D-107's protocol layer, boundaries, and honesty rules are untouched — this reverses only the placement and the copy. The e2e drives Enter and Esc by name; screenshots 120–121 re-shot.

**Revisit when.** A turn holds enough workers that a flat row list stops scanning — that is the day the rows earn arrow-key selection, not another surface.

## D-109 — A turn ends the way the harness said it ended

**Context.** The CLI's final `result` line carries a `subtype` saying how the turn ended, and the runner parsed it and read only the error flag. Three lies followed, found by this phase's audit: a turn that stopped at its **turn budget** (`error_max_turns`) exited cleanly and was presented as *Work finished* — the worst sentence a long-horizon product can say about unfinished work; a stream that **died before its result line** fell through every guard to *completed*, fabricating a success the harness never reported; and a **billing refusal** ("credit balance is too low") matched the authentication pattern and told a paid-out machine to *sign in again*. Separately, `archive.ts` kept a hand-maintained copy of the active-execution states that had drifted from the contract in both directions.

**Decision.** Classification reads what was stated. `error_max_turns` becomes `execution.interrupted` with the reason in the product's own words — *ran out of its turn budget before finishing; direct it again to continue* — because interruption is PRODUCT.md's word for "ended mid-work, resume is a human choice", and the session id survives so directing again genuinely continues. A clean exit with **no result line** is `execution.interrupted` — *ended without reporting an outcome* — never completed: a missing outcome is not a success. Billing gets its own classification, checked **before** authentication so a message containing both words still names money, with the vendor's own first line quoted and no sign-in advice. And archive derives its active-state list from `ExecutionStateSchema` exactly as the dispatcher does, ending the drift (it listed `paused`, which nothing can enter, and omitted `needs_direction`).

**Alternatives.** Mapping `error_max_turns` to a failure (rejected: nothing failed — work happened and stopped at a budget; *failed* teaches people to distrust the word); to `needs_direction` (rejected for now: that state is defined as a harness waiting at a boundary, and a process that exited is not waiting — entering a never-entered state deserves its own decision when boundaries are real); retrying transient endings automatically (rejected: an unasked retry is an unauthorized turn — D-062's whole posture).

**Consequences.** The contract's failure classification gains `billing`. Three ending shapes are pinned through the real spawn path (`execution-endings.test.ts`, a stub CLI on PATH per the D-056 pattern). "No false completed" becomes a tested property of the classifier rather than a hope.

**Revisit when.** The CLI grows result subtypes these guards do not name — each lands as the honest state it describes, never as completed by fallthrough.

## D-110 — The outbox never goes silent

**Context.** The runner's event outbox buffered honestly and delivered dishonestly, three ways found by this phase's audit. After ~32 seconds of control-plane unavailability a workstream's outbox **parked** — and the only call that ever un-parked one was the app quitting, so a turn could finish on disk while the room said *Running* until the next relaunch. A buffer restored after a relaunch was **never pumped**: delivery only ran when something new appended, so a lane with no further activity kept its terminal event on disk forever. And a batch the server refused with a permanent 4xx was **dropped with a console warning** — no gap marker, no record, a `execution.completed` that simply ceased to exist.

**Decision.** Three verbs that already existed get callers, and one contract gets a word. Every discovery pass (15 s) re-offers delivery to any outbox still holding events, so parking costs seconds, not the session. Startup pumps every persisted buffer whose workstream this machine is still enrolled for — constructing restores, flushing delivers. And `deliver` may now answer `"refused"`: the outbox replaces the refused batch with a **gap marker in its place**, exactly as overflow always has, so the record states the loss instead of hiding it — with one bounded exception, a refused *marker* is given up out loud rather than replaced with itself forever. A corrupt buffer file, already survivable, now also says what it lost.

**Alternatives.** Un-parking from the 2 s poll (rejected: when the plane is down, each attempt cycle is ~32 s of backoff — a 15 s cadence keeps exactly one polite attempt in flight); retrying permanent 4xxs forever (rejected: it wedges every event behind a batch the server will never take); pumping buffers for un-enrolled workstreams (rejected: no credential to deliver with — the file waits for re-enrolment).

**Consequences.** "Resume after restart" now covers the *report*, not just the work: the worst case for a room reading a stale state is one discovery interval, not an app lifetime. `outbox.test.ts` grows the refused-batch marker and the refused-marker bound.

**Revisit when.** The control plane can push (the poll disappears) — delivery cadence then belongs to the transport, not a timer.

## D-111 — Force interrupt: the escalation of an unanswered stop

**Context.** D-073 refused `force_interrupt` as "what Stop does" — and for a wedged harness under a live runner it was right. But the audit behind this phase found the case it is not: an execution in `stopping` whose stop is never answered. The runner found no process and said nothing; the sweep keys on runner silence, and a healthy desktop polls every two seconds, so the wedged `stopping` lived forever; the state line offered no action; and a second Stop was a transport no-op because the idempotent command had already settled. A dead end with no exit, on a screen that says *Stopping* indefinitely. PRODUCT.md, meanwhile, had promised force-interrupt in prose twice since the first draft.

**Decision.** `force_interrupt` becomes a real capability — the controller's by lease, Mission Admin's by role, exactly the two PRODUCT.md#control names — and a real verb with the narrowest honest gate: the execution must already be `stopping`, and either the stop has gone unanswered past a one-minute grace or the machine has gone quiet. Refused in words otherwise — *Stop it first*; *give it a minute*. What it does then is what the runner-gone stop path has always done: an attributed, event-recorded `interrupted` outcome that frees the lane, settling any straggler approvals in the same transaction. The state line's *Stopping* offers the action only when the server would accept it, with *the stop has gone unanswered* appended. Two runner-side holes close with it: a stop that finds no live process now reports — `stopped (never_started)` when the execution was merely open between turns, `interrupted` in words when nothing was here at all — after checking that no queued start could still legitimately run; so most wedged stops never need the human verb at all.

**Alternatives.** Force interrupt from *Running* (rejected: that is what Stop is for — D-073's argument stands where it was made); a server sweep that auto-interrupts a stale `stopping` (rejected: the runner may be mid-kill on a slow machine, and a person on the scene beats a timer that cannot see it — the sweep still catches the dead-runner case); making the second Stop re-enqueue (rejected: two idempotency keys for one stop is how a stop gets delivered twice).

**Consequences.** D-073 is narrowed, not reversed: the stall's recovery is still Stop; force interrupt exists one step later, where Stop has demonstrably failed. PRODUCT.md's capability table gains the row its prose promised; its stalled-overlay sentence now describes the real path. If the machine was partitioned rather than dead, its process may finish on that laptop — every later report is recorded but changes nothing, the same honesty the offline-stop path already accepted.

**Revisit when.** Runners report liveness within a turn (D-073's own revisit clause) — a stop that is *being worked on* could then hold the grace open honestly.

## D-112 — A queue neither strands nor hides

**Context.** Two queue truths from this phase's audit. First: the completed-turn dispatch — the one place a queued direction is automatically carried forward (D-083) — fires after its ingest transaction commits, and its errors are swallowed; a control-plane restart or crash in that window left a direction queued forever over an idle lane, recoverable only by a human submitting something else. No sweep re-drove it. Second: the state line stated a queue only in the idle, waiting-on-the-holder case; a controller's own backlog behind a running turn appeared nowhere at line level — invisible exactly while it was longest.

**Decision.** The reliability sweep gains a fourth pass: a lane holding a never-carried queued direction, with nothing live and whose **last word was `completed`**, is re-offered to `dispatchQueuedForController` — the same dispatcher, re-checking the baton, capability, and never-carried guard itself. D-083's rule is preserved to the letter: a stop, failure, or interruption still never auto-runs what waited behind it; the sweep re-drives only the dispatch that *should* have happened and was lost. And the working state line gains a quiet suffix — "· {n} directions queued" — whenever the queue overlay stands behind a running, starting, stopping, or approval-blocked turn; nothing renders at zero, and the idle case keeps its full "Waiting for {holder}" line.

**Alternatives.** Re-driving after every terminal outcome (rejected: D-083 decided that deliberately — work queued behind a failure should not start itself); retrying the inline dispatch with backoff instead of sweeping (rejected: the crash window is precisely the case where there is no process left to retry in); a queue list surface (deferred: the trace already numbers positions and the chat rows already say `queued · {n}` — a dedicated surface earns itself when queues get long).

**Consequences.** `SweepResult` gains `queuesDriven`. Head-of-line blocking on an undispatchable direction remains (the dispatcher takes the oldest and stops), recorded in PROGRESS as a known gap rather than silently reordered.

**Revisit when.** Queues grow long enough that the oldest-first, one-at-a-time dispatch visibly head-of-line-blocks — reordering is a product decision about fairness, not a sweep detail.

## D-113 — What a lane has spent so far is stated where its chats are listed

**Context.** D-071 carried what a *turn* cost onto the trace's machinery line, and the Compare surface summed a lane's usage for the decision moment — but no surface answered "what has this lane spent so far?" while the work was still going. For long-horizon work that figure is the difference between letting a run continue and stopping it, and the only way to get it was adding up machinery lines by eye.

**Decision.** The approach overview's facts line — already the lane's one `·`-joined row of counts — gains the lane's own totals: `{n} turns`, `{time} of harness time`, and the harness's cost figure, summed in the renderer over the executions the room already holds. Every rule that governed the per-turn figure governs the sum: claims carried as claims, a figure no turn reported absent rather than zero, text in a row and never a tile, and nothing that reads as a budget or a ceiling (PRODUCT.md forbids Novus imposing one, D-034).

**Alternatives.** The mission header (rejected: no machinery in the header, prohibited pattern 20's neighbor at DESIGN.md's own line); the state line (rejected: state + next action only); a server-computed field (rejected: the executions are already on the wire and the arithmetic is three additions — the D-094 pattern, no new fetch); mission-wide totals across lanes (deferred: Compare already sets lanes side by side, and a cross-lane total is a comparison surface's fact, not a lane page's).

**Consequences.** `usageSoFar` joins the derive module with the null-stays-null tests. The overview line lengthens by up to three facts; each disappears entirely when there is nothing true to say.

**Revisit when.** An organization budget becomes real — D-071's own revisit clause — at which point "spent so far" stops being only a claim and needs a decision about what it may stop.

## D-114 — The turn has a pulse, and a pulse is not progress

**Context.** D-073's revisit clause named this exactly: "Runners can report liveness *within* a turn — a heartbeat while a tool call runs — at which point a stall can be told from a long tool call." Until now the room could not tell them apart: a twenty-minute test suite produces no transcript events, and its legitimate silence looked identical to a dead machine — the stalled overlay said *no progress reported since {time}* about both, and the honest instinct (wait for the suite; stop the corpse) pointed opposite ways with nothing to choose by.

**Decision.** The runner states the turn's pulse: `execution.heartbeat`, every three minutes while the harness process is alive, empty payload, recorded-and-nothing-else at the control plane. **A pulse is liveness, never progress**: the stall watch excludes heartbeats from its clock on both sides of the wire — a turn that reports nothing but its own breathing still stalls at ten minutes, because a wedged process breathes too, and a pulse that reset the clock would let it dodge the watch forever. What the pulse buys is one clause: the stalled suffix reads *no progress reported since {time} — the process is alive* while a beat is fresh (within two beats), and stays the plain sentence when none is — which is when Stop is the right instinct. The trace absorbs the events; nothing new renders in the thread.

**Alternatives.** Dropping the stall threshold now that the signal exists (deferred: the threshold is D-073's, and moving it deserves observation of real pulses first); heartbeating only during long tool calls (rejected: deciding "long" needs the very signal being built); a liveness field on the runner's poll instead of an event (rejected: the poll proves the *runner* lives, not the turn — and the event log is the mission's memory, ARCHITECTURE.md#event-model).

**Consequences.** The runner-event union gains its most boring member. Event volume: one row per three minutes per live turn, absorbed by the trace and excluded from progress. ARCHITECTURE's events list, which has said "heartbeats" since the first draft, is finally true.

**Revisit when.** Real pulses have been observed across long runs — then the stall threshold can drop for turns with no pulse, which is the sharper watch D-073 wanted all along.

## D-115 — Permission profiles: the harness always asks; a profile changes who answers

**Context.** D-062 pinned the permission policy away from the machine — `--setting-sources ""`, `--permission-mode manual`, the stdio control channel — and deliberately issued one approval for one act: no allowlist, no "always allow", the CLI's own `permission_suggestions` discarded. That refusal was of *inherited* and *harness-suggested* standing grants, and it was right; but it left the product with exactly one temperature. Since then two policies have already answered questions at the runner on a person's standing instruction: a read turn's deny-all (D-095) and a scope's allow-inside/refuse-outside (D-097). What was missing was the person-chosen tier in between — the thing Claude Code's own permission modes (`plan`, `manual`, `acceptEdits`, `auto`, `dontAsk`, `bypassPermissions`; all six listed by `claude 2.1.228 --help`, probed 2026-08-11) give a solo operator, and Novus's supervision model had no honest place for.

**Decision — the shape.** A workstream carries a **permission profile**: `plan`, `manual` (the default, exactly the pre-profile behaviour), `accept_edits`, `auto`, or `dont_ask`. It is Novus's own standing **answer policy**, applied at the runner's control-channel router where D-095 and D-097 already decide; it is never the CLI's own mode. The wire does not change with it: every profile runs under the pinned flags with the stdio channel open, so the harness always asks and every act is still routed, recordable, and refusable. What a profile changes is who answers: `manual` routes every question to the room; `accept_edits` answers the path-naming edit tools itself; `auto` answers everything except a shell command — D-097's own line, a shell command declares its targets nowhere; `dont_ask` answers everything; `plan` answers **no** to every privileged act with the reason carrying the instruction to propose. The one flag that moves is plan's: `--permission-mode plan` is passed so the CLI's own plan mode tells the model to propose rather than thrash — the value is in the installed CLI's `--help`, its live semantics are deliberately not load-bearing, and the router denies whatever a plan turn asks regardless: the flag is behaviour, the router is the guarantee.

**The ladder's order is the order of the claims.** Containment first: a read turn is denied whatever the lane trusts (D-095, unchanged, still silently). Then plan, which refuses even an in-scope write, because the person asked for a proposal. Then ownership: a scope's verdict on the edit tools stands whatever the profile says — a sibling's territory is not a trust dial (D-097, unchanged, still its recorded silence). Then trust: the profile's allows. Then the room.

**Every profile-decided answer is recorded.** A new runner event, `approval.policy` — request id, tool, decision, deciding profile, and the same bounded, path-masked, value-redacted summary a card would have carried (D-052) — recorded-and-nothing-else at the control plane: no approvals row, no `needs_approval`, no boundary, because nothing waited. The trace renders it as apparatus, never as a card; the turn's machinery line names the profile it ran under; and the execution row pins `permission_profile` at dispatch, so a receipt can say what supervision every turn had. D-095's and D-097's own decisions keep their recorded silence — this event belongs to the profile alone.

**Setting it is a policy act, not an operating one.** `policy.set`, held by Mission Admin and Operator and never granted by the baton (the `approach.create` reasoning: it decides what gets asked, where the baton decides who answers). One tier further, judged in the route against the one list the contracts package exports: `dont_ask` is **Mission Admin's alone**, refused for an Operator in words, and refused for everyone until the exact warning sentence the person confirmed travels with the request — recorded verbatim on the `policy.changed` event, the D-100 accepted-blockers pattern. The change applies from the next dispatched turn; a running turn keeps the profile pinned at its own dispatch (D-043's rule, as with scope). A fork starts at `manual` whatever its sibling ran: trust is granted to a lane by a person, never inherited by a fork.

**There is no bypass, structurally.** `bypassPermissions` turns the asking off at the harness — no routing, no record, no scope enforcement, no read-turn containment, since all four ride the control channel — so it is not a value `PermissionProfileSchema` can parse, not a word the route or the database CHECK accepts, and not a flag any profile passes; the picker explains the absence in place and names `dont_ask` as the ceiling that keeps the record. The CLI's `acceptEdits`, `auto`, and `dontAsk` modes are never passed either, for the same reason: each makes the CLI act without asking, and Novus's version of those postures is the router answering instantly **on the record**. No profile touches server authorization: who may direct, stop, decide, answer, or change the profile itself is the capability model's, enforced server-side, whatever the lane's profile says.

**Alternatives.** Passing the CLI's own mode flags per profile (rejected outright: measured in D-056, `acceptEdits` performs the act silently — no routing means no record and no enforcement, which is the whole gap); per-session profiles (rejected for now: the profile governs the lane's one workspace, exactly as the baton does; a chat-level trust dial beside chat-level scopes would be two overlapping grants with no story for their disagreement); per-direction profiles in the composer (rejected: a profile is a standing policy with a name on it, not a per-message mood); requiring the baton as well as the role (rejected: the merge of D-062's revoke-then-answer rule with role-held policy acts is that an Admin acts visibly under their own name — and an Operator who holds no baton still holds their role); a warning banner while `dont_ask` stands (deferred: the chip wears the word in the warn tone, the trace sentence carries its meaning, and a persistent banner is the notice-bar pattern DESIGN.md reserves for connection truth).

**Consequences.** `needs_approval` now means: the lane's profile left this act to a person. The approval card's "This one act only — nothing is remembered" stays true — a card's answer never becomes policy, and `permission_suggestions` stay discarded. `workstreams.permission_profile` and `executions.permission_profile` join the schema with the CHECK widened in exactly one place each; `policy.set` joins the capability model; `policy.changed` and `approval.policy` join the event vocabulary. The composer's foot wears the profile beside model and effort, with the picker explaining every mode in a sentence. And the `btn-danger` variant DESIGN.md's primitives have named since D-076 is finally defined in the stylesheet — the D-100 confirm dialogs referenced it and silently fell back to the plain text style until this slice's warning dialog needed it.

**Revisit when.** Org-definable autonomy ceilings arrive (PRODUCT.md's extension point — an org saying `dont_ask` is never available is the natural next tier, and it belongs to `org.policy.configure`); or a second harness needs profiles, at which point the answer ladder becomes an adapter contract rather than Claude Code's router; or live plan-mode behaviour is observed through the opt-in live suite and the flag's usefulness can be judged against what it actually does.

## D-116 — The profile picker is a slider, and evidence is captured over a working room

**Context.** D-115 shipped the permission profiles behind a chip whose menu was five stacked rows, each a name over a sentence of meaning. The owner read the evidence screenshots the same day and redirected on sight, twice over: the menu was a wall of text — "a slider which goes all the way, and Don't ask a different color, with the popup to show" — and the captures themselves looked bad, because both were taken over an empty room that made the product look emptier than it is.

**Decision — the control.** The chip stays; its popover becomes one slider: a single track with five stops, autonomy growing rightward from Plan to Don't ask, the fill run to the stop that is set, the active stop enlarged and filled, the far stop rimmed in the warn tone before it is ever chosen and the fill turning warn when it is. One meta line beneath the track explains the stop under the pointer, falling back to the one that is set, so the five paragraphs collapse into the one sentence a person is actually deciding about. The no-bypass foot sentence stays; the Don't ask warning dialog stays exactly as D-115 cut it, opened from the stop. Semantics are untouched: same testids, same capability gating, same tier, same acknowledgement — this is presentation, redirected on sight, the D-084/D-089 pattern. Composed from the Popover and existing tokens; not a new primitive, the same standing the merge confirm's segmented picker has.

**Decision — the evidence.** The slice's screenshots are captured after a real turn, so the control is photographed in a working room — transcript, machinery line, checkpoint — rather than over a void. Doing that surfaced a real capture hazard worth recording: with two Electron clients, the window most recently opened is frontmost, and a backgrounded renderer's polling is throttled by Chromium — Kartik's room sat frozen at pre-turn state while his runner (main process, unthrottled) had long finished the work. The spec now brings the window forward and waits for the room to catch up before photographing it. This is a fact about capture and background tabs, not a product defect: the durable state was always right, and a refocused room catches up within its poll.

**Alternatives.** The slider always visible on the composer foot (rejected: permanent chrome for an occasional act; the chip's word is the standing fact); labels under every stop (rejected: five labels under a 320px track is the wall of text lying down); a native `input type="range"` (rejected: five discrete stops with per-stop gating and testids are buttons in a radiogroup, which is also what the merge method picker already is).

**Consequences.** `122-`/`123-` regenerated and reviewed: the menu shot now shows the fill at Accept edits with the turn's transcript and its `· Accept edits` machinery line behind it. The row-menu CSS is deleted with the row menu. On the owner's second look the same day — "bigger, neater, too much text everywhere" — the control was scaled up (400px popover, 3px track, 10/16px stops) and the words cut: the stop's name reads out above the track like a value, the meanings became one-line fragments, the foot became one line, and the warning dialog's fact groups one line each; nothing semantic moved.

**Revisit when.** A sixth profile ever exists — a slider that scrolls has stopped being a slider — or the owner's eye finds the stops too quiet at 760 wide.

## D-117 — The profile picker takes Codex's shape: the question, then glyph rows, the dangerous one warn whole

**Context.** D-115 shipped the profiles behind a row menu; D-116 re-cut it as a slider on the owner's first sight, then scaled it up and stripped its words on their second. On the third look the owner named a reference outright — Codex's approval picker — and asked for that: a question as the head, icon-led rows with a name over one line of meaning, the dangerous option coloured whole, the current one checked.

**Decision.** The picker is that shape, in Novus's own materials. The head asks the question in the meta tone — "How should Claude Code's actions be approved?". One row per profile in autonomy order, each a 16px stroke glyph (lines of a plan, the question mark, the pencil, the bolt, the warning triangle — currentColor, from the inline-stroke style the send and sign-out glyphs already use), the name at row weight, one fragment of meaning beneath. The current row ends in a check. The **Don't ask row wears the warn tone whole** — glyph, name, meaning, check — Codex's one genuinely right idea here: the danger is read before the name is. The no-bypass foot line, the Mission Admin gating, the acknowledged warning dialog, every testid, and all of D-115's semantics are untouched. The slider is set aside, not half-kept: its CSS and its hover-preview state are deleted, per the D-084 rule that dead presentation is removed with the presentation it lost to.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the slider with row labels (rejected: the owner chose against it on sight of a working alternative, which is the review this repository's UI answers to); Codex's "Learn more" link (rejected: the foot sentence already carries the one fact a link would, and the room has no docs site to send anyone to); reproducing Codex's shield/hand iconography (rejected: Novus draws its own quiet strokes, and a hand glyph at 16px is a smudge).
