**Consequences.** Third cut in one day of D-115's presentation, each on the owner's sight of the rendered result — the working pattern D-084 through D-089 recorded, operating as designed. `122-`/`123-` regenerated over the working room and reviewed once more.

**Revisit when.** The owner's eye moves again, or a sixth profile makes the row list long enough to deserve grouping.

## D-118 — Project skills reach the agent: published for review, enabled by digest, composed alone

**Context.** D-072 refused to load a project's `.claude/skills` and measured why: `--setting-sources project` re-admits hooks — which run before the permission check — and MCP servers, and `--plugin-dir` pointed at repository content re-opens the same door with better manners, because a plugin can carry both. Its revisit clause named the buildable version: Novus **composing** a directory containing only skill files it copied out of the worktree, under the containment `CLAUDE.md` already has (D-064). Since then the repository has grown every mechanism that version needs: the publish-then-pin rule for project-declared things (D-043), a person-set standing lane policy with a role-held capability, event-recorded and pinned at dispatch (D-115), and the recorded-and-nothing-else audit event. The owner asked for the skills-and-MCP slice; this entry is the half the contracts support.

**Probed, against installed `claude 2.1.229` (2026-08-12).** A hand-composed plugin directory — `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` plus one `skills/zephyr-codes/SKILL.md` — passed with `--plugin-dir` under the full pinned argv (`--setting-sources ""`, `--permission-mode manual`, `--permission-prompt-tool stdio`): the session's own `system/init` line lists `novus-project-skills:zephyr-codes`, the model itself named the skill in its reply, and **`mcp_servers` is empty** — the composed directory brought its skill and nothing else. ($0.017 of quota, one haiku turn.)

**Decision.** A project's skills reach the agent through four rules, each an existing pattern applied to a file the agent itself can write:

1. **Published for review** (D-043's rule). The runner reads the worktree's `.claude/skills/*/SKILL.md` and publishes a bounded manifest beside the declared commands — per skill its directory name (the identity; one path segment, never a traversal, by schema), its frontmatter description, its byte count, and the SHA-256 of its bytes. The control plane stores it verbatim and never reads a body. Bounded at 40 skills and 100 KB per skill, the instructions file's own bound.
2. **Enabled by a person, at a digest** (D-115's shape). `skills.set` — Mission Admin and Operator, never the baton, because it decides what the harness is handed where the baton decides who answers — sets the lane's whole enabled set, and the route refuses any entry the published manifest does not carry at exactly that digest: what is approved is what was reviewed, and a skill the agent rewrote must be re-read by a person before it stands again. The change is one `skills.changed` event with both sets verbatim; a repeat records nothing. A fork starts with none, like the profile: trust is granted to a lane by a person, never inherited.
3. **Pinned at dispatch, composed alone** (D-043 again, then D-072's version). Both turn commands carry the enabled list; at spawn the runner composes a skills-only plugin directory in its own staging — plugin.json authored by Novus, one SKILL.md per skill, resolved through `realpath` into the worktree, regular, bounded, and required to hash to the approved digest, with the verified bytes being the written bytes so the loaded copy cannot drift from the check that admitted it — and passes `--plugin-dir`. A mismatch drops the skill with the reason in words; hooks, `.mcp.json`, commands, and agents are never written, so D-072's refusal becomes structure rather than abstinence. The staging is removed with the turn.
4. **On the record, both ends.** The turn's `execution.running` states what was carried and what was dropped with each reason; the trace renders both as apparatus — the drop in the warn tone, because a dead grant is news. The inspector's Overview lists the manifest with the lane's standing answer per row in words, the action gated on `skills.set` with the capability named.

A skill is instructions, never authority: every tool call a skill-bearing turn makes still reaches the permission router under the lane's profile, and PRODUCT.md says so as a deliberate choice.

**The other half of the ask is surfaced, not built.** An MCP server registry, per-server permissions, and network restrictions are defined nowhere — MCP is tool surface the harness owns (PRODUCT.md's boundary), it is exactly what D-062/D-072 pinned out, a registry sits adjacent to the "integration catalog" non-goal, and the only network-restriction contract is the cloud workspace egress allowlist (D-015) whose provider-IP implementation D-024 already proved unworkable. Building any of that is a product decision the owner has not made, not an implementation detail; nothing here opens a door to it.

**Alternatives.** Loading via `--setting-sources project` or repo-pointed `--plugin-dir` (rejected in D-072; unchanged). Approving by name without a digest (rejected: the agent can rewrite the file between review and turn, which is the exact hole D-043 closed for commands). Carrying a skill's whole directory (deferred: supporting files a SKILL.md references are not composed yet — a multi-file digest is more machinery than the wedge needs, the residue is visible rather than silent, and the worktree's own copies remain readable to the agent through ordinary, routed tool calls). Skills on the execution row as a column (rejected: the event log is the audit surface and the receipt's source; a second copy drifts). Carrying skills into read-alongside turns (deferred: containment governs those turns end to end, and widening what they are handed deserves its own look).

**Consequences.** `workstreams.enabled_skills` and `workspaces.declared_skills` join the schema; `skills.set` joins the capability model; `skills.changed` joins the event vocabulary; `workspace.declared` and `execution.running` gain defaulted fields so an older runner's report still validates. The operator's own user-level skills still load — the CLI reads them regardless of setting sources, a pre-existing fact of whose machine the runner is, unchanged by this slice and stated here so nobody reads "skills-only" as claiming otherwise.

**Revisit when.** A project's skills need their supporting files; or the owner decides MCP servers are product surface, at which point registry, per-server permission, and egress questions come back as one deliberate design; or the CLI gains a way to load skills alone from a named directory, which would retire the composed plugin shim.

## D-119 — Project MCP servers, governed: the registry is the manifest, and their tools always ask

**Context.** D-118 carried a project's skills and surfaced its other half — an MCP server registry, per-server permissions, and network restrictions — as an unmade product decision, because MCP is exactly what D-062/D-072 pinned out and a registry sits near the "integration catalog" non-goal. The owner then directed it built. This entry records that direction and the shape: project-declared harness tool servers under the room's governance — which is not an integration catalog, and the non-goal stands.

**Probed, against installed `claude 2.1.229` (2026-08-12).** A hand-written config passed with `--mcp-config <file>` and `--strict-mcp-config`, under the full pinned argv (`--setting-sources ""`, `--permission-mode manual`, `--permission-prompt-tool stdio`), loaded exactly the one named server — a 30-line stdio echo server — with `mcp_servers: [{name: docs, status: connected}]` in the session's own init line, its tool listed as `mcp__docs__echo`, and nothing else: no user-level servers, no worktree `.mcp.json`, no plugin servers. Strictness is real, and the tool-name prefix the router keys on is confirmed.

**Decision.** MCP servers ride D-118's four rules with two deliberate hardenings:

1. **The registry is the published manifest.** The runner reads the worktree's `.mcp.json` and publishes each reviewable server beside the commands and skills: name, transport, command and args and declared env for stdio, url for http/sse, and the SHA-256 of the canonical declaration — the whole observable behavior, field for field. What cannot be reviewed is not listed and can never be enabled: a path-shaped name, a plaintext remote, a credentialed url.
2. **Network restriction is admission.** A remote server must be https away from loopback, with no credentials in its authority and no control characters — the preview bridge's rules (D-045), enforced in the contracts schema so the wire itself refuses an unpublishable declaration. This is the one network restriction a local runner can honestly enforce; a stdio server is a program whose network Novus does not pretend to filter, and the review shows exactly what would run. Secret isolation is D-041's, stated rather than new: a server process lives in the harness environment, which holds no project secrets, no runner credential, no OAuth token.
3. **Activation is the top tier.** `mcp.set` is Mission Admin's alone — an Operator may enable skills and may not enable servers — because a server is new tool surface: the dont_ask tier's reasoning, applied to activation. Enabling pins the digest; the route refuses what the manifest does not carry at that digest; one `mcp.changed` event carries both sets verbatim; both turn commands carry the enabled list pinned at dispatch.
4. **Composed alone, and their tools always ask.** At spawn the runner re-derives each enabled server from the worktree, digest-checks it, and writes a config Novus authors, passed with `--mcp-config` and `--strict-mcp-config` — the composed file is the CLI's entire MCP world. A changed, vanished, or no-longer-reviewable declaration is dropped with the reason on the turn's record. At the router, `mcp__{server}__*` is treated as Bash is treated (D-097's reason, verbatim): the request declares no effects Novus can read, so `auto` never answers one and `accept_edits` never did — only `dont_ask` answers an MCP tool by policy, recorded. Per-server permission is therefore two-tier: which servers exist at all is the Admin's standing, reviewable, revocable grant; every act any of them takes is still a person's question.

**Alternatives.** Per-server allow/ask modes (rejected for now: a second trust dial beside the lane profile with no story for their disagreement — D-115's own reasoning against per-session profiles; the two-tier model covers the need without it). An acknowledged warning sentence per enablement, the dont_ask pattern (deferred: the Admin-only tier plus always-ask-at-router carries the weight, and a second confirm on every enable is ceremony; revisit if enables prove riskier than they read). Operator-tier activation like skills (rejected: a skill is instructions, a server is capability). Publishing the worktree file to the CLI directly (rejected outright: the agent writes that file mid-turn; the strict composed config is the entire point).

**Consequences.** `workstreams.enabled_mcp_servers` and `workspaces.declared_mcp` join the schema; `mcp.set` joins the capability model, Mission Admin only; `mcp.changed` joins the event vocabulary; `workspace.declared` and `execution.running` gain defaulted fields. The `${VAR}` expansions Claude Code performs inside MCP configs resolve against the harness environment (D-041), which is why declared env cannot smuggle a secret the review did not show. The fake harness's `[fake-ask:{tool}]` tag now admits underscores so a scripted turn can ask as an MCP tool through the production router.

**Revisit when.** A project needs a per-server standing allow (the deferred dial); or org policy wants to forbid MCP entirely (`org.policy.configure`, the D-115 extension point); or a harness beyond Claude Code needs the manifest, at which point transport and strictness become adapter contract.

## D-120 — Home is the board: missions grouped by what they need, opened at the thing that is asking

**Context.** The owner asked for Phase 8's mission inbox and showed the reference on sight — a Conductor-style board of computed columns with per-card diff arithmetic — with the standing caution "do not automatically launch unlimited work". The word in the brief was "Kanban", which PRODUCT.md's non-goals refuse; what the reference actually is, is a **status-grouped inbox wearing a board layout**: nobody drags a card, nobody assigns a status, the columns are computed from what the work is doing. That is buildable honestly, and this entry is the D-100 move — the reference named, the anatomy taken, the semantics ours.

**Decision — the shape.** Home renders every active mission in four computed columns, **Needs you | Running | Waiting | Decided** — attention first because it is the reason Home was opened, where the reference leads with its junk drawer. The class comes from the list projection's own precedence, which this slice completes: the list now reaches the decided states the room's projection always had (`pull_request_open` from an open request, else `decision_recorded` from a standing decision), outranked by attention and by running — a decided mission whose revision is underway is honestly Running. Cards carry the list's own facts, extended for the board (each a cheap indexed aggregate): `lastActivityAt` from the newest event, `working` — the running mirror of D-093's `attention`, naming the first running lane and the chat whose turn it is — `controllerLogin` only for a single-lane mission (several lanes are several batons; one name would lie), churn as distinct changed paths plus summed per-turn `+`/`−` (stated as churn, never a net diff), and a checks tally counting only the lanes' current head revisions (the staleness rule, reduced to what a list can afford). There is no drag and no rank; an empty column says so in one quiet line; the board renders only once a mission exists (prohibited pattern 11).

**Decision — the click.** A card's one action is opening the mission **at the thing that is asking**: the attention's lane and conversation, the running chat, or — for a decided mission with siblings — the Compare surface. This closes the gap D-093 recorded ("the working set takes no open-at instruction yet"): `openMissionAt` composes the working set's existing verbs, the rail's attention lens now uses it too, and a Decided card's Compare intent survives the tab change through a ref the canvas reset consumes exactly once. **Home is a glance, not a purge**: choosing Home deselects the active tab and keeps every open tab in the strip — before this, the Home control wiped the working set, which made "check what needs me" and "close everything" one button.

**From the owner's brief, deliberately not used** (the owner asked to be told): the **"Needs decision"** column — Novus cannot compute that a decision is *due* without manufacturing urgency (Principle 7; comparison is reached, never pushed — D-074/D-075) — its honest neighbour is the Decided column, which states what stands; the **"Complete"** column — no mission can be complete while the terminal lifecycle (`mission.close`, `Completed`) is unbuilt, and an empty fictional column is prohibited pattern 11 — the terminal lifecycle is the natural next slice and the board is the first surface that wants it; **Start Mission / Resume Mission / Request review as card buttons** — starting is words (D-077, the ask-dialog and composer already own it), resuming *is* the card's click, and the review surface does not exist; **Controller on multi-lane cards** — stated only where it is one fact; and **dragging** — a board you can drag is a board that lies. "Do not automatically launch unlimited work" was already structural — nothing on the board starts anything — and stays so.

**Found on the way, by the board's own first screenshot**: a created mission could card twice for up to five seconds — `handleCreated` appended the fresh mission to the shell's list while the five-second poll delivered the same mission, and the duplicate lived until the next wholesale replace. In the rail this was a flicker nobody caught; on the board it was two cards for one mission in two columns. Fixed at the source (the append dedupes by id) and pinned by the spec's uniqueness assertion.

**Alternatives.** Columns as drag targets (rejected outright: state is computed; a hand moving a card would either lie or need verbs the card deliberately does not have). Grouped rows instead of columns (my first proposal; set aside on the owner's sight of the reference — the column layout carries the glance, and the semantics stayed ours). Card-per-chat like the reference (rejected: the unit of attention is the mission — per-chat cards recreate the one-mission-reads-as-two problem D-086 reversed; the card names the chat in words instead). Computing churn as the net branch diff (rejected for now: honest at a price the 5-second poll should not pay; summed per-turn churn with distinct paths is cheap and stated as what it is).

**Consequences.** `MissionSchema` gains the five board fields, defaulted so the detail path is untouched; the list query grows six scalar aggregates and the lane summary three, with `checkpoints_by_mission`, `checkpoints_by_workstream`, and `file_changes_by_mission` indexes added for them; `openMissionAt` joins the working set's vocabulary; the Home control changes meaning (glance, not purge). PRODUCT.md's precedence sentence gains the decided class; DESIGN.md specifies the board as the second sanctioned card use.

**Revisit when.** The terminal mission lifecycle arrives (a real Completed column, and archival's relationship to it); or the board needs a project filter once one person's missions outgrow a glance; or per-column ordering wants something other than recency, at which point the answer must still never be a rank.

## D-121 — The terminal lifecycle: a mission ends by a person's own act, and the receipt is the room frozen

**Context.** Completed and Cancelled have been in PRODUCT.md's state model since the first draft, `mission.close` has sat in the capability table marked unimplemented, and the known-gaps list has said "no terminal mission lifecycle" for as long as it has existed. The Home board (D-120) finally made the absence visible as a column that could not exist, and the owner asked for it by name.

**Decision.** One verb, two endings, each meaning what it says. `mission.close` is Mission Admin's alone, never lease-granted — ending the mission is a decision about the mission, archival's own reasoning. **Completed** is gated on its meaning: a standing decision must exist (a result nobody chose is not accepted) and no pull request may be open (resolved means resolved) — each refusal the useful instruction, "Record a decision first, or cancel instead". **Cancelled** carries the person's own words about what was abandoned, on the event and in the receipt. Both borrow archival's refusals verbatim (D-063): the waiting approval first, live work in words, a stillborn `requested` attempt ended on the way out. The ending is the one stored word the state projection reads — a terminal state is a fact a person made, not a derivation — and it never resumes: direction and forking refuse in words, the composer is hidden rather than disabled, the fork control disappears, and an invitation redeemed after the end joins as a Viewer whatever role it carried (ARCHITECTURE.md failure mode 13, enforced at redemption).

**The receipt is snapshotted at close**, realizing the Receipt row ARCHITECTURE.md has carried since the start: a deterministic projection of durable rows — participants, applied directions, every decision verbatim (superseded ones kept), churn, the ledger's final rows each stating whether it still proved a lane's head at close, a mechanical "remains uncertain" list, the pull request's last word — validated through the contract schema before storage, with the event range it covers. The `mission.closed` event is recorded **before** the projection, so the receipt's range includes the record of its own ending and re-projecting over the unchanged log reproduces it exactly (the determinism test compares deep equality, because jsonb normalizes key order). The terminal room renders the snapshot, never a recomputation; the board files the mission under **Complete**, the fifth column the lifecycle finally earns.

**Also in this slice, on the owner's sight of the board**: column headings moved onto the cards' own text axis, cards tightened, and columns now flex between a readable floor and a card's ceiling so all five fit a default window before the board scrolls.

**Alternatives.** Deriving "completed" from a merged PR automatically (rejected: completion is acceptance, and acceptance is a person's act — the product's whole posture). A full receipt export and the D-022 artifact store (deferred: the snapshot row with its event range is the durable contract; export is presentation it can grow). Blocking archival of closed missions or auto-archiving on close (rejected: ending and filing away are different acts, a mission can be both, in order, and the record of that is two events). A "View receipt" primary action on the terminal states (set aside with DESIGN.md amended: the canvas already is the receipt, and a button re-showing what is shown is a control doing no work).

**Consequences.** `missions.closed_at/closed_by/closed_outcome` and the `receipts` table join the schema; `mission.close` joins the capability model and `completed`/`cancelled` the wire's state vocabulary; `mission.closed` joins the event vocabulary; `MissionDetailResponse` gains the stored `receipt`. The list and detail projections read the stored outcome first; overlays empty in a terminal room. `close.test.ts` found one real projection bug on the way: the receipt's range originally excluded the closing event itself, which made re-projection differ by one sequence number.

**Revisit when.** Receipt export or the artifact store arrives (D-022); or reopening-as-new-mission wants a first-class "continue from receipt" affordance; or org retention policy needs terminal missions swept to archive on a schedule.

## D-122 — Artifacts are durable evidence with verified bytes, behind D-022's storage boundary realized

**Context.** PRODUCT.md has defined the Artifact and ARCHITECTURE.md its row since the first draft; D-022 chose an S3-compatible store; and every surface that wanted visual evidence — the PR page, the receipt, runtime evidence — has said honestly that none exists. The preview (D-098) put the running application inside the room, which finally gave a capture something true to capture.

**Decision.** The Artifact becomes real as **durable visual evidence with a verified lifecycle**. The durable database stores metadata, provenance, and relationships; bytes live behind D-022's interface, addressed by object keys the control plane generates and scopes, and no signed URL is ever stored, logged, or written into an event or receipt — viewing mints a fresh expiring grant per authorized request, under the same mission authorization as every other read. A capture *begins* by promising kind, MIME, length, and SHA-256; the upload grant is bound to exactly that promise; and only the **store's own confirmation** of length and digest moves the row to `available` — the uploader's word never does. States mean what they say: `pending` and the client's own `uploading` are never evidence, `failed` says why, `interrupted` is a recording with real bytes and a dishonest ending, and only `available` and `interrupted` can be viewed, attached, or cited. A pending upload the reliability sweep finds abandoned is failed by name. Attachments are relationship rows (checks, pull requests) or ids frozen on the decision at record time; the artifact itself is immutable, and every attachment change is an attributed event. Two new capabilities: `artifact.capture` tiered as `workspace.command` (the preview exists only on the machine holding the workspace, and capturing it is operating it — the lease grants it), `artifact.attach` tiered as `review.approve` (changing what the record presents as evidence is a review act).

**The storage boundary, realized.** One interface, two adapters. The **local store** — blobs on the control plane's own disk behind HTTP routes whose sole authorization is an expiring HMAC signature over key, length, digest, and expiry — is the local-first deployment's store and the deterministic test adapter: it genuinely hashes what arrives and refuses a mismatch with nothing left behind. The **S3 adapter** — hand-written SigV4 presigned PUT/GET/HEAD with `x-amz-content-sha256` pinned into the upload signature and the digest echoed as object metadata — is opt-in via `NOVUS_ARTIFACT_S3_*` and is **written, not proven live**: no credentials or bucket exist in this repository, PROGRESS.md says exactly that, and a deterministic run against the local store is never called live S3 proof. An unconfigured store refuses by name rather than degrading.

**Alternatives.** Database blobs (rejected by D-022 already: size economics, and a 200 MB recording in a jsonb row is a database problem forever). Trusting the uploader's digest claim (rejected: the store's verification is the difference between evidence and a claim about evidence). Deletion and org-wide retention management (deferred deliberately: no existing canonical contract requires them, immutability and traceability matter more, and D-014's redaction cascade will reach blobs when redaction itself is built). A `bypass`-style direct-serve route session-authorized per byte-range (rejected: it would put the control plane in the media-serving path forever and make the S3 swap a semantic change instead of an adapter swap).

**Consequences.** `artifacts` and `artifact_attachments` join the schema, `artifact_ids` joins decisions, and the receipt snapshot freezes the evidence set — ids, digests, provenance, and where each was attached, in words — so reopening a completed mission reconstructs the same set and viewing any of it mints a fresh grant then. `artifact.captured/failed/attached/detached` join the event vocabulary. The detail carries the mission's artifacts and the reverse references on checks and the pull request. Capture surfaces (D-123) build on this and add nothing to the trust model.

**Revisit when.** Real AWS credentials and a dedicated bucket exist to run the live S3 proof; transcripts or receipt exports arrive and want the same boundary; or redaction (D-014) is implemented and the deletion cascade must reach blobs.

## D-123 — Capture is the main process's act: a person's click, or an agent's request that always asks

**Context.** D-122 gives visual evidence a durable home. What may produce it: the preview surface (D-098) is a `WebContentsView` owned entirely by the Electron main process, showing one validated loopback origin of one live run process — the only surface in the product whose pixels Novus can honestly attribute, and the only one it should ever capture.

**Decision.** The Electron **main process is the sole capture authority**. The renderer may ask; it supplies no URL, no window id, no path, no object key, and no authority claim — the main process captures only the embedded preview it already validated (D-098's own gates), refuses a stale, foreign, or absent preview in words, reads the worktree revision from git at the moment of capture with dirtiness stated, and uploads under D-122's grants. Nothing ever captures the desktop, other windows, the terminal, approval dialogs, or secret-entry surfaces, because nothing ever captures anything but that one view. Two initiators. A **person** clicks Capture on the preview surface, gated on `artifact.capture`. A **coding agent** asks through a governed first-party tool: the runner composes a `novus` MCP endpoint — a loopback HTTP server inside the Electron main process, per-turn bearer token, one screenshot tool — into every turn's strict MCP config beside the person-enabled project servers (amending D-119's "exactly the enabled servers" by exactly this one first-party entry, stated here so it is never a surprise). Its tool call reaches the same router every tool call reaches, is treated as every MCP tool is — a person's question under every profile short of `dont_ask`, answered on the record either way — and an *allow* additionally mints a one-shot, short-lived capture grant at the runner; the endpoint performs a capture only when a live turn presents its token **and** such a grant stands, then applies every check a person's capture faces. Approval is necessary but not sufficient; the preview's own validity always is. **Recordings** are person-initiated only, produced by Chromium's own recorder (`MediaRecorder`, WebM/VP9 with VP8 fallback) via a display-media handler that hands the hidden capturer the preview view itself — a supported format Electron reliably produces and replays, recorded here because it is a durable compatibility constraint. Recordings carry no audio, stop themselves at a stated bound, can be cancelled leaving nothing, and end as `interrupted` — never as silently successful — when the process, the preview, or the app goes down mid-recording. Temporary encoding files live in a controlled directory with restrictive permissions and are removed on success, failure, cancellation, and the next start after a crash.

**Alternatives.** A skill or prompt telling the agent to run a shell capture command (rejected: a shell command's effects are declared nowhere, which is the exact problem D-097 and D-119 name; a structured tool declares itself). A standalone MCP stdio child process talking back over a socket (workable, rejected for surface: the HTTP endpoint keeps every line of capture authority in the main process that already owns the view). Granting capture from Claude's own settings or a standing allowlist (rejected: a hidden screenshot capability is the thing this decision exists to prevent). Pixel-level secret detection (not claimed: Novus applies its known-secret redaction to textual metadata and refuses credentialed origins, and the interface warns that application pixels may themselves contain sensitive data — stated plainly instead of advertised away).

**Consequences.** The composed MCP config exists on every turn (previously only when project servers were enabled); the argv assertions and D-119's probe notes move with it. The capture path adds two runner-authenticated routes (begin/complete) and no new event kinds. A capture requires the preview **open with a loaded page** — visible on the canvas or not, because the view is the approved surface either way and its page keeps rendering unthrottled while hidden; what it photographs is the live embedded page's own render, never a stale frame and never anything outside that view.

**Revisit when.** Cloud runners need a capture authority that is not an Electron view; a second harness's MCP dialect needs a different endpoint shape; or pixel redaction becomes trustworthy enough to claim.

## D-124 — The turn spine: the room's hierarchy retold as Direction → Execution → Checkpoint → Review

**Context.** Owner-directed on sight of a target layout. The room answered its questions, but not in the order a glance asks them: the title, a state line, a lane line, and an authority row each took their own band with their own spacing, so the header spent height without building hierarchy; the trace told a turn's whole story in one undifferentiated column, so where a turn *stood* — directed, executed, checkpointed, reviewed — was recoverable only by reading it; and the composer's foot named its target in a mono aside easy to miss under the words being typed. D-065 had removed the trace's side rule because an unlabelled line beside everything said nothing; the owner's target says the line was not the mistake — the silence was.

**Decision.** Three re-compositions, all presentation, no behavior. **The header stacks tight**: mission title, then the state line directly beneath it, then one context row — the lane fact (moved from its own line), the baton sentence, a participant count once more than one person is in the room, and the quiet actions (Request control, Try another approach) after the facts they act on. **The trace becomes the turn spine**: each turn renders its stages as labelled nodes on one hairline — *Direction* (the person and their words), *Execution* (machinery line, the harness's speech, workers, tool activity, the outcome), *Checkpoint* (one node per checkpoint, whose row drops its own label because the gutter now says it), *Review* (checks and any waiting approval) — the stage words at the micro step in a fixed gutter, the node filled when the stage happened, awake in `--accent` while it is the live one, hollow while nobody has looked. The hairline is the turn's own and breaks between turns; under 900px the gutter folds into a heading above each stage and the line withdraws. This supersedes D-065's bare column and re-founds signature element 2: lifecycle is still wording and treatment, never a badge — the treatment is now the node. **The composer is the direction dock**: it names its whole target — lane and conversation — as a micro-step eyebrow on the box's own top edge before a word is typed, where the foot's mono note used to hide it.

**Alternatives.** Copying the target's iconography and colored status text (rejected: the header carries no colored text and no icon set — the stage words and the tokens are the theme). The base ref in the header, as the target shows (rejected: prohibited pattern 12 — machinery stays in Overview). A spine spanning the whole feed rather than per turn (rejected: turns are the unit of accountability, and one endless line says "this is all one thing", which is false). Nodes reflecting judgment — green for good (rejected: `--ok` belongs to checks; a stage node states that a stage occurred, never that it went well).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's signature element 2 and the composer's paragraph say the new shape; the state line, every testid, and the trace's reading order are unchanged, so every deterministic and end-to-end assertion holds as written. The checkpoint milestone's inline label is gone — the one word the spine made redundant.

**Revisit when.** A second stage vocabulary appears (plan-mode turns, cloud provisioning) and the four labels stop covering what a turn is; or the Review node gains a real reviewing surface to open.

## D-125 — The spine reversed: a turn is grouped speech and milestone rows, not a four-step timeline

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-124's built result. The turn spine — Direction, Execution, Checkpoint, Review as labelled nodes on a hairline — was judged too opinionated for what a turn actually is: real agent work loops between direction, execution, checks, and revision, and forcing every turn into a clean four-stage timeline read as workflow theater. The owner's line: Novus's signature should come from Compare, multiplayer control, evidence, and decisions — not from making ordinary chat unusually complicated.

**Decision.** The spine is removed the same day it landed; the header hierarchy, the composer's target eyebrow, and the rail rhythm from D-124 all stay. The trace returns to grouped speech in the shape of a modern coding harness: the author's mark and words; the harness's mark once for a run of consecutive speech, the reply still the only `--text-1` in the turn; tool activity as one quiet disclosure beneath it; **checkpoints and verification as compact milestone rows** — a hairline above, the micro label, the facts inline, the action at the row's end — a divider that states its fact rather than a stage in a timeline; the outcome in words, last. Grouping is rhythm, not machinery: tight inside a speaker's group, a breath before the harness answers, nothing drawn down the side. Signature element 2 is rewritten accordingly.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the spine for multi-stage turns only (rejected: two trace anatomies in one feed is worse than either); a horizontal stage strip above each turn (rejected: the same theater, rotated); keeping the hairline without the nodes (rejected: D-065 already found an unlabelled line beside everything says nothing).

**Consequences.** The checkpoint milestone regains its own label, which the spine's gutter had absorbed. Every testid and reading order is unchanged for the second time in a day, so the deterministic and end-to-end suites hold as written. D-124's spine paragraphs stand in the record as a decision made and reversed on sight — the same honesty D-085/D-086 established.

**Revisit when.** A reviewing surface exists that would give a "review" stage a real destination, or turn shapes emerge (plan-mode, cloud provisioning) that grouped speech genuinely cannot carry.


## D-126 — The room sheds its heading: structure lives in the rail, authority lives on the state line

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-125's built result. The foundation was judged better but still wasteful: the giant mission title repeated what the tab strip and the rail already said; three header rows (title, state line, authority row) spent height on facts that belong beside the things they describe; and the rail hid the product's actual structure — Mission → Approach → Chat — behind a rule that a single-approach mission grows no tree, so the ordinary mission looked structureless. The owner's seven points, verbatim in intent: remove the repeated title; place controls contextually (verification beside the checkpoint, Request control with the controller, Try another approach on the Approach row, branch under the Approach); always show the tree; participant marks on the Mission row with a ring on the controller; hide `+` and Archive until hover; stop duplicating the open mission under Needs attention and Projects at once; keep the response readable without a padded container.

**Decision.** Presentation only; every capability keeps its server enforcement and its testid. **The header collapses to one slim strip**: the goal at the row step, truncated with the full text as its title attribute, then the state sentence — and the authority facts move onto this same line's end as the baton sentence with Request control beside it. The mission-step type size is retired. **The rail always grows the tree** for the active mission: mission row, approach row (carrying the lane-context sentence and the `+` for a sibling approach), the selected approach's mission branch beneath it in quiet mono, each session row, and a quiet **Try another approach** row while the lane can fork — the fork control's home moves from the room's chrome to the structure it forks. **Presence moves to the mission row**: up to four participant marks in a tight cluster, the controller's mark wearing a `--accent` ring — the sentence keeps the words, the ring is only where the words don't fit. **Reveals are hover-only**: the `+` and Archive controls appear on their row's hover or their own keyboard focus, never merely because the row is selected. **The attention lens excludes the open mission**: a mission being read never also queues under Needs attention. The response column keeps `--measure-speech`; there is no boxed container around the reply.

**Alternatives.** Keeping a smaller title above the state line (rejected: any heading step repeats the tab and the rail — the goal is a fact on the strip, not a banner). A hover menu on the Approach row holding the fork action (rejected for now: a visible quiet row is one fewer hidden gesture; a menu becomes right when the row earns a second action). Showing the tree only past one approach, as before (rejected: the tree is the product's shape, and hiding it made the ordinary mission look like a chat app). Presence as a count ("3 people") on the state line (rejected: D-066 already chose marks-inline over counts; the count was the thing being removed).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's type scale retires the mission step; signature elements 1, 4, and 5 and the IA's rail paragraph say the new shape. Two e2e specs that asserted the no-tree rule now assert the always-tree rule — a deliberate behavioral-assertion update, not an accommodation. The room's `lane-context` sentence moves into the rail's approach row; `try-another-approach` moves into the tree; `room-goal`, `controller`, and `request-control` live on the state strip. The header's height drops from three rows to one.

**Revisit when.** The Approach row accumulates enough actions that a menu beats a row; or missions with many participants make a four-mark cluster misleading rather than orienting.

## D-127 — The strip sheds the goal too: the room never restates what the tab, the rail, and the direction already say

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-126's built result beside Conductor. D-126 shrank the heading to a compact truncated goal on the state strip — and on screen that copy still sat directly above the first turn, which opens with the person's own words, which for the first turn *are* the goal. The same text three times in one glance (strip, feed, rail — four with the tab), where Conductor states it nowhere inside the transcript. The owner's line: we have the "read the README…" thing again; frankly not sure we need it.

**Decision.** The state strip carries no goal at all. It opens with the state sentence and its action, and ends with the baton sentence and Request control, exactly as D-126 placed them. The mission's name lives where it already lived: the working-set tab (full goal in its title attribute past the tab's own truncation), the rail's mission row, and the direction that started the work. Ten end-to-end assertions that used the room's goal as "the canvas is showing mission X" are deliberately re-anchored, not weakened: room-presence waits move to the state line, which is the room's actual announcement of itself, and canvas-follows-rail polls move to the active tab's full title — the strip is where the window names what the canvas shows.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the goal only while the room's tab is out of view (rejected: the tab strip scrolls the active tab into view by design — the case doesn't exist). Naming the goal in the composer's eyebrow (rejected: the eyebrow names the *target* lane and conversation, a different fact). Dropping the assertions instead of re-anchoring them (rejected: "the canvas follows the rail" is a real behavior and keeps its proof).

**Consequences.** The `room-goal` testid is gone; navigation and runtime specs anchor on `state-line` and the active tab's title. The strip's first element is now the state name, which was already the only weighted element on it. DESIGN.md's element 5 says the room never restates the goal.

**Revisit when.** A surface appears where none of tab, rail, or feed is on screen with the room — a detached or full-screen room — and the goal genuinely has no other home.

## D-128 — The rail says structure with a spine, not with dots: elbow lines, a repository glyph, and the identity dot confined to the room

**Context.** Owner-directed, with Conductor's rail beside ours. Three observations: a project row was a bare name where a small repository mark would anchor the column; the tree's nesting was carried by indentation alone, which reads as a list that drifts rightward rather than as structure; and the lane identity dots (`--lane-current` blue, `--lane-alt` amber) sat on the rail's approach rows — where the lane's *name* is already the row — so the first colored thing the eye found in the rail was machinery. The owner's ask: the icon, the connector lines, and no dots — any dots — with no functionality changed.

**Decision.** Presentation only. **Every project row leads with a repository glyph** — a book from the approved stroke set (1.5px, 16 viewBox), `--text-2` beside the `--text-1` name, always with adjacent text per the icons rule. **The tree draws a hairline spine**: one `--edge` line dropping from the mission's row, a rounded elbow (`--r-control`) into each child — approach rows, then the selected approach's branch and conversations one level deeper on their own spine, then Try another approach, Compare, and the PR row — continuing past a row while anything follows and stopping at the last, all offsets from the spacing scale. The selected approach's children render inside one block so the mission-level spine has a single element to pass through. **The rail carries no lane dots**: the approach row is the lane's name alone — the spine already says "child of", and a dot beside a name said only "different" in a place where the name says it better. The dot keeps its work on the room's working row, session and file tabs, and the Compare columns, where two lanes' artifacts sit side by side and the name is not always on screen — that is the surface D-086 built it for.

**Alternatives.** Removing the identity dots everywhere (rejected: a file tab from another lane carries the dot so "the dot and the bytes on screen can never disagree" — D-088's guarantee needs it; the rail was the redundant site, not the system). A chevron per approach row to fold sessions (rejected: selection already discloses exactly one approach's children — a second fold control would be state the canvas doesn't have). Provider-specific glyphs, GitHub against local (rejected for now: one repository glyph, one meaning; provenance lives in Overview).

**Consequences.** The tree's indents deepen one step (approach text one spacing step further in, sessions two) to make room for the elbows; the branch row joins the chip coordinate space. DESIGN.md's token table, rail paragraph, and Workstreams bullet say the dot's home is the room. No testid, capability, or reading order changes; the deterministic tree evidence regenerates with the spine in place.

**Revisit when.** The tree gains a depth the spine's two levels cannot say, or a provider distinction earns its own mark in the rail.

## D-129 — The spine made legible: a heavier line, and the branch folded into the row it describes

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-128's built result. Two faults: the spine was drawn in `--edge` (white at 11%), which vanishes against the rail — structure that cannot be seen says nothing; and the mission branch, rendered as the first child row on the selected approach's spine, hung at the same depth and rhythm as the conversations — so a mono machinery string read as one more clickable row, and its shorter height broke the tree's pitch besides.

**Decision.** The spine's every segment moves to `--edge-hover` — the border scale's own stronger step, no new value. The branch leaves the spine entirely: it renders inside the approach's chip as the row's quiet second line, mono and `--text-3` under the name, exactly the anatomy the mission chip already uses for its "2 approaches" count — a fact of the row, not a sibling of the rows. The two-line chip's elbow still meets the name line, half the single-line row's height, not the taller chip's middle. Only navigable things hang on the spine; the tree's pitch is uniform again.

**Alternatives.** A dedicated spine token (rejected: the border scale already has exactly two steps and the stronger one is right — a third would be a local design value with a token's name). Removing the branch from the rail (rejected: the owner placed it there in D-126 and the fact earns its place — it was the *presentation as a row* that misled). Keeping the branch row but stripping its elbow (rejected: it would still occupy a slot in the tree's rhythm and still sit where rows sit).

**Consequences.** The branch is inside the approach's click target, which selects the approach — the row's one action, unchanged. `rail-branch` keeps its testid and its assertions. The spine selectors lose their branch cases; the approach chip gains a `side-approach-branching` state with its own elbow geometry. DESIGN.md's rail paragraph and Workstreams bullet say the branch is the row's second line and the spine is `--edge-hover`.

**Revisit when.** A theme lands where `--edge-hover` is too loud for a persistent structural line, or the approach chip accumulates a third line.

## D-130 — The folder open, the branch legible: two refinements on sight of D-128/D-129

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight. The repository glyph D-128 drew as a closed book read as a document; the owner asked for the open-folder shape the coding harnesses (Codex among them) made familiar — a place being worked in, not a thing being read. And the mission branch, folded into the approach chip by D-129 at the meta step in `--text-3`, was now positioned right but still whispered: it is the one fact of the chip the owner actually reads, and quiet buried it.

**Decision.** The repository glyph becomes an **open folder** — same approved stroke set, 1.5px, 16 viewBox, beside the name as before. The branch line takes **weight 500 and `--text-2`** — still mono, still the meta step, still the row's second line; emphasis by weight and one text step, never by color, exactly the house rule for a state name.

**Alternatives.** A closed folder (rejected: closed says storage; open says the work is here). Bumping the branch to the row step or `--text-1` (rejected: it would compete with the lane's name above it — the name is what the row *is*, the branch is its strongest fact).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's rail paragraph names the open folder and the branch's weight. Nothing moves; no testid, geometry, or behavior changes. D-128's "book" stands in the record as drawn and replaced on sight.

**Revisit when.** A second glyph joins the rail and the set needs a documented inventory, or branch names grow long enough that weight alone cannot keep the truncation readable.

## D-131 — The branch leaves the rail, and the spine becomes a token that can be seen

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-130's built result on their own screen. Two verdicts. The mission branch — placed in the rail by D-126 at the owner's ask, folded into the approach chip by D-129, weighted by D-130 — was still judged wrong on sight: a mono machinery string the owner does not want in the rail at all. And the spine, twice strengthened (`--edge` → `--edge-hover`), was still not visible enough on the owner's screen: "I want the actual elbow line bolded — it's not still."

**Decision.** **The branch is removed from the rail**, reversing D-126's placement after two attempts to make it sit right: the approach row is the lane's name and nothing else, and the mission branch's home is the evidence panel's Overview, where every other piece of workspace machinery already lives. Three same-day decisions on one element and then its removal is the record working as intended — D-085/D-086 honesty. **The spine gets its own token**: `--edge-strong` (white 30% dark, black 32% light) at 1.5px, added to the token table with its use named — structural lines that must be seen at a glance — reversing D-129's alternative that rejected a dedicated token: the border scale's two steps are for edges you find when you look, and the spine failed twice because it needs to be found without looking.

**Alternatives.** Using `--text-3` for the line (rejected: its documented restriction is timestamps and disabled labels; borrowing it for borders erodes the token's meaning). Thickness without brightness or brightness without thickness (rejected: each alone was tried in spirit by D-129's step and still read faint on the owner's screen; both together is the decisive move, and one revisit beats two).

**Consequences.** The `rail-branch` testid is gone; the sessions spec's assertion flips from count 1 to count 0, deliberately. The approach chip is single-line again and the two-line elbow geometry is deleted. DESIGN.md's token table gains `--edge-strong` with light overrides; the rail paragraph and Workstreams bullet say the branch lives in Overview.

**Revisit when.** A second surface needs `--edge-strong` (its "nothing else yet" is a fence, not an invitation), or the owner wants any branch fact surfaced closer than Overview — a hover title on the approach row would be the smallest next step.

## D-132 — The spine pared to nesting, and the wash never crosses a line

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight beside the reference mockup. Two faults in D-128's spine as built. The full mission-level run — one vertical from the mission through approaches, Try another approach, and Compare, plus a second vertical through the selected approach's children — put two parallel lines and six elbows in a five-row tree: more structure drawn than structure said, where the reference draws connectors only under each mission. And the selection wash reached left past the connector — the chip's padding carried the depth, so its background began before its own elbow and the line cut through the highlight, reading as a glitch.

**Decision.** **Connectors draw nesting only**: the selected approach's conversations hang on one `--edge-strong` spine with rounded elbows; mission-level rows indent with no lines — the indent already says they are the mission's. **Depth moves from padding to margin** for the conversation chips: the chip's left edge starts after its elbow, the connectors live in the margin beside it, and a wash can never cross a line again.

**Alternatives.** Keeping mission-level connectors but only when the tree is deep (rejected: two connector regimes in one rail is worse than either). Clipping the wash at the line with an inset (rejected: the chip would still sit under the line's coordinate space — margin is the honest geometry).

**Consequences.** The spine selectors at the tree's top level are deleted; `.side-children` keeps the elbow/continuation pair, repositioned into the margin. The sessions' indent is margin-borne; their text lands where it did. No testid or behavior changes; the deterministic tree evidence regenerates.

**Revisit when.** A third nesting level appears (sessions growing children), or the mission-level rows multiply enough that indent alone stops saying whose they are.

## D-133 — The spine aims at approaches, the open path brightens, and the identity dots retire everywhere

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight. Three verdicts on the tree and the tabs. D-132 had aimed the connectors at the nested conversations; the owner wants the opposite emphasis — elbows from the mission into **Current work** and **Alternative**, the structural fact that matters, with the conversations denoted some quieter way. The lane identity dots, pared to the room's tabs by D-128, were still the first colored thing on the canvas ("the tab open in the center has the dot — remove that"). And a selected approach should be findable at a glance: "bold that entire line from approach to it."

**Decision.** **The spine draws approaches**: one 1.5px `--edge-strong` vertical from the mission with a rounded elbow into each approach row; conversations indent with no connectors; fork and Compare rows are actions, not children, and hang on nothing. **The open approach's path brightens**: its elbow, and the vertical of every row above it, render in a new `--edge-selected` token (white 55% dark, black 55% light) — where the canvas is standing, visible from across the room; the fence is that one use. **The identity dots retire everywhere**: the room's approach tabs, session tabs, file tabs, and the Compare pair chips carry names — a foreign lane's session or file tab names its lane in its title, and choosing it still moves the room there, so D-088's dot-equals-bytes guarantee is carried by words and behavior. `--lane-current` keeps its definition and renders nowhere; `--lane-alt` keeps one use, the comparison column's 1px edge. The spine's sibling/selected states are computed in the renderer as classes rather than `:has()` — the pseudo-class failed to match in the shipped Electron and the tree knows its own indices anyway.

**Alternatives.** Elbows at both levels (rejected by D-132 already: two parallel verticals). Brightening the open approach's wash instead of its path (rejected: the wash marks the deepest selected row, which is often a conversation — the ask is about the approach's line). Keeping file-tab dots for the cross-lane case (rejected: the owner asked for no dots; the title names the lane and the tab's click moves the room — the guarantee survives without the mark).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's token table adds `--edge-selected`, retires `--lane-current`, and narrows `--lane-alt` to the comparison edge; element 3's dotted-tab wording is rewritten; the rail paragraph says the new aim. The `.lane-dot` classes are deleted. No testid moves; the deterministic suites hold as written.

**Amended same day.** `--edge-selected` lifted 55% → 85% (light 55% → 70%): beside the 30% spine the first value read as the same line on the owner's screen. Calibration verified by build and a fresh tree screenshot at the owner's direction; the deterministic suites were not rerun for the value change.

**Revisit when.** Same-named lanes make word-identity ambiguous (lane names are user-authored), or a third emphasis state appears and the edge family needs an inventory.

## D-134 — The tree folds from the mission's own row

**Context.** Owner-directed. D-126 made the active mission's tree permanent, and permanence had no escape: with one mission's structure disclosed, a second mission's row sat beneath a tree the owner could not put away — "I can't toggle it off."

**Decision.** The active mission's row carries a quiet disclosure — the project row's own twisty convention, hover-revealed like every quiet control, absolute in the chip's left inset so sibling names stay aligned. Folding hides the tree; the mission row takes the selection wash while its structure is away, keeping D-084's one-selection rule on the deepest visible row; the room on canvas never changes. The fold is this window's own choice for this session — not persisted, not shared: the structure remains the default, and a fresh window starts with it shown.

**Alternatives.** Clicking the active mission row to toggle (rejected: rows are navigation, D-084 — a click that sometimes navigates and sometimes folds is two controls in one skin). Persisting the fold (rejected for now: a folded tree that stays folded across relaunches becomes a preference nobody remembers setting; revisit if the session-local choice proves annoying). A twisty on every mission row (rejected: only the active mission has a tree — a disclosure on rows with nothing to disclose is furniture).

**Consequences.** The sessions spec proves fold and unfold with the tree's count and captures the folded rail as evidence `153-mission-tree-folded.png`. `activeTreeShown` now accounts for the fold, which is what moves the wash. One new testid, `mission-twisty`.

**Amended same day.** The twisty is gone — one control too many: the owner's ask is touch-the-row. The active mission's row toggles its tree on click anywhere along its length (the whole chip is the target, because a short goal leaves most of the row outside the name button and a tap there must not be dead); a non-active mission's row still opens it — the click's navigation meaning is vacant only on the row whose room is already the canvas, which is what D-084's rows-are-navigation rule actually protects. Every spec's "make sure the mission is open" click gained an active-aware guard, since a blind click on an auto-opened mission now folds; the full suite reran green after the change. The `mission-twisty` testid lived one commit.

**Revisit when.** The fold wants remembering across relaunches, or a second surface (Home cards, Compare) needs the same gesture.

## D-135 — Every approach shows its conversations: selection is not disclosure

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight. The tree rendered conversations only under the *selected* approach, so choosing Alternative made Current work's chats vanish — "when I swap to the other one don't close what was open, that doesn't make sense." Selection and disclosure had been fused: moving the room was silently rewriting what the rail showed.

**Decision.** Every approach renders its own conversations beneath its row, always. Selection changes the wash and the canvas — nothing else. The selected approach's row toggles its own conversations on click, the same no-twisty gesture D-134 gave the mission row (a non-selected approach's click still selects it, leaving every fold as it was); the whole chip is the touch target, as everywhere. A session clicked under another approach moves the room to that approach and opens that conversation — D-088's dot-equals-bytes guarantee, the rail's version. Folds are per-approach, this window's own, for this session.

**Alternatives.** Remembering which approach was disclosed and restoring on swap-back (rejected: state that pretends to be the owner's choice but is actually the selection's history). Auto-folding background approaches past some count (rejected: the count that justifies it does not exist yet — revisit if trees grow tall).

**Consequences.** Background approaches' session rows carry their D-094 state words exactly as before; the wash stays singular on the selected path. No testid changes; the sessions and decision specs rerun green focused, the full suite deliberately not run for this round at the owner's direction. DESIGN.md's rail paragraph says every approach shows its sessions.

**Revisit when.** Tall trees (many approaches × many chats) make always-visible children noisy — a default fold policy would need its own decision.

## D-135 addendum — The bright path's real gap: a before-open row's own continuation and children block never brightened

**Context.** After D-135 shipped (every approach shows its conversations), the owner found a genuine visual defect the earlier D-133 fixes had not covered: with Current work before an open Alternative, the top elbow into Current work lit up correctly, but the line directly beneath it — through Current work's own two sessions — stayed the dim spine color, then Alternative's own elbow lit up again. Two bright segments with a dim one sandwiched between them, which reads as a broken line.

**Root cause.** D-133's before-open overrides only reached the approach row's own pseudo-elements (`::before`, `::after`). Two elements in the same physical line were never included: `.side-spine-below` (a real child of the row, resuming the stroke behind the curve's sweep) and `.side-children-more::before` (the separate sibling element drawing the vertical through that approach's own children block). Both stayed hard-coded to `--edge-strong` regardless of whether the row was on the path to the open approach.

**Fix.** Two additional overrides, placed after their base rules so cascade order resolves in their favor: `.side-approach-before-open > .side-spine-below` and `.side-children-before-open::before`, both setting `border-left-color: var(--edge-selected)`. The children block's class list gained a `beforeOpen` conditional alongside its existing `more` conditional.

**Verification.** Not the app: a standalone HTML fixture reproducing the exact DOM shape (Current work before-open with two sessions, Alternative open with one), loading the real `tokens.css` and `app.css`, rendered in Chromium and sampled pixel-by-pixel down the line's x-coordinate. Confirmed `#DADADA` (the exact `--edge-selected` value) unbroken from the top curl through both session rows to the open approach's own curl — zero dim pixels, zero gap. Chosen over a live-app screenshot because the live-turn reproduction hit unrelated runner-registration friction in a throwaway probe harness, and because a pixel sample against the actual production stylesheet is a stronger, faster proof for a pure-CSS geometry claim than eyeballing a screenshot.

**Consequences.** No testid, JSX structure, or behavior changed beyond the one class addition. The existing evidence 152 (Current-work-open, no before-open case to exercise) does not exercise this path; a future screenshot with a background approach that has its own sessions would be the app-level confirmation, not taken this round per the owner's standing "don't run the whole suite" instruction for iteration-speed fixes.

## D-136 — The Decision Room revamped: the goal as title, the fork drawn, one shared row axis, and the lane hues come home

**Context.** Owner-directed, with a target mockup. The room's anatomy was right — no ranking, absence stated plainly, every number the server's — but its presentation was two independent stacks of facts: rows drifted out of alignment between columns, the header said the verb ("Compare approaches") instead of the subject, and nothing told the fork's story at a glance. The owner asked for the upgraded shape: a fork diagram, a structured labeled comparison, and the identity colors doing their work.

**Decision.** Four presentation moves, every testid and recorded string kept (the decision spec passed unmodified on the first run). **The header**: a *Decision room* eyebrow at the micro step, the mission's goal as the title, and one meta line — "{n} approaches · Shared checkpoint · `{sha}`". **The fork, drawn**: a checkpoint chip, hairline branches in the spine's stroke (`--edge-strong`, the D-133 curve grammar) curving into one row per compared lane, each led by its lane identity dot, converging into a *Human decision* chip — decorative, `aria-hidden`, a retelling of facts the columns state. **One shared row axis**: the labels move to a left gutter and every column becomes a subgrid over the parent's tracks, so "Verification" is one line across the room no matter how either side wraps; at narrow widths the gutter goes and in-cell labels return. **The lane hues come home**: `--lane-current` and `--lane-alt` — retired from tabs and rail by D-133 — return for this one surface: column names and fork-map dots, because two lanes side by side is exactly what identity color was minted for (D-080: which lane, never which is better). A standing badge beside each name says `Verified` (`--ok`) only when every check that ran against the current revision passed, `Not verified` (`--warn`) otherwise — the zero-checks value now reads "Not verified at all — no check has run against this", keeping both of the spec's phrases in the cell itself.

**Alternatives.** The mockup's inline radio-and-footer decision flow (rejected: the record dialog already carries the required rationale, the evidence summary, and the artifact citations — an inline bar would duplicate a flow the server contract and spec both pin). The mockup's Cost & time estimates and Open risks rows (rejected: no such domain facts exist — cost renders only as the harness *reported* it, and risks enter at decision time as the decider's own words; inventing product truth is prohibited). Icons in the gutter (deferred: labels alone read cleaner at the micro step; add if the owner asks).

**Consequences.** DESIGN.md's Decision Room bullet and both lane-token rows say the new shape. The room's columns are subgrid (Chromium has shipped it since 117; the fixture and the spec's own screenshots confirm alignment). No behavior, capability, or route changed; the receipt and both dialogs are untouched.

**Revisit when.** A third compared column returns (the pair rule bounds it today); or the owner wants the inline decision bar, which would need its own decision against the dialog's contract.

## D-137 — The Decision Room re-dressed the same day: one quiet sheet, and the lane hues retire for good

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-136's built result, with a sharper critique than any single element: the room "feels rendered out of the 2010s" — clean but dated. And two specifics the owner had now named three times across the session: the colored identity line on the approach column, and dots — both of which D-136 had reintroduced because the owner's reference mockup happened to contain them. The standing taste should have outranked the mockup's incidental details.

**Decision.** The anatomy D-136 built — goal as title, shared row axis, subgrid alignment, standing badge — stays; its dress changes. **One quiet sheet**: the comparison becomes a single `--surface-1` slab at the dialog radius, separated from the canvas by tone, not a border; columns are regions of the sheet, not boxes on it — no column borders, no backgrounds of their own. **The fork diagram is removed** the day it landed: the header already states the shared checkpoint in words, and a drawing that retells the sentence is ornament. **The lane hues retire for good**: no colored names, no dots, and the comparison column's 1px `--lane-alt` edge — pre-dating this session — is gone too; the name at the section step in `--text-1` is the identity. **Tracked caps go**: the eyebrow is deleted (the surface names itself in the meta line, lowercase), and the gutter drops uppercase/letter-spacing for sentence case at the meta step. **Choose demotes to secondary**: two identical primary buttons violated the one-primary rule all along; this surface's primary is the record dialog's own.

**Alternatives.** Keeping a refined fork diagram (rejected: D-124→D-125 already established that drawings of process read as theater here). Keeping the hues at lower alpha (rejected: three explicit owner complaints is a verdict, not a calibration problem).

**Consequences.** `--lane-current` and `--lane-alt` are fully unreferenced; both token rows say retired-for-the-record. DESIGN.md's Decision Room bullet is rewritten a second time today — D-136 and D-137 stand together in the record as a decision built and re-dressed on sight, the D-085/D-086 honesty. The decision spec passed unmodified again; evidence regenerated.

**Revisit when.** A surface genuinely cannot tell two lanes apart by name — same-named lanes are user-authorable — which would need its own decision, and probably words, not hues.

## D-138 — Every approach is a column: the pair rule reversed

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of the re-dressed room with three approaches: "there are 3 approaches but we only check 2." The pair rule — three or more lanes compare as a chosen pair, swapped through chips — was written when columns were heavy bordered cards; on D-137's quiet sheet, hiding a lane behind a picker reads as the room withholding evidence.

**Decision.** Every approach renders as a column, in creation order, always. The sheet scrolls sideways past what fits, with the label gutter held sticky at the left so rows stay readable mid-scroll; the column track count follows the approach count. The pair chips, their selection state, and their eviction logic are deleted.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the picker past some larger count (rejected: the count that justifies it has not occurred; when it does, sideways scroll is still the honest default and a picker would need its own decision). Wrapping columns to a second row (rejected: the shared row axis is the surface's one idea — a wrapped grid breaks the straight read-across).

**Consequences.** The decision spec's pair-picking passage became a three-column assertion — count, order, and no chips — and its evidence renamed to `90-three-approaches-compared.png`. DESIGN.md's Decision Room bullet drops the pair sentence. `decision-pair` and `pair-chip` testids are gone.

**Revisit when.** Missions with many approaches (five and up) make sideways scroll genuinely slow to compare — the answer then is probably column pinning, not hiding.

## D-139 — Branches become choosable at birth, the picker grows up, and drift is said out loud

**Context.** Owner-directed, from one observation: "why can't I open branches?" Novus's mission model always pinned a base (`baseRef` + `baseSha`) and the server contract always accepted any ref — but the interface never offered one: the creation dialog fed it the default branch head, the Add-project picker was a logo wall with no grouping or recency, and nothing anywhere said when a pinned base had moved, been rewritten, or vanished. The owner's scope, agreed in conversation: modernize the import page, add branch choice, and make Novus warn — with base-switching inside a room explicitly rejected (a mission's history is evidence about code descending from its pin; rooms never change what code they are about), a different branch meaning a new mission, and "sync a moved base" affirmed as the direction for the moved-forward case but deliberately deferred (below).

**Decision.** Four parts, one slice. **The base chip**: the new-mission dialog's header gains `Base · default` beside the project picker — a filterable branch menu in the product's one chip-menu style, listing the repository's branches (GitHub through the control plane, a local checkout through this machine's own git), default first. The chosen ref is resolved again at Enter: the pin is the branch's tip when the mission is created, not when the menu was opened. The prepared pull request already targeted `workstream.baseRef`, so PR-follows-base held without a change. **The provider boundary grows**: `listBranches` (host's first page, capped and stated), `baseStatus(ref, pinnedSha)` (current / moved with the host's ahead count / rewritten / missing, from the host's own ancestry answer), and `pushedAt` on repository listings; the fake provider implements all three deterministically, the unconfigured one refuses as it refuses everything. **Drift is said in words**: the control plane computes a GitHub lane's base status on the detail read — cached sixty seconds, refreshed in the background so the room's poll never waits on a host, an unanswerable check recorded as `unknown` and never treated as fine — while a local lane asks this machine's git over the bridge on the same cadence. The workspace row wears the answer only when it demands attention (`base moved — {n} commits ahead` · `base rewritten since this began` · `base branch is gone`, in `--warn`); Overview names every non-current state including could-not-check. **The Add-project dialog is re-dressed**: rows are words — name, default branch, recency — grouped under owners ordered by their freshest repository, the per-row source logo deleted, keyboard hints named in the action row. Every existing testid and state sentence kept.

**Deferred, by name.** *Synchronize a moved base* — the owner's stated direction for the moved-forward case ("if main moved, there should be a way to just sync it"). Not shipped here because a sync button without a conflict story would be a lie: merging a moved base into a mission branch can conflict, and conflict presentation is the still-unbuilt leg of the D-025 continuity contract. It is the named next slice of that contract, recorded in PRODUCT.md so no future review mistakes the warning for the whole feature.

**Alternatives.** Branches inside the Add-project picker (rejected: a project is the repository, imported once; branch choice belongs at the moment it binds — mission creation). A branch switcher inside the room (rejected above; the mission is one base, and Try-another-approach already covers same-goal-different-way). Blocking the detail read on a live host check (rejected: a warning is not worth a slow room; background refresh with a stated `unknown` is honest and fast).

**Consequences.** Contracts gain `BranchInfo`, `BaseStatus`, `pushedAt`, `repos.branches`, `repos.baseStatusLocal`, and a ref on `repos.baseLocal`; the control plane gains one route (`GET /repositories/available/:id/branches`) and a provider-fed status on `getMission`; the desktop main gains local branch listing and ancestry checks. One slice-spec assertion moved from fixture order to keyboard meaning; the runtime spec proves the chip and captures it (`156-base-branch-picker.png`); Add-project evidence (`12`–`14`) regenerated in the new dress. Basing off active feature branches makes drift more likely — which is now visible instead of silent.

**Revisit when.** The sync slice lands (it should absorb the moved-state warning into an action); a repository's hundred-branch cap is genuinely hit; or branch-of-branch missions need the PR chain drawn.

**Amended 2026-08-19, owner-directed on sight**: the results list is bounded (the dialog had grown to the window's height — the scroller now lives inside one height), the "Signed in as" line is gone (the rail's foot already says it), and rows lead with the repository's open-folder mark — D-130's glyph, a shape and never a host logo, so the no-logos rule stands.

## D-140 — Push is an agent capability, and a one-lane mission publishes: Claude Code parity with Novus's spine intact

**Context.** Owner-directed, with a principle stated for the record: *if Claude Code can do it, withholding it needs a strong, defensible reason — otherwise Novus should have the capability.* The owner had finished real work in a one-approach mission on their own repository and found no way to push it: publication was decision-gated and the decision surface only existed at two approaches, so the most ordinary mission there is had no in-product path to GitHub; and asking the agent to push — the way Claude Code is asked — was impossible by construction. The defensible parts of Novus's stance (attribution, no `--force`, mission branches only, per-operation credentials) had been protecting an indefensible part (nobody may start a push without a two-lane ceremony).

**Decision.** Two halves. **A one-lane mission publishes**: the server never had a two-approach guard — the gap was pure interface — so the rail now offers **Publish** on a single-approach mission once a checkpoint exists, opening the decision surface with one column; record the decision (same rationale, risks, and cited evidence) and the existing publish flow — push the decided revision, open the draft PR — lights up unchanged. **The agent may ask to push**: the `novus` loopback endpoint (D-123's capture server) carries a second tool, `push_branch`, under the identical two-key lock — the per-turn token names the asker and grants nothing; a person's allow, routed like every tool call, mints a one-shot grant **for that tool alone**, so a capture allow spends nothing of push. The tool pushes the lane's worktree HEAD — its latest checkpoint; the current turn's work lands at turn end, and the tool's own description says to ask in a follow-up — through the exact hardened path a person's publish rides: `pushMissionBranch`, mission branch only, no `--force`, a write-scoped credential minted by the control plane per operation and forgotten; a local-folder lane is refused by the credential mint itself, in the server's own words; the push lands on the record as `workspace.pushed`. **The line deliberately held**: the agent may share the branch; *opening a pull request stays a person's act*, because a PR asks humans for review under a stated rationale — that is a social act with an author, not a transport step.

**Alternatives.** A separate CP route with approval-verification for agent pushes (rejected: the runner already holds workstream-scoped push authority through the credential mint, and the grant-at-the-router pattern already answers "who allowed this" — a second verification path would be duplicate authority bookkeeping). Letting the agent open the PR too, full Claude Code parity (rejected for now, with the reason recorded above; revisit if the person-click proves to be ceremony rather than authorship). Pushing uncommitted work (rejected: checkpoints are the unit of record; the worktree's dirt is nobody's claim yet).
