The owner's mp4 also surfaced a real contract bug: `ArtifactViewResponseSchema.mimeType` still carried the closed capture enum, so any D-153 attachment kind failed its view parse with "the server answered in an unexpected shape" — widened to the open mime schema.

**Revisit when.** Text kinds want in (a viewer question worth answering deliberately), or a packaged build changes where the temp corner may live.

## D-163 — The preview composites in the room: webview, and the overlay era ends

**Context.** Owner-directed, after the frozen-frame swap still showed cracks over the open-in menu: "I don't see why they conflict — it's sort of two independent processes, one on top of the other. Think like that." He was describing the correct architecture. The native `WebContentsView` paints above every piece of DOM, which spawned an era of compensating machinery — the rectangle keeper and its coverage hit-testing (D-158), the placement heartbeat, the page-zoom conversion (D-160), and finally the frozen-frame snapshot swap — each patching a symptom of the one structural fact that the page and the room could not share a stacking order.

**Decision.** They share one now. The preview renders through an in-DOM `webview`: the page keeps its own isolated renderer process, and the element composites in the room's own z-order, so a menu above the preview is simply above it. The whole overlay apparatus is deleted, not superseded in place — keeper, heartbeat, zoom conversion, snapshot IPC and its bridge surface, the frozen-frame image, the bounds plumbing. Authority does not move an inch: `preview.open` still approves only a live-reported loopback address; `will-attach-webview` refuses any element that is not exactly the standing approval and replaces the renderer's attributes with the locked-down set; adoption applies the same origin-confined navigation, denied permissions, and refused downloads the native view carried; capture and recording keep photographing the adopted page from the main process with unchanged provenance.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the native view and refining the frozen-frame (rejected by the owner on sight, and rightly: it was the fourth patch on one structural mismatch). An iframe (rejected: no process isolation and the loopback page would share the room's renderer). Electron's own guidance disfavoring `webview` was weighed: the tag's instability warnings concern general-purpose embedding — arbitrary sites, plugins, popups; this use is a single, main-process-approved, loopback-only page with every capability stripped, which is the narrow case the warnings do not reach.

**Consequences.** `workspace-preview.ts` rewritten around approval + adoption (~40% smaller); `preview-surface.tsx` loses the keeper era entirely; `webviewTag: true` under the will-attach gate; `preview.setBounds`/`hide`/`snapshot` removed from bridge, preload, and handlers; D-158's keeper and D-160's conversion are retired with their bug class — the loopback-equivalence half of D-160 stands, it lives in the policy layer. Verification: typecheck, build, and the 31 preview-adjacent deterministic tests green; live confirmation and the e2e preview suite regeneration pend the owner's restart, stated honestly in PROGRESS.

**Revisit when.** Electron removes or further degrades the `webview` tag (the successor would be `WebContentsView` plus this era's machinery again, or whatever in-DOM embedding replaces it); or the preview wants multiple simultaneous pages.

## D-166 — The workspace row moves into the header: an action that demands attention must never scroll away

**Context.** Owner-hit: the workspace row — "Workspace ready · {branch} @ {sha}", the base-drift words, and the one Sync action — rendered as the feed's first block, so it scrolled out of view with everything else in any conversation longer than a screen. A base that had moved forward, and the one action that resolves it, required scrolling to the top of the chat to reach — exactly the kind of thing D-139 and D-144 built to demand attention, undone by its own placement.

**Decision.** The workspace row moves from the scrolling feed into the room's persistent header, as a second row beneath the state line — the same header that already never scrolls (D-127's slim state line, the baton beside it). Nothing about the row's own logic changes: `feed.setup` still supplies the label, `baseDriftWords` still renders only when the base demands attention, Sync still offers only for a base that moved forward (never rewritten or gone), and Overview still opens the same section. Only its parent changed.

**Alternatives.** A sticky-positioned copy inside the feed (rejected: two things needing to agree, guaranteed to drift, when the header already exists as the one place in this room that does not scroll — the correct anatomy needs no CSS trick). Duplicating the row in both places (rejected: exactly the repetition D-127 spent itself removing the goal line to avoid).

**Consequences.** `project-room.tsx`'s `workspace-row` block relocates from inside `feed.blocks` to inside `<header className="room-header">`, gated on `detail && feed?.setup` rather than the feed's own conditional render. DESIGN.md's Evidence-inspector and Workstreams paragraphs both note the row's new home. Testids (`setup-row`, `base-drift`, `base-sync`, `setup-overview`) are unchanged, so `e2e/sync.spec.ts` and `e2e/slice.spec.ts` need no rewrite — position-independent queries, verified by reading both specs before moving the block.

**Revisit when.** The header grows a third persistent row and wants a general pattern rather than one-off stacking; or a lane with no setup step at all makes the header's height visibly jump between missions, which nothing here currently smooths.

## D-167 — Evidence jumps to its conversation, when it honestly has one

**Context.** Owner question on sight of the artifact detail view: why does clicking a screenshot land on a provenance page instead of jumping to the session that discussed it? The provenance page answers "is this trustworthy" — captured when, by whom, at what revision, with what digest — which is the whole reason Novus's evidence system exists (D-122/123): a claim that survives independent of any conversation. But roughly half the artifacts *do* have a real conversation behind them (agent-requested captures carry `sessionId`), and for those, "what was being talked about" is a genuinely different, also-legitimate question with no answer on the page at all.

**Decision.** Both questions get an answer, each honest about what it can promise. The provenance page stays exactly as built. A new action, **View in conversation**, renders in its head only when `artifact.sessionId` is non-null — closes the artifact view and opens that session, the same navigation a session tab click performs. A person's own manual capture carries no `sessionId` by construction (D-122: "a person's own click belongs to no chat and stays null — never assigned to the most recent one"), so it gets no button — inventing a destination for it would be exactly the fictional-precision prohibited pattern 11 forbids. The jump lands on the conversation, not a specific message inside it: no per-turn anchor exists in the schema, and the button's tooltip says "opens the conversation" rather than implying more precision than the data has.

**Alternatives.** Scrolling to the precise originating turn (rejected for now: no turn or execution anchor exists in the transcript's DOM to scroll to; a future slice could add one, named in Revisit). Showing the button disabled/explained for person-captures rather than omitting it (rejected: a control that can never fire is not gated, it is decorative — the denied-not-hidden pattern is for capability refusals a person could someday clear, not a structural absence that will never change for this artifact).

**Consequences.** `ArtifactView` gains an optional `onViewInSession` prop, rendered only when both it and `artifact.sessionId` are present; `project-room.tsx` wires it to the existing `onOpenSession` session-tab mechanism (D-089), closing the artifact view first so the jump reads as one navigation, not a layer on a layer. DESIGN.md's artifact-over-canvas paragraph lists the new action in its truthful condition. Evidence: `e2e/artifacts.spec.ts`'s agent-capture test clicks the button by the artifact's own `data-artifact` id and confirms it lands back on `chat`.

**Revisit when.** The transcript grows a stable per-turn or per-execution anchor — then the jump can land on the exact turn instead of the conversation's current scroll position, and the tooltip's honesty gets to promise more.

## D-168 — View in conversation lands on the turn, not just the session

**Context.** Owner-hit, immediately after D-167 shipped: "the view in conversation doesn't actually go to that exact spot where the image is... we need to link these images to which message they were a part of." D-167's Revisit clause named this honestly as a known gap — no per-turn anchor existed, so the jump could only open the session and leave the reader to scroll.

**Decision.** One exists now. Every turn's block already carries a stable identity — `block.key`, the direction that opened it — so the `<article>` wrapping each turn in the trace now carries it as `data-block-key`, a real DOM anchor. On the capturing side, `Execution.startingDirectionId` is already on the wire; `View in conversation` resolves it — `detail.executions.find(execution => execution.executionId === artifact.executionId)?.startingDirectionId` — and hands it to the room as a target. `sessionView` (the feed's per-session filter) is a pure client-side read over `detail`, which already holds every session in the lane, so switching sessions costs no fetch; an effect keyed on `[feed, scrollToBlockKey]` finds the anchor the moment the target session's feed is the one rendered and centers it with `scrollIntoView({ block: "center" })`, then clears the target. No highlight flash was added — D-141's own receipt-scroll set the product's precedent of landing without one, and this follows it.

**Alternatives.** A highlight/flash animation on arrival (rejected: no such flourish exists anywhere else in the room; D-141 scrolls plain, and a new animated affordance for one surface would be its own inconsistency). Storing a scroll target as a query param or route state (rejected: there is no router here — local component state scoped to the one room already reading the artifact is the whole mechanism, and it needs no more).

**Consequences.** `direction-trace.tsx` gains the `data-block-key` attribute (additive, no behavior change to existing tests). `artifact-view.tsx`'s `onViewInSession` now carries `{ sessionId, executionId }` instead of a bare string. `project-room.tsx` gains `scrollToBlockKey` state and the landing effect. `e2e/artifacts.spec.ts`'s agent-capture test extended: reads the execution's `startingDirectionId` off the wire, clicks through, and asserts the resolved block's bounding box centre sits inside the viewport — not merely present in the DOM. Typecheck and build clean. **Honest limit, stated rather than papered over**: a live run of this spec tonight did not reach the new assertion — it timed out 90s into a *pre-existing, untouched* step (the agent's approval-reply wait) under three concurrent Claude sessions' dev processes competing for the same machine; no diff in this change touches that code path, and no uncommitted change existed in the capture pipeline at the time. Recorded as unverified-live pending a rerun under normal load, not claimed as proven.

**Revisit when.** The e2e suite is rerun clean and this note should be struck for a real "Proven in the app" claim; or a turn wants a highlight micro-affordance after all, at which point this decision's "no flash" clause is what gets reversed on sight.

## D-169 — The reload race is real and partly caught; the deeper cause is diagnosed, not yet fixed

**Context.** Owner-hit, live: "r u sure it lands on actual conversation doesnt wokr for me" — hours after D-163's webview rebuild. Instrumented live with temporary logging (removed after use) rather than guessing further, and found two distinct, stacked problems:

1. **A genuine reload-on-return race.** D-098's original native view survived a tab switch untouched — the same already-loaded page, hidden and shown. The webview rebuild broke that invariant by construction: `<PreviewSurface>` only renders while its tab is selected, so leaving the Preview tab unmounts the `<webview>` element and destroys its guest page; returning remounts a fresh one, which must reload from scratch even though the underlying app process never stopped. A capture or recording-start landing in that reload window was refused as "still loading" — reproduced exactly, timestamped: first load 74ms, capture succeeds; navigate away and back; second load starts; a request 8ms later is refused, correctly, on the state as it stood.
2. **A second, unexplained failure underneath it.** Extending the wait from nothing to 4s to 8s did not help, because in the failing run the *second* webview's guest process was **destroyed roughly 7ms after being created** — before its page had any chance to load at all. This is not a slow reload; it is the element being torn down almost immediately after remounting, for a reason not yet isolated. One hypothesis (a render-time side effect calling `onSelectLane` during the preview tab's click handler) was checked directly and ruled out — it fires only in `onClick`, never during render.

**Decision.** Ship what is verified, state plainly what is not. `captureScreenshot` and `startRecording` now wait up to 8 seconds for *this lane's own* preview to leave the "loading" phase before evaluating the existing refusal — a real, independent improvement for the honest slow-reload case (a dev server that takes a second or two to re-serve), with every other refusal reason (wrong lane, unreachable, crashed, stopped, no live process) exactly as immediate as before. It does **not** fix the immediate-destroy pathology — verified by direct reproduction, not assumed — because there is nothing to wait for when the page is torn down before it can load. That deeper cause is real, reproducible, and not yet root-caused to a specific line; it is the true explanation of the owner's report and of the failing capture/recording e2e tests, and it deserves fresh investigation rather than further guessing at 1am against files a sibling session's D-164 restyle is simultaneously touching.

**Alternatives considered and set aside for tonight, not rejected.** The architecturally correct fix — keep the webview element persistently mounted across tab switches (matching D-098's original guarantee), toggling visibility rather than mounting/unmounting — was scoped and understood but not attempted live: it requires restructuring the room's exclusive-canvas rendering and precise positioning against `room-main`'s layout, real work that deserves a clear head and a quiet file, not blind surgery at this hour on a file also mid-edit elsewhere.

**Consequences.** `artifact-capture.ts` gains `awaitReadyIfReloading`, exported and reused by `artifact-recording.ts`. PROGRESS states honestly that the owner's report is reproduced and partly understood, not resolved. No claim of "fixed" anywhere for the destroy-on-remount pathology.

**Revisit when.** Next session, with capacity to either (a) keep the webview persistently mounted (the architecturally correct fix), or (b) instrument the exact render sequence around a tab switch to find what causes the near-instant second destroy — starting from `previewTab`'s own derivation and the `key` passed to `<PreviewSurface>`, since a changing key is the most common cause of an unwanted remount.

## D-170 — The webview stays mounted, painted, and honest about what it's carrying — D-169's fix, done

**Context.** D-169's own Revisit clause named the architecturally correct fix and deferred it: keep the webview persistently mounted across tab switches instead of destroying and recreating its guest page on every selection. A sibling session (`sdlc-harness-b4`) reproduced the same destroy-on-remount pathology from the e2e side, root-caused it to the exclusive canvas ternary in `project-room.tsx` unmounting `<PreviewSurface>` whenever the Preview tab lost selection, and handed the fix back — this being the D-163 architecture's own regression to close. Doing it live surfaced two further, smaller bugs the persistent-mount change itself exposed, both fixed in this same entry rather than left as new open threads.

**Decision.** `<PreviewSurface>` now renders once, unconditionally, for as long as its tab exists — moved outside the exclusive canvas switch to a sibling slot rendered every time, toggled by a `.room-canvas-slot.hidden` class instead of a React mount/unmount. Three things followed from making "hidden" mean "still alive" instead of "gone":

1. **Capture went blind.** `capturePreviewImage`'s `contents.capturePage()` returned an empty image whenever a capture was requested while the person was looking at chat, not the preview — reproduced live via the agent-capture e2e path, which sends its `[fake-ask]` while the room tab, not the preview tab, is on screen. `display: none` removes an element from the render tree entirely; Chromium's compositor has no surface for a `capturePage()` call to read. The hidden state now stays in the render tree — `position: absolute; inset: 0; opacity: 0; pointer-events: none; z-index: -1` — painted but invisible and non-interactive, so the guest keeps compositing and a capture taken from any tab still has real pixels to read.
2. **The scroll landed, then got overridden.** D-168's "View in conversation" scroll-to-turn now reliably found its block (proven directly: a live diagnostic traced the block to a stable position across ten seconds) but the assertion still failed, because a second effect — "stay pinned to the latest activity on new events," keyed on the same session-switch that D-168's landing runs on — fired in the same commit and forced `scrollTop` back to the bottom, since `pinnedRef` still read `true` from before the artifact view opened. `onViewInSession` now sets `pinnedRef.current = false` in the same synchronous handler that sets the scroll target, so the pin-to-bottom effect sees the correct, already-updated flag by the time it runs.
3. **The evidence toast outlived its own action.** Making `<PreviewSurface>` persistent also made its local UI state persistent: `captured`'s six-second-lived confirmation ("Captured at …") no longer reset on every canvas switch, so a screenshot toast from one action could still be showing — genuinely, not just in the fast e2e suite — when a recording finished moments later and expected its own words on screen. `startRecording` now clears `captured` at its own start, matching the clear `capture()` already did.

A fourth thing turned out not to be a product bug at all: the D-168 e2e assertion used `page.viewportSize()`, which Playwright never populates for an Electron `BrowserWindow` — it is `null` unconditionally, so the check `if (!box || !viewport) return false` could never pass regardless of correct app behavior. Fixed in the test by reading `window.innerWidth`/`innerHeight` from the page itself.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the mount/unmount model and only widening `awaitReadyIfReloading`'s wait further (rejected: D-169 already proved this cannot work — a destroyed-before-loading guest has nothing to wait for). Hiding via `visibility: hidden` instead of the absolute-position/opacity approach (rejected untested: Chromium's guest-view compositing behavior under `visibility: hidden` is not documented as reliably distinct from `display: none` for OOPIF-style webviews, where the absolute-position technique is a known-working pattern for keeping a layer live off-screen).

**Consequences.** `project-room.tsx`: the preview branch of the canvas ternary is now a no-op comment; a persistently-mounted `<div className="room-canvas-slot">` wrapping `<PreviewSurface>` sits just above it; `onViewInSession` sets `pinnedRef.current = false`. `app.css` gains `.room-canvas-slot` / `.room-canvas-slot.hidden`. `preview-surface.tsx`'s `startRecording` gains one `setCaptured(null)` line. `runner-agent.ts` and `workspace-preview.ts` are unchanged from D-163 — the destroy pathology lived entirely in the renderer's own render tree, not the main process. `e2e/artifacts.spec.ts` fixed to read the window's real inner size instead of the always-null `viewportSize()`. **Verified live**: `e2e/artifacts.spec.ts`, all 8 tests, run twice in a row, both green — the agent-capture test's D-168 landing assertion included, for the first time since D-167 shipped. Typecheck clean.

**Revisit when.** A second persistently-mounted, non-chat canvas is added (pull view, a future worker inspector) — the same pin-vs-navigate race this entry fixed for the preview can recur anywhere a scroll-to-target lands inside the exclusive-canvas ternary's "else" branch while a stale `pinnedRef` is still true.

## D-171 — An attached image lands on its message, and two canvases never share the screen

**Context.** Owner-hit, on sight, with a screenshot showing both at once. First: **View in conversation** on an image the person had *attached* to a message opened the session but landed nowhere — D-168's landing resolves the turn through `executionId → startingDirectionId`, which only agent-requested captures carry; an attachment has a `sessionId` and no execution, so the exact-turn promise D-168 made was only half kept. Second: the artifact view and the preview surface were painting together, stacked in one column — the artifact opened over the canvas while the Preview tab was still the selected one, and D-170's persistent slot decided its own visibility from tab selection alone, knowing nothing about the artifact view standing over every canvas.

**Decision.** Two fixes, one entry, because the owner hit them in one breath. **Landing**: the wire already links an attachment to its message — `DirectionSchema.attachments[].artifactId` (D-153) — so `onViewInSession` now carries the artifact's own id and, when no execution exists, resolves the landing block as the direction whose attachments name this artifact. Precedence is honest: the capturing execution's starting turn when there is one, the attaching message otherwise, the session's own scroll position when neither is on record. **Delineation**: the persistent preview slot hides when the artifact view is on the canvas, not only when another tab is selected — `previewSelected && !openArtifactView` — using the same painted-but-invisible hidden state D-170 built, so a capture requested mid-read still has pixels.

**Alternatives.** Adding a `directionId` column to the artifact itself (rejected: the link already exists on the direction's side of the wire, and a second copy of one fact is a drift waiting to happen). Rendering the artifact view inside the exclusive ternary again so the slot's tab test would suffice (rejected: that mount/unmount exclusivity is exactly what D-170 just dismantled; the artifact view is a look *over* the canvas by design — D-122 — and the slot learning one more fact is smaller than re-architecting the overlay).

**Consequences.** `artifact-view.tsx`'s `onViewInSession` target gains `artifactId`; `project-room.tsx` resolves the attaching direction as the fallback landing and adds `!openArtifactView` to the slot's visibility; no CSS change. `e2e/artifacts.spec.ts` asserts, at the exact moment the owner's screenshot captured — artifact view open, Preview tab still selected — that the slot carries `hidden`; all 8 tests green. **Honest limit**: the attachment-landing path itself has no deterministic e2e (attachments only flow in `live-attachment.spec.ts`, which needs a live harness); it is verified by typecheck, build, and structural symmetry with the proven execution path, and awaits the owner's own click for live proof.

**Revisit when.** A deterministic attachment fixture lands in the e2e harness — then the attaching-message landing gets a real assertion; or artifacts grow more origin kinds (a check's own capture, a PR comment's image) and the landing precedence list needs a third honest branch.

**Amended on sight (owner, via the D-164/165 session), then generalized minutes later when a file tab showed the same hijack**: the artifact look renders only where the conversation canvas would — never over a file, the preview, the pull page, or Compare. Opening an artifact IS a navigation to the conversation: the selected tab steps back and Compare closes, so the look never contends with another canvas anywhere. The artifacts spec asserts the step-back; 8/8.
## D-172 — The guest paints its own canvas: captures stop coming out black

**Context.** Owner-hit with a recording whose every frame was near-black, faint dark text barely visible — the same symptom the preview itself showed right after the D-163 rebuild. That earlier fix put `background: var(--preview-canvas)` on the *host* `<webview>` element: correct for the person looking at the room, because the app's compositor paints the white backdrop beneath the transparently-composited guest. But `capturePage()` and the recording pipeline photograph the guest's surface alone — the host element's CSS does not exist there — so any page that declares no background (the common class designed against the browser's default white canvas, the owner's RLHF playground included) captured as black frames with its dark text invisible. The e2e suite never caught it because its fixture page declared `background:darkslategray` and so always painted itself.

**Decision.** The guest paints the default canvas itself. The sanctioned lever in this Electron version is CSS insertion (`setBackgroundColor` exists on windows and views, not on a webview guest's WebContents — checked against the typings, not assumed), so adoption wires `dom-ready` — which fires per navigation — to `insertCSS` of one rule: `@layer novus-preview-canvas { :where(html) { background-color: #ffffff; } }`. Named layer plus zero-specificity selector means any page that declares any background at all still wins the cascade; only a page that declared nothing gets the white it was designed against. And the fixture is now the regression's own guard: its declared background is deliberately removed, so the suite's evidence screenshots show exactly what a no-background page captures as.

**Alternatives.** `contents.setBackgroundColor` (rejected by the compiler: not on WebContents in Electron 37 — it lives on BaseWindow/BrowserView/View). Leaving the fixture styled and adding a separate unstyled one (rejected: two fixtures to keep honest where one, made representative of the failing class, guards the bug in every existing assertion's path).

**Consequences.** `workspace-preview.ts`'s adoption gains the `dom-ready` insertion; the artifacts fixture drops its `background:darkslategray`. **Proven visually**: after the fix, evidence `131-artifact-view.png` and `134-recording-playback.png` show the captured page white with black text — before it, both would have been black rectangles. 8/8 e2e green. **Honest limit**: one edge differs from Chrome — a page with a transparent `html` but a `body` background shorter than the viewport propagates the body's color to the whole canvas in Chrome, while the layered white stops that propagation; no real page in this product's path does this, and the failure mode is a wrong gutter color, not lost content.

**Revisit when.** Electron grows `setBackgroundColor` on guest WebContents (the direct lever, better than CSS insertion); or a project's page genuinely relies on body-propagation and the gutter-color edge stops being theoretical.

## D-174 — Settings is a place, and it holds only what exists

**Context.** Owner-directed, with the peer field named: Conductor, Codex, Amp and kin all carry a multi-page settings surface, and Novus reaching for parity should feel no different to operate. The ask placed it at the rail's account corner.

**Decision.** A gear beside the theme control and sign-out opens one dialog of six pages — Account (the GitHub identity and sign out), Appearance (the theme segment, same mechanism as the foot's quick control), Agents (Claude Code and Codex as this machine actually holds them, read by the setup room's own probe), This machine (the local repositories Novus knows, with on-another-machine said in warn), Keyboard (the room's real keys — the chords the code binds, nothing aspirational), About (app and Electron versions over a new one-line `system.version` bridge). The standing rule is the surface's constitution: a page states or controls something that exists; a knob appears here when its behavior does — no notifications page while nothing notifies, no environment page while no such concept exists.

**Alternatives.** A settings *page* on the canvas (rejected: peers use a dialog, and the room's canvas is the mission's). Folding it into the theme popover (rejected: that block is a one-question control; several pages need a nav). Shipping empty placeholder pages for parity's optics (rejected outright: prohibited pattern 11).

**Consequences.** `settings-dialog.tsx`, `GearGlyph`, the rail-foot trigger, `system.version` across contracts/main/preload, settings CSS block. Evidence 199–201 from the live app — the Agents page reading real installs.

**Re-dressed on sight (owner, Codex side-by-side: "settings should be the full page")**: the floating dialog became a full-window takeover — Back to app and a working search field over a grouped, icon-led nav (Personal / This machine / Novus), the page title at the display step, rows as cards: title, plain-words description, the control or value at the row's end. Search is real: it filters every page's rows and lands the hit on its page. The layer overlays the shell rather than replacing it, deliberately — the preview's webview must never unmount (D-170). Evidence 199–201 recaptured.

**Revisit when.** A real preference accumulates (default model/effort, notification behavior once notifications exist) — it gets a page here the day it gets behavior.

## D-173 — A new chat continues from its siblings: the transcript is a projection, never a summary

**Context.** Owner-directed, with Conductor's "Add chat transcripts" on screen: agentic IDEs (Conductor, Amp's thread handoff, Claude Code's own compaction and resume-fork, Cursor's @Past Chats) all carry one conversation's context into another, and Novus had no bridge at the one place its own model says one belongs. Sessions inside a workstream share branch, workspace, files, lease, and scopes by construction (D-083) — the transcript is the *only* thing they deliberately do not share — so "start a review chat that knows what the implement chat did" meant re-explaining by hand. The owner set the boundary himself before design started: within one approach's chats, never across repos or projects.

**Decision.** A new chat may **continue from** one or more of its lane's existing chats. The draft surface (D-077's "the first direction starts this session") lists them as themed checkboxes under a quiet *Continue from*; the overview's session rows carry a matching *Continue* action that opens the draft preselected. On the first direction's submit, each chosen chat's feed is rendered to markdown by the renderer's own feed derivation — a **deterministic projection of the record, like the receipt**, never a model-written summary that could fabricate or quietly drop — uploaded through the D-150 attachment machinery as a new artifact kind, `transcript`, and carried on the direction like any attached file: staged in the worktree under D-153's protections, its path handed to the harness with the words. Server-side honesty is structural: `transcriptOf` on the begin route must name the lane's own session or the answer is not-found; the MIME must be markdown or the kind is refused; the label derives from the source chat's own title, never the spoofable filename; and the row's `csn_id` is the **source** chat, so D-167's "View in conversation" opens what was projected, free. The word is *continue*, never *handoff* — `HandoffOffer` already names the control baton's transfer between people. Approaches are explicitly out: their clean fork (profile, skills, MCP reset — D-115/118/119) exists so competing attempts stay uncontaminated, and a carried sibling transcript would break that on purpose-built ground.

**Alternatives.** A model-written handoff summary (Amp/compact style — rejected for V0: lossy and a fabrication surface; may layer on later as an explicitly-labelled second option). Native harness resume-fork (rejected: harness-specific semantics behind a uniform-looking control, exactly what the harness boundary forbids promising). A server-side projection (rejected for now: the feed derivation lives in the renderer, and reimplementing it server-side is the drift the "one matcher is every judge" rule exists to prevent; the artifact carries source session and digest, so anyone can re-render and compare). A new `directionId` column on artifacts (unnecessary: the direction's own attachment row already links them).

**Consequences.** Contracts: `ArtifactKind` + `transcript`, `TRANSCRIPT_CLAIM`, `BeginAttachmentInput.transcriptOf`, bridge `missions.attachTranscript`. Control plane: the begin route's transcript branch; `direction_attachments` and the runner-view leg accept the kind; schema constraints widened once, at the end, D-035's lesson. Desktop: `components/transcript.ts` (the projection), the draft picker and overview action, markdown artifacts readable in place in the artifact view, kind-honest meta/actor/claim words. Proven: `attachments.test.ts` 15/15 (label from source, spoofed filename never lands, foreign-lane 404, non-markdown 422); `e2e/sessions.spec.ts` extended — a real window creates chat three continuing from chat one, the wire shows the transcript artifact pointing at the source and riding the first direction, the turn completes — evidence `202`/`203`. Typecheck, build, gate clean.

**Revisit when.** A model-written summary variant is asked for (it gets its own kind or a stated `summarized` flag, never this one's name); or transcripts want server-side projection (receipt-grade, needs the feed derivation shared); or a transcript outgrows the staged-file road and wants inlining into the turn's context.

## D-175 — The transcript picker sheds its checkboxes: pills that put a chip in the box

**Context.** Owner-hit on sight of D-173's first surface, with Conductor open beside it: "i dont like the checkbox technique… bigger fan of how conductor does it… seems archaic," and separately, that Conductor shows the picked transcript *inside the chatbox* — which he liked — while our sent message wore the transcript as a full file card that "sticks out."

**Decision.** Three surfaces, one principle: the composer's box shows everything the send will carry, and everything else stays quiet. The draft's checkbox list becomes **Add chat transcripts** — one toggle pill per sibling chat, pressed while picked. Picking one puts a removable `Transcript · {title}` chip in the composer beside the attached-image chips (the transcripts upload at send, so the chips are named intentions, not artifacts yet — stated in the prop's own doc). And on the sent message the transcript renders as one quiet meta-toned pill instead of the document card: context the reader may open, not cargo the turn is about. To make that rendering honest rather than a label sniff, `DirectionAttachment` gains `kind` on the wire (defaulted `attachment` for every row written before the vocabulary widened) — found necessary live when the first run still showed the card, because both direction projections re-shape the attachment rows and had silently dropped the new column.

**Alternatives.** Keeping checkboxes (rejected by the owner in words). A select/dropdown (rejected: hides the inventory a lane's chats are). Sniffing the `Transcript · ` label prefix in the trace instead of carrying `kind` (rejected: a person's own markdown file named the same way would silently wear the wrong chrome — a label is not an identity).

**Consequences.** `composer.tsx` gains `pendingTranscripts`/`onRemoveTranscript` and renders them in the existing chip row; `project-room.tsx` swaps the checkbox rows for pills and wires the chips; `direction-trace.tsx` renders `kind: transcript` attachments as the quiet pill; contracts gain `DirectionAttachment.kind` (and `PreparedAttachment` explicitly omits it); both mappers in `directions.ts` carry it. Proven: `e2e/sessions.spec.ts` re-run green asserting the pressed pill and the composer chip; evidence `202`/`203` recaptured and reviewed — the draft shows pills with one pressed and the chip in the box, the sent message shows the one-line pill.

**Revisit when.** The pills outgrow a row (a lane with many chats wants the picker searchable); or a second pending-intention kind wants a chip in the composer and the two need one shared shape.

## D-176 — The box is the same box everywhere, and its top edge belongs to cargo

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of D-175's chip: the transcript chip must sit at the **top** of the box, and the "Directing a new session in Current work" eyebrow must go — "keep the chatbot consistent everywhere. adding this stuff to the chatbot area is the start of a new modernification where we can add extra context to chatbot." Two instructions with one shape: the composer's top edge is where the send's extra context lives, and nothing else varies the box from surface to surface.

**Decision.** The chip row — carried transcripts first, then attached images — moves above the input, so the send's cargo reads before the words it travels with. The D-124 target eyebrow ("Directing {lane} · {title}") is **retired everywhere**, partially reversing D-124: the selected tab, the rail's washed row, and the context row under the state line already name the conversation and lane, and a box that grows a different eyebrow per surface is a different box per surface. Routing is untouched — a direction still carries the lane and session on screen; what changed is only who says so.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the eyebrow on multi-lane rooms only (rejected: "consistent everywhere" was the instruction, and the lane is already the context row's first fact). Chips below the input as built (rejected by the owner with Conductor's top-of-box chip on screen).

**Consequences.** `composer.tsx`: the chip block renders before the textarea; `contextNote` prop deleted; `project-room.tsx` drops `composerTarget` and its derivation; `.composer-target` CSS removed. Three e2e assertions that pinned the eyebrow's words were updated deliberately, not weakened: `sessions.spec.ts` now asserts the **selected tab** names the conversation (twice), and `decision.spec.ts` asserts the rail's `lane-context` names the lane — each the surface that actually carries the name now. Proven: sessions + decision specs green in a real window; evidence `202` recaptured showing the chip on the box's top edge and no eyebrow.

**Revisit when.** A surface gains a composer with no tab, rail, or context row naming its target — then something must say where the words go, and this entry is what it weighs against; or the "extra context" the owner named grows past chips (pinned files, referenced checks) and the top edge wants a real grammar.

## D-177 — The surfaces get the keys every editor already taught

**Context.** Owner-directed, beside the settings re-dress: shortcuts should exist "for every feature", and the Keyboard page should look like a real registry, not a leftover.

**Decision.** Three global toggles land on their industry keys: `⌘B` the projects rail, `⌘J` the terminal dock, `⌘E` the evidence panel — wired in the shell's one global handler beside `⌘T`/`⌘,`/`⌘1–9`. `⌘J` is deliberately reassigned from DESIGN's never-built "next needs-you item": a shipped standard beats an unshipped aspiration, and the keyboard section now separates shipped keys from the `⌘K` palette destination. The settings Keyboard page lists all twelve real bindings as keycap chips. Editable bindings are the named revisit — a rebinding UI is real behavior worth its own slice, not a decoration.

**Consequences.** project-shell keydown handler; the settings Keyboard list; DESIGN's Keyboard section rewritten to shipped-vs-destination; evidence 201 recaptured. Also the settings takeover's Back button un-broken: Electron drag regions bleed through overlays, so the layer says no-drag with its own top drag strip, the side column clears the traffic lights, and Back to app spans the panel.

**Revisit when.** A rebinding UI (the Codex pencil-and-trash anatomy) or the ⌘K palette gets built — both are the Keyboard page's natural growth.

## D-178 — Every act one keystroke away

**Context.** Owner-picked as next after D-177: "there should be keyboard shortcuts for every feature." Keys don't scale to every feature; a palette does.

**Decision.** `⌘K` opens the command palette, wearing the mission search's existing anatomy — one palette system, not a second. It lists every act the window can take *right now*: Go (Home, find a mission, the project's missions with their `⌘n`, Archived), Toggle (rail/terminal/panel with their keys), Panel (the sections with their chords), Mission (new mission, find in conversation, request control), Novus (Settings, the three themes). Two house rules shape the list: acts the state cannot offer are absent — nothing to refuse — while acts a capability gates are present, dimmed, and carry the same denial words their buttons do (rule 13 in the mirror). The room's find bar opens through a consumed ask counter, forkAsk's own pattern. Rows the palette runs are the same handlers the buttons run; the palette can never do what the interface cannot.

**Alternatives.** A new floating anatomy (rejected: the search palette already is the house's palette). Hiding capability-refused rows (rejected: the palette may not hide a verb the server would allow — same reasoning as every gated button).

**Consequences.** `command-palette.tsx`; `⌘K` in the shell's global handler (closing Settings if open); `findAsk` through the room; the Keyboard page's first row; DESIGN's keyboard sentence flips from destination to shipped. Live-proven: `⌘K → "find conv" → Enter` opened the find bar, `⌘K → "terminal"` opened the dock; evidence 204.

**Revisit when.** New verbs land (they should register a palette row the day they ship), or the palette wants per-mission acts from the rail's tree (sync, publish, compare) — a natural second ring.

## D-179 — The rail is told, not asking: one stream for every mission

**Context.** Owner-picked from the roadmap after landing D-176: "finish the eventing move… do this one first." The room had been event-driven since D-149, but the rail still learned about background missions by brute force — every five seconds, the whole mission list, the archived list, local repos, checkouts, **and a full `missions.get` for every mission in the list**: N+4 requests per tick against a control plane that already knew exactly which mission had moved and told nobody but its open room.

**Decision.** One more stream, same two load-bearing rules as D-149. `GET /streams/missions` carries the address — {missionId, seq, kind} — for every mission the watcher *participates in*, on one connection the desktop's main process holds for the app's lifetime. What crosses is never content, and the set is never the organization's: visibility is participation (missions.ts's VISIBLE_TO), and here the address IS the payload, so an address for a mission you cannot read leaks that it exists. Fan-out is **org-keyed with per-event verification**: the `pg_notify` payload gains `orgId`, the bus gains `subscribeOrg`, and the stream forwards an address only once this watcher's participation in that mission is on record — re-resolved the instant an unknown mission speaks (so a mission created mid-stream streams from its **first** event), cached negatively for the confirmed-foreign, and realigned on a 30-second beat so removals take effect and fresh invitations un-ignore. Deliberately absent: presence and lease touching — those are per-mission facts about a room being read (D-051, D-091), and this connection only proves the app is open. The shell answers a signal with two debounced, bounded reads: the named mission's detail, and the list — whose rows carry the board fields (attention, working, lastActivityAt) the detail path deliberately leaves null, which is why a detail's mission row must never replace a list row. The five-second sweep becomes the 45-second floor the D-149 pattern demands, now also the only reader for what no mission event describes (local repos, checkouts, the archived list).

**Alternatives.** Pushing mission summary rows through the stream (rejected: content on the wire breaks the address-only invariant that keeps the projection the single authority on what a person sees). Connect-time participant subscription with a timed re-resolve only (built first, rejected by its own e2e failure: the home board's mission, created seconds before its approval, sat invisible to the stream for up to the 30-second beat — the org-keyed fan-out with per-event verification closed exactly that gap). Folding the room's per-mission stream into this one (rejected: that stream's presence and lease touching are per-room semantics, and D-149's machinery stands untouched).

**Consequences.** Control plane: `events.ts` notify payload +`orgId`; `mission-bus.ts` +`subscribeOrg`; `mission-stream.ts` +`registerAllMissionsStreamRoute`; registered in `server.ts`. Desktop: `api-client.watchAllMissions`, `watch-all`/`unwatch-all` IPC, bridge `watchAll`/`unwatchAll`/`onAnyChanged`; the shell's stream effect with per-mission detail debounce and a global list debounce. Proven: `mission-stream.test.ts` 15/15 — four new (every participant mission on one connection; a mid-stream-created mission from its first event; **silence** about a same-org mission the watcher does not participate in; every subscription dropped on close) — and the rail-dependent e2e suites (home board, navigation, sessions) 12/12 in a real window, the board spec having been the one that caught the resubscribe gap live. **Honest limits**: the room's nudge still triggers a full `missions.get` (one mission, correctness-critical — delta application into the room's state is deliberately not attempted); the 60-second local-base check in the room stays a poll because it reads this machine's own git, which no server event can describe.

**Revisit when.** The list read itself becomes the cost (hundreds of missions — then the stream wants to carry a projection-grade summary row, and the address-only invariant needs a deliberate amendment, not an erosion); or a second window per machine wants the main process to multiplex one connection to many renderers.

## D-181 — A workspace the machine kept says so, where a person is already looking

*(Written as D-170 in the same hour another session's D-170 already stood on main — the fourth number race; renumbered per the standing convention: the later claim renumbers.)*

**Context.** D-155 gave a closed mission's workspace back, and refused to remove one still holding uncommitted work — the lane is kept, the count reported, nothing deleted. That refusal is the right behaviour and it was recorded as its own honest gap in the same change: the reason went into the event log and **no surface rendered it**. So a person closed a mission, saw nothing unusual, and had no way to learn that their unsaved files were still sitting in a folder. A protection nobody is told about is half a protection.

**Decision.** The Overview panel says it, in the block that already answers "where does this mission's work live": **Workspace — Kept, {n} files are uncommitted**, in the warn tone, with **Copy path** beside it on the machine that holds the folder.

Only `kept` earns a surface. `released` and `absent` are the workspace being gone, which is exactly what ending a mission is supposed to do; announcing it would be the product congratulating itself for working.

The path is **not** in the event and is not put there. Machine-local paths are masked before anything leaves the runner (D-032), and a folder on somebody's Mac means nothing to a participant reading the same room from another one. Copy path resolves it locally, through the verb D-159 already built, and renders only where the checkout actually is.

Read from the events rather than carried as a column on the workstream: it is one fact that changes at most once per mission, at its ending, and the room already derives what it shows. The **last** such event wins, because a close can be attempted more than once and a surface still saying "kept" after the lane was settled and removed would send somebody looking for a folder that is gone.

**Alternatives.** A column on the workstream projection (rejected: a schema change and a migration for one fact the event log already carries, and the derivation is four lines). Putting the path in the event (rejected: D-032 masks machine-local paths for a reason, and the path is meaningless to every reader on another machine). A dialog on close (rejected: the mission is ending and a modal in the way of that is the product interrupting the one act it should get out of the way of; Overview is where a person goes to ask where things are). Announcing every outcome (rejected above).

**Consequences.** `keptWorkspace` joins `derive.ts` beside the room's other derivations rather than living inside the panel, so it is exercised in plain Node: `room-presentation.test.ts` (+5 — the count, both quiet outcomes, a mission that has not ended, the newest answer winning after a second close, and another lane's workspace never reported as this one's). DESIGN.md's Overview list gains the row. D-155's stated gap closes; the other one it named — worktrees from missions that ended before it existed — stays open and stays named.

**Revisit when.** A settle-it-here action belongs on that row (open the folder, discard, or commit), which is a different decision about who may do what to a lane whose mission has ended; or terminal-mission retention arrives and a kept workspace becomes something a sweep has an opinion about.

## D-180 — The machine tells you when the room needs you

**Context.** Owner-picked: the biggest gap against Conductor and Codex was silence — a turn could finish or the agent could ask a question while the person was in another app, and nothing said so.

**Decision.** Native notifications for exactly two moments, because exactly two are a person's to act on: **a turn ending** (completed, or failed/interrupted — the work is ready to read) and **needs you** (the harness asked; nothing moves until somebody answers). A *stopped* turn stays silent — the person themselves asked. Both are suppressed while the window is focused: a watcher needs no echo. Each moment is its own machine-local switch, on by default, on the settings page this feature finally earns (the D-174 rule holding: the page landed the day its behavior did). The body is the mission's own goal, fetched when the moment comes; the click focuses the window and opens the mission that asked. The notifier is a pure gated module with the OS notification injected — the runner's only addition is a `notify` callback at its turn-terminal and approval-requested sites.

**Alternatives.** Notifying every event kind (rejected: a feed relocated into the notification center is noise, not attention). Renderer-side notifications (rejected: the renderer may be reloading or hidden; the main process owns the window and the runner already lives there). OS notification permissions flow (nothing to build: Electron's Notification asks the OS itself).

**Consequences.** `notifications.ts` (+5 gating tests), the runner's notify hooks, main's notifier + prefs IPC + open-mission focus path, the bridge's `notifications` namespace, the settings Notifications page, the shell's open-mission listener. Live-proven: the page's switch flipped Off persisted through the real IPC (`{turns:false, needsYou:true}` read back); evidence 205.

**Revisit when.** Notifications want mission-level muting, sound, or a "checks failed" third moment — each is a switch the day it exists.

## D-182 — The composer's top edge grows a grammar: pinned files and referenced checks

**Context.** Owner-picked, in the same breath as the transcript restyle that created the slot: "adding this stuff to the chatbot area is the start of a new modernification where we can add extra context to chatbot" — then, from the candidate kinds, "do 1 and 2 first": worktree files, and verification checks (a failing one above all). These are the two things people paste into agent chatboxes by hand everywhere.

**Decision.** A direction may carry **pinned references** — at most eight — beside its words: `{kind: "file", path}` for a worktree file the turn should look at, and `{kind: "check", checkId, name, outcome, command, output}` for a check it should answer. References, never bytes: the agent already holds the worktree, so a file travels as the path the person pointed at; a check's facts are **snapshotted at submit** (the D-043 discipline) so the turn runs against what the person actually saw, not whatever the row says by the time it runs. They ride the direction row (a `context` jsonb column), are served back on the wire, ride both dispatch payloads beside the attachments, wear chips on the composer's top edge exactly like transcripts (removable, read before the words they steer), and render on the sent message. Picked from the surfaces that already show them: the inspector's file and check rows, and the open file view.

**Alternatives.** Staging pinned files as attachment blobs (rejected: the worktree is already the agent's — a copy would be a second truth that drifts by the next checkpoint). Resolving a check's output at dispatch instead of submit (rejected: the person referenced the failure *they were looking at*; a re-resolve could silently swap in a different run's output). @-mention autocomplete in the textarea (deferred, not rejected: the chip grammar is the substrate it would decorate).

**Consequences.** Landed in two parts, honestly. **Part 1 (this entry)**: contracts (`DirectionContextRefSchema`, `MAX_DIRECTION_CONTEXT`, direction/input/bridge surfaces), the `directions.context` column, storage at submit, serving on the wire, both dispatch payloads, and the composer/room chip wiring — proven by `sessions.test.ts` (+2: stored-served-dispatched exactly as submitted; the ninth reference refused in words). Part 1 ships partly because a sibling slice's commit swept the contract hunks onto main ahead of their entry (the gate's cited-but-never-written check went red for everyone), and restoring consistency beat holding the whole slice. **Part 2 (follows immediately)**: the inspector/file-view pickers, the sent-message chips, and the runner's prompt block that actually hands the references to the harness — PROGRESS states plainly that until part 2 lands, a pinned reference is carried and displayed but not yet spoken to the agent.

**Revisit when.** A third reference kind arrives (a checkpoint, an artifact, a sibling chat's single message — each named in the design conversation); or @-mention wants building on top; or eight proves the wrong bound.

## D-183 — What an approach's row does, in the order a person wants it

**Context.** Owner-reported, with the sequence: fold an approach's conversations, go to its sibling, come back — and the click that chooses it changes nothing on screen. *"i cant open that again… the workaround is if i click on another approach inside of that then i click it and see it."*

D-134 amended the rail so the selected approach's row folds and unfolds its own conversations, and said in the same breath that any *other* approach's click selects it "leaving every fold as it was". That second half was written to protect somebody's disclosure from moving when the selection did, which is right for the lanes you are not choosing — and wrong for the one you are. A folded lane you return to swallows the click: nothing moves, so the control reads as broken, and the way back is a second click nobody discovers except by accident.

**Decision.** Selecting an approach **unfolds it**. Choosing a lane is asking to see it, and a request to see something that answers with nothing is not a fold, it is a dead control.

Every other lane's fold still survives the selection moving, which is what D-134's rule was actually for. And the selected row stays a toggle: once it is the lane you are reading, its click folds and unfolds as before.

**Alternatives.** Leaving it and treating the second click as the way (rejected: that is the bug, described as a feature). Folding the lane you leave (rejected: it moves a disclosure the person did not touch, which is exactly what D-134 refused). Never folding on selection *or* on click, and putting a twisty on the row (rejected: D-134 removed that twisty deliberately — the row is the target, and a chevron on every lane is the row-controls accretion the rail has been pruning since D-084).

**Amended immediately, same report, and the amendment is the part that mattered.** The first fix read the report as being about the fold and was not wrong, but it was not the bug: *"if i click inside the approach and i want to go back to seeing stuff in the approach page i cant, it just toggles it off. the workaround was i had to go to another mission then i can enter the approach page."*

An approach's own page — its intent, its facts, its conversations as links (D-089) — **had no way back from inside one of its conversations.** The row's only behaviour when selected was the fold, so once the canvas was in a chat, the overview was unreachable except by leaving the mission entirely and returning. A page you can only reach by accident is a page that does not exist.

So the row does three things, ordered by what a person wants at that moment:

1. **Not the lane you are reading** → select it, and unfold it.
2. **The lane you are reading, from inside one of its conversations** → go back to the approach's own page.
3. **The lane you are reading, already on its page** → fold and unfold its conversations, which is all that is left to mean.

The ordering is the decision. The fold is the *least* likely thing a person wants from that row, so it is what the row does only when nothing else is left; navigation comes first because the row is a place, and a place that will not take you there is a decoration.

**Consequences.** Three branches in the rail's `activate`. The decision spec — the only fixture with two approaches, which is what this needs — now folds a lane, leaves, returns in one click, and then toggles it twice, because a toggle that works once has state somewhere it should not. The sessions spec was where this was first probed and it passed, which is the honest reason the bug survived: one approach cannot express it.

**Revisit when.** A lane can be folded from somewhere other than its own row, and "selection unfolds" starts fighting an explicit fold made elsewhere; or the fold wants a gesture of its own, at which point the row is navigation alone and the ordering above collapses to one thing.

## D-184 — One palette: the mission search folds into ⌘K

**Context.** Owner, with Conductor's ⌘K beside ours: "it also includes the search stuff… let cmd k have everything, merge them." We had two palettes wearing one anatomy — ⌘K for commands, the rail's Search for missions.

**Decision.** One. The palette gains a **Missions** group — every mission across every project, labelled `goal · project` so typing either finds it, the current project's first nine keeping their `⌘n` hints — and the separate search dialog is deleted, not hidden: the rail's Search row now opens the same palette. D-066's deliberate scope survives inside the merge: missions and not code, because the rail's job is getting to a room.

**Alternatives.** Keeping both with cross-links (rejected: two surfaces wearing one anatomy for one kind of question is the confusion the owner named). Missions-first ordering over commands (deferred: groups are stable and typing disambiguates; reorder if use shows reaching past commands).

**Consequences.** SearchDialog deleted from project-shell; rail-search rewired to the palette; the palette's per-project mission entries widened to all projects. Live-proven: the rail's Search opened the palette and a typed mission opened its room (the palette closing on run asserted); evidence: the palette shot with the Missions group.

**Revisit when.** Mission count makes the flat group heavy (recency ordering or per-project subheads), or code search genuinely arrives — as its own feature, not this control.

## D-185 — @ speaks the worktree: the mention is an input method for chips

**Context.** Owner-asked, mid-evening: "if i type and do @ i should be able to refer stuff from codebase … like in conductor codex every other session … start with that." D-182 had just named @-mention as the deferred decoration on the chip grammar; the ask makes it now, files first.

**Decision.** Typing `@` in the composer opens a popover of the worktree's files — **git decides what the codebase is** (`git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard`, so build output and node_modules never crowd the list), filtered by the token typed after the @, at most twenty candidates crossing the bridge and eight shown. Arrow keys move, Enter or Tab picks, Escape closes, and while the popover is open Enter never sends. Picking is not insertion: the file lands as a **D-182 context chip** and the `@query` text is consumed — the chip grammar is the one place cargo shows, so the mention is an input method for it, not a second annotation system. Everything downstream is D-182 unchanged: dedupe, the eight-reference cap, the dispatch payload, the prompt block, the quiet pill on the sent message. Where no worktree exists yet (the ask-dialog), @ stays an ordinary character — never a dead popover.

**Alternatives.** Keeping `@path` inline in the text beside the chip (rejected: two renderings of one reference, and the text one would go stale against renames). Walking the tree-level `listFiles` per keystroke (rejected: one flat bounded git read is the cheaper and truer census). Fuzzy matching (deferred: substring-on-path covers the ask; a ranker can land behind the same popover).

**Consequences.** `workspace.searchFiles` (main-process git read, masked-path discipline, bounded), the bridge/preload/IPC leg, the composer's token detection + popover + key handling, the room's mention provider feeding the same `pendingContext`. Proven in a real window: `e2e/sessions.spec.ts` +1 — type `@READ`, the popover offers README.md, Enter pins the chip and consumes the query, Enter then sends as ever, and the wire carries `{kind:"file", path:"README.md"}` (evidence `208`, reviewed). **Honest observation recorded, not hidden**: in this suite's full sequence the canvas lands on the approach overview after the previous test's turn settles — nothing in the test navigates there, the repro is deterministic, and the cause is not yet found; the @ test now composes from an explicitly selected chat (what a person does), and the observation is in PROGRESS for its own hunt.

**Revisit when.** @ wants more kinds (checks, chats, checkpoints — the popover grows groups, the grammar already carries them); or the file list wants fuzzy ranking; or the overview-jump observation reproduces live and earns its investigation.

## D-186 — What rides along is never silent: the machine's global skills are published for display

**Context.** D-118 measured and left standing a limit: the CLI loads the operator's user-level skills (`~/.claude/skills`) into every turn no matter what Novus pins — no flag it passes removes them. The owner met the fact from the product's side (2026-08-20): the inspector's skills section rendered nothing while his machine's own skills rode into every turn unlisted. Asked whether to suppress them by redirecting the config dir, the answer was the opposite — "I do wanna keep them … right now it's silently adding them" — the skills are wanted; the silence is the defect. A teammate reading the record cannot see what shaped a turn, and the list would change without visible cause if the runner ever moved machines.

**Decision.** The runner reads the machine's user-level skills the way the CLI resolves them — `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` when set, `~/.claude` otherwise, then `skills/` — under the manifest's own bounds, and publishes them on `workspace.declared` as a separate `globalSkills` list: names, descriptions, sizes, digests, never bodies. The control plane stores them beside the project's (`declared_global_skills`) and serves them on the workspace. The inspector renders them under Project skills in the same row grammar, wearing `global · always on` in words — never a badge — with no action and a foot sentence saying whose machine they ride from. Display only **by structure**: they live in their own list, so `skills.set` — which reviews against the project manifest alone — refuses a global name as unknown (409, test-pinned), and nothing global is ever pinned, composed, or carried by Novus. The CLI's own loading, now on the record.

**Alternatives.** Suppressing them via a redirected config dir (rejected by the owner: visibility was the ask, not removal; noted as the mechanism a future takeover of user-level loading would use). Making the rows enableable (rejected: Novus does not load these, so a toggle would be presentation-only — rule 13's exact sin). A `source` tag inside the one `skills` array (rejected: the enable route would have to learn to refuse a source, where a separate list cannot be named at all). Worktree-style containment on the read (rejected: a personal skill symlinked out of a dotfiles repository still loads, and a listing that skipped it would lie; the read keeps the size and name bounds only).

**Consequences.** Contracts gain `GlobalSkillsSchema` on the workspace and the declared event (defaulted, so an older runner's report still validates); `workspaces.declared_global_skills`; the runner's announce publishes and re-publishes on change like the rest of the manifest. Publishing puts the operator's home-machine skill names in front of every mission participant — deliberate; that visibility is the point. The empty skills section the owner hit now almost always has rows on a local runner.

**Revisit when.** Novus takes over user-level loading — a redirected config dir with chosen globals composed exactly like project skills — at which point these rows earn a real enable control; or runners move to machines nobody owns, where "global" names nothing.

## D-187 — / speaks the project: slash commands are governed like skills and offered from the composer

**Context.** The owner asked for "all the Claude stuff" Conductor has, starting with slash commands (2026-08-20). The audit found none anywhere: the composer had no `/` handling, and D-118's composed plugin directory deliberately never wrote commands — excluded in the same breath as hooks, though the *reason* only ever held for hooks and MCP servers, which escalate. A command is a prompt template: instructions the same way a skill is, granting nothing, expanded by the CLI into the turn's own prompt. Excluding it was blast-radius caution, not principle.

**Probed, against installed `claude 2.1.237` (2026-08-20).** A hand-composed plugin directory carrying `commands/relnotes.md` under the full pinned argv (`--setting-sources ""`, `--plugin-dir`): the session's init line lists `novus-project-commands:relnotes` among its slash commands, and invoking `/…:relnotes alpha beta` headless expanded the template — the model opened with the template's codeword and acknowledged `$ARGUMENTS`. ($0.003, one haiku turn.)

**Decision.** The worktree's `.claude/commands/<name>.md` files ride D-118's machinery end to end. The runner publishes them on `workspace.declared` as a `slashCommands` manifest (name = filename minus `.md`, under the skill-name grammar; frontmatter description; digest; bytes). Enabling is `skills.set` — the same tier as skills, because it is the same kind of act — through its own route, recorded as one `commands.changed` event, pinned into both turn commands at dispatch. At spawn the same composed plugin directory gains `commands/<name>.md` per enabled command, digest-verified with the verified bytes written; the manifest description drops its "never commands" clause while **hooks, `.mcp.json`, and agents stay never-written** — D-072's boundary, now stated at its true width. The turn's record states `Slash commands carried:` and drops by name, the skills' grammar. In the composer, `/` as the first character opens a popover of the lane's enabled-and-current commands (the mention popover's exact anatomy and key grammar); picking inserts the full registered invocation `/novus-project-skills:<name>` and the person types the arguments — the prompt *is* the command, so dispatch needed nothing new. Built-in CLI commands are deliberately out of scope: interactive ones have no terminal here, and the rest earn their own look.

**Alternatives.** Surfacing built-ins in the same menu (deferred: their headless semantics are unprobed and their jobs — `/plugin`, `/config` — are Novus's own product surface already). A separate capability `commands.set` (rejected: same risk class as skills, and a second tier would imply a difference that does not exist). A second plugin directory for commands (rejected: one composed artifact, one containment story). Expanding templates Novus-side before dispatch (rejected: the CLI owns `$ARGUMENTS` and its command semantics; re-implementing them invites drift).

**Consequences.** Contracts: `slashCommands` on workspace, declared payload, running event, and workstream (`enabledSlashCommands`); `EnabledSlashCommandsSchema`; the client's `setEnabledSlashCommands`. Persistence: `declared_slash_commands`, `enabled_slash_commands`. The inspector gains a Slash commands section between skills and MCP; the composer gains the `/` popover; the trace gains the carried/dropped rows. D-118's enumeration test now asserts skills + commands + manifest and still no hooks, `.mcp.json`, or agents.

**Revisit when.** Built-in commands earn their probe; or a command wants supporting files (D-118's same deferral); or the plugin's registered prefix (`novus-project-skills:`) reads wrong enough in the room to be worth renaming the composed plugin and the evidence that names it.

## D-188 — The / menu offers what the harness already has: its own commands, captured from its own announcement

**Context.** D-187 shipped the governed half — repo commands — and the owner typed / and got nothing, because his projects declare none. What he wanted was what the CLI's own terminal and Conductor show: Claude's built-in and user-level commands ("these slash commands are not supposed to be in a repo right"). Those already load into every Novus turn regardless of the pinning (D-072 measured ~45) and already work when typed; the only thing missing was the list.

**Decision.** The list comes from the one honest source: the CLI itself. Every session's `system/init` line enumerates `slash_commands` and marks its `terminal_slash_commands`. The harness stream hands that announcement out; the runner remembers it per machine (`global-slash-commands.json` in userData, atomic write, filtered — no terminal-only entries, no plugin-scoped names, schema-refused shapes dropped, bounded at 200) and publishes it on `workspace.declared` as `globalSlashCommands`, names only. Stored (`declared_global_commands`), served on the workspace, and offered by the composer's / menu after the project's governed rows, each inserting plainly as `/name`. Nothing to enable, because nothing new is granted: the menu tells the truth about what a typed /command would already do. The fake harness announces a deterministic list so the loop is provable end to end.

**Alternatives.** Discovering `~/.claude/commands` as files (rejected: built-ins are not files; two mechanisms where the init line is one). A probe turn at enrolment to warm the list (rejected: spends real quota for a menu; the first genuine turn warms it free). Filtering to a curated "known prompt-shaped" allowlist (rejected: maintaining a mirror of the CLI's evolving command set is drift on a timer; the CLI's own terminal-only marking is the boundary it maintains itself). Publishing per-lane rather than per-machine (rejected: the list is machine+CLI truth, identical for every lane the runner serves).

**Consequences.** A fresh machine offers no globals until its first turn has run anywhere — stated, visible, self-healing. `GlobalSlashCommandsSchema` on workspace and declared payload; `HarnessStreamOptions.onSlashCommands`; the capture module; the menu's second group. The D-186/D-187 rows in the inspector are untouched — a global command needs no review surface, the menu is its surface.

**Revisit when.** The CLI grows a way to enumerate commands without a session (retires the capture-on-turn lag); or descriptions for built-ins become available and the rows earn their second half; or a headless-inert built-in shows up unmarked, at which point a small exclusion earns itself.

**Amended within the hour, owner-hit ("still dont see it did i do it right").** The owner ran a real turn and / stayed empty. The capture worked; the publish never ran: discovery announced a lane's manifest **only at first enrolment**, so a restarted app served whatever the previous run had published — a standing gap that had also been starving D-186's global skills for existing missions. Two fixes, both in the runner: discovery now announces each enrolled lane **once per app run** (the ordinary republish paths carry changes after that), and a capture that actually changed the stored list republishes its mission immediately, so the menu fills seconds after the machine first hears the announcement instead of on the next turn. `e2e/skills.spec.ts` re-run 4/4; the restart path is the app's own launch, verified live on the owner's machine.

## D-189 — Extensions are their own surface, and no row wears a tag

**Context.** After D-186/D-187/D-188, the inspector's Overview was carrying four groups of rows and three foot-paragraphs on top of the mission's facts, and every global row wore `· global · always on` inline. The owner on sight: "i hate the little . global . always on use tags also im not sure how good this is in overview... it j feels tacky and icky." Asked to choose between restyling in place, relocating, or building real global control, he chose relocation.

**Decision.** The workspace drawer gains a third section, **Extensions** — Overview · Extensions · Output — holding everything the lane's harness is handed beyond the repository: the project's governed groups (skills, slash commands, MCP servers, rows and refusals unchanged) and, last and quietest, **From this machine · always on** — one group heading that states the global fact once, so the rows under it are just name and description, no suffix, no control. The machine's captured slash commands appear as one counted sentence in that group's meta line rather than as rows — the / menu is their surface. Each governed group's foot shrinks to one line. Overview keeps a single kv row, `Extensions · {n} of {m} enabled · {k} from this machine`, which is a text action opening the section; a lane with nothing to say shows no row at all.

**Alternatives.** Restyling inside Overview (declined by the owner's pick: the section didn't belong there, not just look wrong there). A dialog off the summary row (rejected: these rows are live multiplayer state on the room's poll, and the drawer is where workspace state already lives). Real enable/disable for globals (deferred, its own slice: Novus would first have to take over user-level loading — until then a toggle is presentation-only, rule 13).

**Consequences.** `WorkspaceSection` gains `extensions`; the sections move; `ManifestRows` loses its tag-and-no-button special case; DESIGN.md's Overview anatomy moves to the new section; PRODUCT.md names the collective term. e2e re-drives the flow through the new tab and the summary row's open action, screenshots regenerated.

**Revisit when.** Globals earn their real toggle (the group's heading loses "always on" for enabled/disabled words), or the drawer's section count makes tabs the wrong shape.

## D-190 — Origin is a tag; state stays a word. Extension groups fold

**Context.** The owner, on the relocated Extensions section: "i need this to be a sort of open close thing where i can shrink these also get rid of the fucking dot between from this machine alwaus on. j have a tag system yk tags like colored tags for global or not."

**Decision.** Two changes, and one line held. Each group in Extensions is a **disclosure**: the heading is the control across its whole width — fold glyph, name, origin tag, and the count at the end so a shut group still says how much it holds — remembered per group on this machine. And origin becomes a **tinted tag**: `in repo` and `this machine`, in the file badge's exact anatomy and tokens (D-048's `--term-*` hues at 14%, no colour invented), one per *group* rather than per row, because the groups are homogeneous and thirty repeated tags is the tackiness being removed. The dotted `· global · always on` suffix is deleted.

**What is held:** DESIGN.md's status rule (D-047) is untouched — *state* is still words, never a pill: `enabled`, `changed since enabled`, `no longer in the project`. The tag carries **provenance**, which is a fact about the thing the way a file's extension is (D-048's own reasoning, and its own tokens), never what the thing is doing.

**Alternatives.** A tag per row (rejected: the group already says it once — that repetition was the complaint). Hue for enabled/disabled (refused: that is status by colour, D-047's exact prohibition, and it would make the two vocabularies indistinguishable). Distinguishing "written by us" from "vendored from outside" inside the project group (**not built, and not knowable**: a repository records no such provenance, and inferring it from git history would be a guess wearing a tag — the honest split available is repo versus machine, which is what the tags say).

**Consequences.** `ExtensionGroup` and `OriginTag` in the inspector; `.origin-tag` / `.extension-group-*` in the stylesheet from existing tokens; the machine group's caption folds into its heading. DESIGN.md's Extensions anatomy updated.

## D-191 — The machine's own skills never loaded. Novus carries the chosen ones

**Context.** The owner asked why global skills could not be enabled; the answer given was "Novus does not load them, the CLI does, so a toggle would be presentation-only." Building the slice that would take loading over began with a probe — and the probe said the premise was false in the other direction.

**Measured, against installed `claude 2.1.237` (2026-08-20).** Under Novus's pinned argv (`--setting-sources ""`): **16 skills, and neither of the operator's own** (`unslop`, `thermo-nuclear-code-quality-review` absent). With `--setting-sources user`: **19 skills, both present.** The 16 are the CLI's built-ins.
