So a person's `~/.claude/skills` have **never** reached a Novus turn — D-118 recorded the opposite as a standing limit ("the operator's own user-level skills still load"), inheriting D-072's misreading of its own 16-skill measurement, and D-186 then built a display on it that told every reader these rode along. The product was making a false claim about what shaped a turn, which is the one thing this product exists not to do (PRODUCT.md P3).

**Decision.** The correction and the feature are the same act. Nothing needs suppressing — there is nothing loading to suppress — so the machine's skills join the governed road exactly as the project's do: published for review (unchanged, D-186's manifest was always honest about *what exists*), enabled at the reviewed digest under `skills.set` through its own route (`global-skills.changed`), pinned at dispatch, and **composed by Novus into the same plugin directory** from the operator's own config dir. What a turn carried is stated apart from the project's — *Machine skills carried: …* — because for a reader of somebody else's turn, where a skill came from is the fact that matters. The worktree containment rule deliberately does not extend here (that directory is the person's own, outside any repository); every other bound does — real file, non-empty, size-capped, digest-verified, verified bytes written.

**Probed live (2026-08-20).** A composed plugin directory holding a skill sourced from outside the worktree, under the full pinned argv: the model answered with the skill's codeword. ($0.024, one haiku turn.) The composition path does not care where the bytes came from, which is why this slice is small.

**Alternatives.** Redirecting `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` to an empty directory (planned, then unnecessary — nothing loads to suppress; the probe confirmed auth survives the redirect on macOS, so it remains available if the CLI's behaviour ever changes). Leaving the display and its false caption (refused outright). Auto-enabling every machine skill to restore what people assumed was happening (rejected: nobody approved them, and a lane's extensions are a person's choice — D-118's rule, and a fork starting empty is the same rule).

**Consequences.** `enabled_global_skills`; `EnabledGlobalSkillsSchema`; the route; dispatch pinning; `composeSkillsPlugin` gains the machine source and its own carried/dropped report; the rows gain real Enable/Disable and lose "always on"; the trace gains its own carried/dropped lines. D-118's honest-limits clause and D-186's premise are corrected in PROGRESS rather than quietly edited away.

**Revisit when.** The CLI changes whether user-level skills load under pinned settings (the redirect is the ready answer); or machine *commands* deserve the same treatment (today the / menu offers built-ins, which genuinely load, and a user-level command would not — the same correction, one surface over).

## D-192 — A skill says whether it may fire unasked, and a Skill call says which skill

**Context.** The owner, holding two of his own skills: "unslop … runs without me asking for it … how do we know what are the skills which run without me asking versus ones which require me? And … does it show up in our tool calls in the chat tab?"

**Measured (2026-08-20).** Both answers were already in the data and neither was surfaced. A SKILL.md may carry **`disable-model-invocation: true`** — the owner's `thermo-nuclear-code-quality-review` does, his `unslop` does not — which is exactly the difference he was asking about: without it the model reaches for the skill when the description matches; with it, only a person naming it can. And invoking a skill **is a tool call**: probed under the pinned argv, the stream carried `tool_use` named `Skill` with input `{"skill":"novus-project-skills:melon-codes"}`, so it already flowed through Novus's router and into the trace's technical activity.

**Decision.** Publish the frontmatter fact and render the tool call properly. The runner reads `disable-model-invocation` into the manifest as `modelInvocable` (defaulted true, so an older runner's manifest reads as what it was), and the row says **`· on request only`** for the restricted case alone — "the model may use this when it judges it relevant" is what a skill *is*, and captioning every ordinary row with it is the noise D-190 just removed. The stream's tool summary gains `skill` as a detail key, so the trace names *which* skill launched instead of a bare `Skill` step that tells a reader nothing they did not already know.

**Alternatives.** A tag for invocation mode (rejected: D-190 just settled that tags carry provenance and words carry behaviour; this is behaviour). Captioning both cases (rejected above). Inferring the distinction from description phrasing like "Must always apply" (rejected: the CLI has a real field, and guessing from prose is how D-118's false limit happened).

**Consequences.** `ProjectSkill.modelInvocable`; `frontmatterDisablesModelInvocation` in the runner; `detailOf` gains the key; the row gains one conditional phrase. **Not decided here**: whether a `Skill` call should ask a person under `manual` — it is an ordinary tool call today and the router treats it as one, which means a person who already enabled a skill may still be asked when it fires. That is a real question about what an enablement promises, and it deserves its own look rather than a change smuggled into a display slice.

## D-193 — Instructions are carried, not approved. The record says which bytes ran

**Context.** The owner, on the Extensions surface: "the enable button and stuff i dont want it there. this can be a place to just see the skills. let the skills be in the /menu as well to use from directly global and personal." Offered the tradeoff explicitly — approve-before versus record-after — he chose carrying everything.

**Decision.** Every skill a project declares, every slash command it declares, and every skill the operator's own directory holds is **carried into each turn**. There is no enabled set: the runner discovers at spawn and composes what it finds. What replaces the approval is the **record** — `execution.running` now names each carried thing with the SHA-256 of the bytes that actually reached the harness, so "which instructions shaped this turn" is answered by the turn's own account rather than by a prior grant. The `/` menu offers all of it: project commands by their registered invocation, skills by the sentence that starts one. Extensions becomes a place to read: groups named **Repo skills**, **Repo commands**, **Global skills**, each row folding its own description so a dozen skills read as a list of names.

**What did not move, and why.** An **MCP server keeps its gate**. A skill is instructions — it changes what the harness knows, and every act it inspires still reaches the permission router. A server is a program this machine runs and new tools the agent can call: different thing, different risk, and D-119's Mission Admin tier and reviewed digest stand unchanged. The composition bounds also stand: name grammar, worktree containment for repository content, regular file, non-empty, size cap — those decide what is *loadable at all*, and none of them was an approval.

**What this gives up, stated plainly.** D-118's guarantee was that bytes the agent rewrote between review and turn were dropped rather than loaded. That guarantee is gone: a turn that rewrites `.claude/skills` changes what the *next* turn is handed, and the record is what makes it visible rather than a refusal. That is a real reduction in a supervised-autonomy product, taken deliberately by the owner with the trade named. It is recoverable — the manifest, the digests, and the publish-then-pin machinery all still exist — if the record proves insufficient in practice.

**Consequences.** Three routes deleted (`skills`, `commands`, `global-skills`) and their dispatch pinning with them; `enabled_*` columns for those three go unread; `composeSkillsPlugin` takes a worktree and discovers; carried entries become `{name, digest}` through the contract, the runner, and the trace. The control-plane suite loses its enablement tests and keeps its manifest and MCP ones. `skills.set` now governs nothing — left in place because MCP's sibling still reads the same shape, and removing a capability is its own change.

**Amended 2026-08-29, owner-called noise on sight:** "skills showing on every run … idt thats necessary." The stated-carried rows — `Project skills carried`, `Machine skills carried`, `Slash commands carried`, `MCP servers carried`, `Machine MCP servers carried` — leave the stream. This does not thin the record this decision traded approval for: carriage rides the `execution.running` event with each digest, and the drawer's Extensions section reads it back; the stream was a third restatement wearing itself on every turn. What still speaks there: every **drop**, in the warn tone with its reason (a dead grant is news), and `Accounts carried` (D-217) — whose account a turn spends is a security fact, kept visible on purpose. `Claude Code resumed its earlier session` also stops being a sentence: continuity is apparatus, one quiet `· resumed` on the machinery line. Trace tests flipped to assert the silence and the surviving warns; the skills e2e now syncs on turn completion and asserts the carried rows are absent.

**Revisit when.** The record proves insufficient — an agent-rewritten skill actually causes harm and nobody saw it in time — at which point the gate returns for repository content alone, since the machine's own directory never had the threat.

## D-194 — A label is meant to be found

**Context.** The owner on the origin tags shipped hours earlier: "way too dull in color not pop out like yk we use the cursor red and green for code that pops."

**Decision.** The origin tags keep their vocabulary and gain weight: the **bright** terminal hues rather than the base ones, a 22% wash rather than 14%, a 34% inset edge, and 500 weight. The token source is unchanged (D-048's `--term-*` family, no colour invented), and the rule they live under is unchanged (D-190: provenance, never status). A label a person cannot pick out of a list at a glance is decoration, which is what the first version had become.

## D-195 — A collection is what a thing is; a label is what a team calls it

**Context.** The owner, on the origin tags: "i rly like the linear labels and my recommendation would be copying linear interaction model with raindrop's collection/tag distinction. so i can rename the tags to be more customizable."

**Decision.** Raindrop's split, taken literally, because it maps exactly onto what this surface already had. An extension's **collection** is its origin — repository or machine. It is intrinsic, exclusive, uneditable, and it is the group the row sits in with its tag (D-190, unchanged). A **label** is the other half: the organization's own word, many per extension, named and coloured by people, and **organizational only** — no label changes what reaches a turn, which is why they are guarded by `skills.set` rather than a tier of their own and why nothing here is event-recorded. The event log is the record of what happened to the work, not of how somebody filed it.

A label belongs to the **organization**, so a team shares one vocabulary rather than each person inventing their own. Its name is unique per organization, case-insensitively, which is what makes Linear's create-by-typing safe: typing a word that exists selects that label instead of making a twin. An assignment is keyed by the extension's own name **and** its collection **and**, for repository content, its repository — because two projects may each declare a skill called `review` and mean different things, while a machine skill belongs to the person, not the project, and travels with them.

**The interaction is Linear's.** One field that filters and creates; arrow keys and Enter; Escape closes; toggling a label leaves the popover open, because filing is usually several decisions. A label's own maintenance — rename, recolour, remove — happens on the label's row inside the picker rather than in a settings screen elsewhere, so the vocabulary is kept where it is used. Colours name tokens from the terminal family the file badge already draws from (D-048); nothing here invents a value.

**Alternatives.** Per-machine labels in local storage (rejected: this is a multiplayer product, and a taxonomy only one person can see is a private note). Labels on the lane (rejected: a skill outlives a mission). A `labels.set` capability of its own (rejected: labels grant nothing, and `skills.set` — which D-193 left governing nothing — is exactly the "how this lane's extensions are organized" verb this needs). Event-recording label changes (rejected above). Free-form colour (rejected: rule 14).

**Consequences.** `extension_labels` and `extension_label_assignments`; four routes under the mission for uniform authorization; the label vocabulary and this mission's assignments served on the mission detail; a `LabelPicker` and chips on every extension row. Because a label change records no event, it does not travel the live stream — the section re-reads the mission itself and hands the room the fresh detail.

**Revisit when.** Labels want to filter the list rather than only decorate it; or an extension outside this section wants them, at which point "extension" stops being the right noun and the table needs a subject column.

## D-204 — A key you can change, on the row that names it

**Context.** D-177 recorded the Keyboard page's growth outright: "a rebinding UI (the Codex pencil-and-trash anatomy)". The owner asked for it by that line.

**Decision.** Every binding that is one ⌘ chord — palette, new mission, rail, dock, panel, find, settings — becomes editable, and only those. The ⌘1–9 range, the room's modal letters (G sequences, R), and Esc stay fixed: a range is not a chord, a bare letter is modal to the transcript, and Esc is the platform's word. The chords live in one renderer registry (`keybindings.ts`) — defaults are the shipped keys, overrides in this machine's localStorage like the theme — and the shell's global handler, the room's find chord, the palette's hints, and the settings page all read the same live snapshot, so a rebound key is honored and *said* everywhere at once, never listed as one thing and bound as another.

**The anatomy is Codex's.** Each chord row: the keycap chip, a pencil that turns the chip into "Press the new keys… Esc cancels" and takes the next keydown at capture phase (so the chord being replaced cannot also fire), and a trash that appears only once a key differs from shipped and returns exactly that. A chord is refused in words rather than taken blindly: no ⌘ at all (it would eat plain typing), a reserved bare-⌘ key (`Q W C V X A Z`, digits, Enter, `.` — the system's and the room's own), or a collision, named with what the chord already means.

**Alternatives.** Rebinding everything including modal letters (rejected: R-in-a-composer needs context the registry cannot see; a wrong scope there breaks typing). A main-process settings file (rejected: bindings are per-person presentation, the theme's own storage tier). Unbind-to-nothing on trash (rejected: a row with no key is a feature turned off by furniture; trash means "back to shipped").

**Consequences.** `keybindings.ts` (registry, `useSyncExternalStore` hook, chord parse/label/refusal); project-shell and project-room handlers match against the registry; palette hints read it; the settings Keyboard page splits Chords from Fixed keys and carries the recording row; `settings-key-*` styles.

**Revisit when.** A second surface wants a modal key rebound, or two people share a machine account and want profiles — either is the registry growing a scope, not a new mechanism.

## D-197 — A file wears its language's own mark

**Context.** Owner, on the extension badges shipped hours earlier and iterated three times: "u see conductor how nice it looks our looks shit … its j bad thats what i wanna fix a better design of those badges/chips here and when i do @." Measurement had already shown the badge tuning was chasing the wrong quantity — letter-to-chip contrast was ~8:1 throughout and one "brighter" step made it *worse* (6.47:1) — while the reference the owner kept pasting was never a better-tuned letter chip at all: it was Python's own logo, git's diamond, the markdown mark.

**Decision.** The file mark is **the language's own icon** where the sanctioned brand-icon set has one (D-029, `simple-icons`, already a dependency), drawn at 15px in the brand's own colour with no chip, no fill, and nothing framed around it. A brand whose own colour is black (Markdown, JSON, Rust) wears `--text-2` instead, because black is unreadable in a dark room and muddy in a light one. A type no brand owns keeps a plain stroke glyph carrying its family's terminal hue — page, braces, gear, picture, angle brackets, globe — so no row shows an empty slot. This retires D-048's two-to-four-letter badge: that badge existed to avoid a glyph set's maintenance and licence burden, and a curated map onto an icon set already vendored here carries neither. The same component serves all three surfaces — the All files panel, the @-mention popover (D-185), and the pinned file chip (D-182) — so a file looks like itself wherever it is offered.

**Alternatives.** Tuning the wash further (rejected on measurement: it moves a quantity that was never the problem). A filled chip at full hue (tried and shown to the owner: contrast 8.8–12.1:1, and still refused — a row of filled blocks reads as a list of labels, not a list of files). Drawing an in-house glyph per language (rejected: exactly the burden D-048 named, with none of the recognition a person already has).

**Consequences.** Brand hexes enter component source, which the repository gate forbids as literals — they are read from the icon set at runtime (`icon.hex`), never written down, and the no-local-values rule stands: a language's colour is a fact about the language, the same standing the terminal palette has under D-046. DESIGN.md's All files bullet is restated from "tinted badge" to the mark. Proven in a real window: `runtime.spec.ts` 19/19 with two typed files added to the fixture so the coloured branch is exercised rather than assumed (evidence `57`, reviewed — TypeScript blue, the Python logo, the Markdown mark, and the cyan page for `.txt`); `sessions.spec.ts` 5/5 for the popover and chip (evidence `206`/`208`).

**Revisit when.** A type a person meets often has no brand and no fitting family glyph; or a brand mark reads badly in the light theme in practice rather than in theory; or the icon set's licence terms change.

## D-196 — A machine's own servers take two keys, and a secret's whole journey is one disk

**Context.** The owner hit the gap directly: his terminal shows six MCP servers, a Novus turn shows one (Novus's own capture endpoint), and there was no road between. His framing set the line: "if i have these things right i can still use them … he cant use it obv if he doesnt have it … this is a tricky line with others cause privacy."

**Measured first.** The six are **claude.ai account connectors** — they live in no local file; the CLI injects them from its signed-in account state, and `--strict-mcp-config` excludes them. A thing the runner cannot read is a thing nobody can review, so they are stated as uncarryable rather than half-trusted. What *is* readable is the CLI's user-level config (`~/.claude.json` `mcpServers`, or `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/.claude.json`) — what `claude mcp add -s user` writes, and what a teammate's real local servers will be.

**Decision.** Machine servers ride D-186's road at D-119's tier, with one more key. The runner publishes each reviewable user-level server as a **redacted summary**: the command line with secretish flag values masked, the url, and env variable **names** — never values — while the digest is computed over the full local declaration. Enabling takes **both facts on one caller**: `mcp.set` (new tool surface for the mission) **and** being the machine's owner, checked against the runner row (a Mission Admin cannot switch on somebody else's Gmail; an owner without the tier cannot widen the mission alone). The enable confirms its consequence in a sentence recorded verbatim on `machine-mcp.changed` (the D-100 pattern): while it is on, anyone directing the lane can ask it to act. At spawn the runner re-reads its own file, digest-verifies, and merges the full entries — values and all — into the same strict config beside the project's servers, written on the owner's machine and removed with the turn: **a secret's whole journey is file-to-file on one disk** (D-041). A name collision with a project server drops the machine one with the reason stated; the router already treats every `mcp__*` tool as a human question under every profile short of Don't ask (D-119, unchanged).

**Alternatives.** Loading the user config wholesale (rejected: D-186's lesson — one person's laptop silently shaping shared turns, now with credentials). Owner-only enablement without the tier (rejected: a Contributor owner could widen the whole mission's tool surface). A two-person offer/accept flow when owner and admin differ (deferred: the refusal names whose machine it is, and the handoff-offer grammar exists when this earns building). Carrying account connectors by relaxing strictness (rejected outright: strict config is the guarantee).

**Consequences.** `MachineMcpServerSchema` (env names only, laxer name grammar, same url admission), workspace/declared/workstream/running-event fields, `declared_machine_mcp`/`enabled_machine_mcp`, the two-key route, `machine-mcp.ts` (discover-redacted / resolve-full), compose merge, the **Global MCP servers** group with the consequence dialog, and the trace's own `Machine MCP servers carried:` line. The panel states the account-connector limit in words.

**Revisit when.** The CLI grows a way to name account connectors in a config it accepts under strictness; or owner-and-admin diverging in practice earns the offer/accept flow; or per-server standing allows (D-119's deferred dial) arrive and should cover machine servers the same way.

## D-198 — A machine's own servers take two keys, and a secret's whole journey is one disk

**Context.** The owner hit the gap directly: his terminal shows six MCP servers, a Novus turn shows one (Novus's own capture endpoint), and there was no road between. His framing set the line: "if i have these things right i can still use them … he cant use it obv if he doesnt have it … this is a tricky line with others cause privacy." (Renumbered from a second D-196 collision the same evening — the keybindings decision's claim is baked into ab66e3d's immutable message, so this one moved, exactly as D-197 had to.)

**Measured first.** The six are **claude.ai account connectors** — they live in no local file; the CLI injects them from its signed-in account state, and `--strict-mcp-config` excludes them. A thing the runner cannot read is a thing nobody can review, so they are stated as uncarryable rather than half-trusted. What *is* readable is the CLI's user-level config (`~/.claude.json` `mcpServers`, or `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/.claude.json`) — what `claude mcp add -s user` writes, and what a teammate's real local servers will be.

**Decision.** Machine servers ride D-186's road at D-119's tier, with one more key. The runner publishes each reviewable user-level server as a **redacted summary**: the command line with secretish flag values masked, the url, and env variable **names** — never values — while the digest is computed over the full local declaration. Enabling takes **both facts on one caller**: `mcp.set` (new tool surface for the mission) **and** being the machine's owner, checked against the runner row (a Mission Admin cannot switch on somebody else's Gmail; an owner without the tier cannot widen the mission alone). The enable confirms its consequence in a sentence recorded verbatim on `machine-mcp.changed` (the D-100 pattern): while it is on, anyone directing the lane can ask it to act. At spawn the runner re-reads its own file, digest-verifies, and merges the full entries — values and all — into the same strict config beside the project's, written on the owner's machine and removed with the turn: **a secret's whole journey is file-to-file on one disk** (D-041). A name collision with a project server drops the machine one with the reason stated; the router already treats every `mcp__*` tool as a human question under every profile short of Don't ask (D-119, unchanged).

**Alternatives.** Loading the user config wholesale (rejected: D-186's lesson — one person's laptop silently shaping shared turns, now with credentials). Owner-only enablement without the tier (rejected: a Contributor owner could widen the whole mission's tool surface). A two-person offer/accept flow when owner and admin differ (deferred: the refusal names whose machine it is, and the handoff-offer grammar exists when this earns building). Carrying account connectors by relaxing strictness (rejected outright: strict config is the guarantee).

**Consequences.** `MachineMcpServerSchema` (env names only, laxer name grammar, same url admission), workspace/declared/workstream/running-event fields, `declared_machine_mcp`/`enabled_machine_mcp`, the two-key route, `machine-mcp.ts` (discover-redacted / resolve-full), compose merge, the **Global MCP servers** group with the consequence dialog, and the trace's own `Machine MCP servers carried:` line. The panel states the account-connector limit in words.

**Revisit when.** The CLI grows a way to name account connectors in a config it accepts under strictness; or owner-and-admin diverging in practice earns the offer/accept flow; or per-server standing allows (D-119's deferred dial) arrive and should cover machine servers the same way.

## D-199 — A terminal command routes to the person's own session, primed and never fired

**Context.** The owner, on Conductor's embedded terminal answering `/mcp` with the full interactive dialog: "is that a possibility … the /menu will open into the terminal we alr built im assuming." The assumption was right: the dock (D-049) is a real PTY in the lane's worktree, and running `claude` there is already the whole Conductor experience — personal settings, account connectors, everything the pinned turn deliberately excludes.

**Decision.** The composer's / popover gains one last row: `claude "/{query}"` · *Open in terminal — your own session*. Picking it consumes the /query (a routed command is not a message), raises the dock the shell owns, and **types** the command into the dock's own shell — primed, never submitted: the person presses Enter in their own session or does not, so Novus never fires a personal session on anyone's behalf. The row exists for every query, so a terminal-only surface like /mcp is never a dead end, and it is offered only where a dock can exist (the machine hosting the workspace). What happens in the dock stays what it always was (D-049): the person's own act, outside the turn record, its worktree leftovers caught by the settle machinery like any human edit.

The prime waits for the shell to speak before typing — bytes fired into a still-initialising line editor are eaten (measured: zsh under bracketed-paste swallowed an early write whole) — and it types into the dock's *own* shell rather than racing its mount effect with a second one (also measured, as a duplicate session).

**Alternatives.** Executing the primed command (rejected: firing a person's own credentialed session is theirs to do, and the unfired prompt is also what makes the affordance testable without spawning real sessions). Resuming the governed harness session interactively, Conductor's shape (rejected: untracked turns inside a recorded conversation would make the receipt lie). Listing the CLI's terminal-only commands as named rows (rejected: D-188's anti-drift rule — the generic row covers every name without curating any).

**Consequences.** `terminal` prop on the composer; `onOpenTerminal` from the shell through the room; `prime`/`onPrimed` on the dock with the readiness wait; the tab carries its session id for the test to read scrollback through the bridge.

**Revisit when.** The dock wants a "claude" tab kind of its own; or people ask for the primed command to auto-fire, at which point the consent question returns as itself.

**Amended the same evening, owner-driven ("I want all my connectors there as well"), with the bridge probed live.** The revisit clause's question is now measured, against installed `claude 2.1.237`. A provider's **own** remote MCP endpoint added at user scope IS the road: `claude mcp add --transport http -s user notion https://mcp.notion.com/mcp`, one interactive `claude mcp login notion` (browser OAuth, refused off-TTY), and the stored token is **reused headless under the full pinned argv** — `connected`, 28 tools, inside `--strict-mcp-config`. Personal Notion therefore reaches governed turns through this decision's machinery unchanged. The account connectors' own endpoints turned out to be real, named URLs (`gmailmcp.googleapis.com/mcp/v1` and siblings, surfaced by `claude mcp list`) — but Google's authorization server **refuses dynamic client registration** ("Incompatible auth server"), so only claude.ai's pre-registered client may authenticate: Google-class connectors stay terminal-only (D-199) until Google opens client registration or Anthropic exposes brokered credentials. The wall is a vendor's client policy, located exactly; the pattern for every provider that speaks standard MCP OAuth is proven.

## D-205 — The fan-out night: workers by their new name, Stop that never blinks, checks a `cat` can't mint

**Context.** Owner-run WRK-01 probe, live, 2026-08-21 02:30–02:39: a five-subagent survey turn (`exe_218xq0…`, 289 tool events, six approval cards). Three failures surfaced at once, each verified afterward against the run's own event log rather than from the screenshot.

**Decision.** Three fixes, one slice, because they are one story — the product misreading a modern fan-out turn.

1. **Workers by their new name.** The run's five subagents were real (five spawn calls, 279 parented child events) and rendered as no workers at all: the installed CLI names its spawn tool `Agent`, and both ends of D-107 matched only `Task`. The stream parser's end-detection and the trace builder's spawn-detection now match the pair — `Task` for older CLIs, `Agent` for current ones. Nothing else about D-107 moves: one level of nesting, purpose and end only as stated.

2. **Stop that never blinks.** The event log holds not one `execution.stop_requested` — the owner's presses never became requests, because Stop lived only in `agent_running`. Six approval cards meant the state line flipped to *Needs approval* (no action) again and again, a button vanishing mid-aim; `agent_starting` had no Stop either. Both now offer it. The server was always ready — every non-terminal execution state is stoppable, and a stop settles pending approvals in the same transaction — the interface just withheld the control.

3. **Checks a `cat` can't mint.** The survey's "3 checks passed" were `cat eslint.config.mjs` (recorded as lint), a grep whose pattern contained `"build":` (recorded as build), and a grep for `eslint-disable` (lint again). `classifyCommand` matched checker names anywhere in the command text. It now judges each command segment by the program actually invoked — env assignments and runners stepped over, package-manager script position honored — and an argument, filename, or grep pattern decides nothing. The wild commands are tests verbatim.

**Alternatives.** Treating any tool whose children carry its id as a spawn (rejected: guessing identity is what D-107 refused; the name pair is what the CLI actually ships). A global Stop divorced from the state line (deferred: one control in one place is the room's grammar; it now simply persists across the states a live turn passes through). Keeping worker shell commands as mission evidence but labeled (out of scope here: the classifier fix removes the false positives; the D-107 known gap — a worker's *real* check reading as the turn's — stands recorded).

**Consequences.** `harness-stream.ts` (classifier rewrite, spawn-name pair), `derive-feed.ts` (spawn-name pair), `derive.ts` (Stop in `needs_approval` and `agent_starting`); tests carry the wild commands and the Agent-named worker end-to-end; DESIGN's *Needs approval* row states the standing Stop.

**Revisit when.** The CLI renames the spawn tool again (the pair becomes a set); or steering grows a real "stop" verb distinct from a queued direction — the owner typed the word *stop* mid-turn and the product correctly, uselessly, steered it into the survey.

## D-206 — The stop square lives where the eye already is

**Context.** Owner, the morning after D-205, holding up Codex's composer: the circled stop square "is quite easy to understand — we don't have that." Novus's only Stop was a word on the state line, a control the eye is not on while typing.

**Decision.** The composer's send control doubles as the turn's stop control, the pattern every agent product has taught: while the conversation on screen has a live turn and the box is empty, the accent circle carries a filled square and stops that turn; the moment there is text, it is the send arrow again, because a message typed mid-turn queues and steers (D-054's boundary rule) and must stay sendable. Scope is the conversation on screen only — its read-alongside turn by session, else the lane's write turn when this conversation is the one it runs for. Another chat's turn never gets a square here: that case already has the alongside offer, and a square that killed a different conversation would be a misfire. The state line's Stop stays; two positions, one verb, same server path.

**Alternatives.** A separate stop button beside send (rejected: two circles at the composer's edge is a toolbar; the swap is the taught pattern). Replacing send entirely while running (rejected: it would amputate queue-and-steer, which is half the point of a running composer). Stopping whichever turn is live regardless of session (rejected on D-095's grounds — the square must never reach across conversations).

**Consequences.** `composer.tsx` takes `onStop` and swaps the glyph; the room computes the on-screen turn (`composerStop`) and wires it through the same `missions.stop` the state line uses; DESIGN's Composer bullet states the swap. No new styles — the existing send circle, a `currentColor` square at 15 of 24 viewBox units, sized against Codex's on the owner's eye after 9 and 12 both read as a dot. The mid-turn stop spec grew the swap's assertions and the magnified evidence shot (`216-`); magnification is a temporary transform on the real button, because window zoom relayouts the room into a third of its width and captures the composer off-frame — measured during this slice, and worth knowing before the next glyph is photographed.

**Revisit when.** Mid-turn steering grows a real cancel-current-step verb, at which point the square may deserve a long-press or menu; or sessions gain per-worker stops, which belong on the worker rows, not here.

## D-200 — A worker outlives the block it was spawned in

**Context.** Owner-reported, watching a fan-out: "the subagents which spawn — when I click inside of it, the stuff in there isn't updating; I see in the main that it is modifying and getting more stuff in it." A live-watch e2e written to reproduce it passed — the open inspector does grow, promptly, on the room's live signal — which located the fault elsewhere: not in the refresh, in the join.

**Decision.** A worker is looked up by its spawn id across the whole feed, not inside the trace block that happens to be open. The feed groups a turn's activity by the direction that caused it, so **a direction applied mid-turn opens a new block** — steering a running turn does exactly this — while the subagent already working goes on reporting under the same spawn id. Looked up per block, everything after that seam missed the worker and fell through to the block's own anonymous grouped activity: the open worker view froze mid-run, its end never landed, and the room around it went on plainly moving. Same rule for the end as for the steps, since a Task result arriving after a steer is the ordinary case, not the exotic one.

Nothing about D-107's honesty changes: activity whose parent joins to no recorded spawn is still grouped activity claiming nothing, an end without a recorded start still renders nothing, and a worker still appears only in the block it was spawned in.

**Alternatives.** Re-parenting the worker into the newest block (rejected: the worker belongs to the turn that spawned it, and a row that migrates as you steer is a worse lie than a frozen one). Not splitting blocks on a mid-turn direction (rejected: the split is the trace's grammar — one direction, one thread). Refreshing harder (rejected: measured first; the refresh was never the problem).

**Consequences.** `derive-feed.ts` keeps a `workersById` map beside the blocks and routes all four joins through it; `trace-presentation.test.ts` carries the seam case, which fails against the previous code; `e2e/worker-live.spec.ts` is new — it opens a worker *between* its own steps under a paced harness and asserts the view grows within a window tighter than the room's 30s fallback read, so "updates eventually" cannot pass for "updates live".

**Revisit when.** Workers nest more than one level, at which point the map needs a parent chain rather than a flat id.

## D-201 — What a mission is born with: its files and its answer policy

**Context.** The owner, on the ask dialog: "when I do + here, why is there no permission showing up here or ability to add images." Both were absent by construction — an attachment uploads against a mission, and a profile is set on a lane, and the ask dialog is the thing that *creates* both. D-077's Create row was recorded as model, effort, and send alone.

**Decision.** Both join the ask, and both are applied the moment their target exists, before the first turn is dispatched.

**Attaching** becomes a two-phase act. The composer's `attach.upload` is now optional: where it is absent there is nothing to upload *to*, so the composer holds the chosen paths and hands them to `onSubmit` as `attachmentPaths` — named intentions, exactly as a pending transcript already was. The ask dialog creates the mission, uploads each held path against it, and passes the resulting ids into the first direction. A file that cannot be prepared is stated rather than swallowed, and it never costs the person their words: the turn goes with whatever did upload. Clipboard paste stays out — its bytes have no path to hold, and inventing a temporary file to manufacture one is a later slice, not a thing to fake here.

**The answer policy** is chosen in the ask and applied to the lane the instant it exists, *before* the direction — because a profile is pinned at dispatch (D-115), so applying it after would leave the first turn running under a policy nobody chose. The creator is the mission's Mission Admin, so every profile is theirs, including the one whose warning must be acknowledged; the chip's own confirm dialog collects it and the acknowledgement travels verbatim as always. A refused profile stops the turn rather than starting it quietly under the default: the mission exists, the person lands in the room, and the reason is on screen.

**Alternatives.** A staging area on the server so files could upload before a mission (rejected: new surface for a case the two-phase hold already answers). Applying the profile after the first direction (rejected: pinned at dispatch means the first turn would miss it). Creating the mission on the first attach (rejected: picking a file is not deciding to start work).

**Consequences.** `composer.tsx`: `upload` and `paste` optional, held paths rendered as chips beside the uploaded ones, `attachmentPaths` on submit, both kinds counted against the same bound. `project-shell.tsx`: the ask holds a profile, wires attach, and orders creation → profile → uploads → direction. One CSS fix rides along: `.dialog-body` scrolls, and a scroll box clipped the chip menus opening upward out of the composer's foot — the policy menu's rows landed outside the dialog on the scrim, unclickable — so `.ask-body` opts out of that clipping. Found by the spec, not by eye. `e2e/ask-dialog.spec.ts` proves the profile end to end (chosen in the ask, read back off the lane) and that both controls are present; evidence `218-`.

**Revisit when.** Clipboard paste in the ask earns its temporary file; or a second surface needs deferred attachments, at which point the hold belongs in a shared hook rather than in the composer's own state.

## D-202 — A background agent ends when the CLI says so, and the pipe stays open until then

**Context.** Owner, inside a worker view: "it starts and ends in the same minute… but back in main it's not done yet." The view showed *done · started 19:15 · ended 19:15*, and its "Report" read *Async agent launched successfully. (This tool result is internal metadata — never quote or paste any part of it…)*. All eleven workers of the live run had that exact end in the event log. Also: "ctrl-f search doesn't work inside these subagents."

**What the CLI actually does.** Captured from `claude 2.1.239` with the runner's own flags, twice. A spawn the CLI takes into the background announces `system/task_started` (with the spawn's `tool_use_id` and `is_backgrounded: true`), answers the spawn call *immediately* with a tool_result whose top-level `tool_use_result.status` is `async_launched` — the launch receipt — and states the real end later as `system/task_notification`: the same `tool_use_id`, a `status`, the agent's `summary`, and its own `usage` (tokens, tool uses, duration). `background_tasks_changed` lists what is still pending. Between receipt and notification the *model ends its turn to wait*, which is a `result` line; the CLI then wakes it with the notification, emits a fresh `system/init`, and runs a follow-up turn on the same process.

**Decision.**

1. **The receipt is not the end.** The parser remembers backgrounded spawns from `task_started` (and from the receipt's own `async_launched` status), says nothing on the receipt, and records `harness.worker.ended` from `task_notification` — `failed` from the status, the report from the summary, and the usage the CLI stated, which the contract now carries as an optional field on the ended event. D-107's per-worker-cost gap closes for background workers the only honest way: the vendor started stating it. A synchronous spawn's end is unchanged and states `usage: null`.

2. **stdin waits for the last result, not the first.** The runner closed the CLI's stdin on the first `result` line, which with background agents is the *waiting* result. Measured: the CLI does not die on that close — it drains pending tasks and runs the follow-up — but the follow-up turn can no longer answer a permission prompt, because the pipe the answer travels on is gone. Stdin now closes on a result only when the CLI lists no pending background task. A stub that exits on stdin-close makes the hazard observable: against the old rule the follow-up turn and the worker's real end never arrive.

3. **Find walks whichever canvas is showing.** ⌘F collected its ranges from the feed's scroller, and the worker view replaces the feed. The inspector's scroller has its own ref; find walks the one on screen.

**Alternatives.** Reading the end from `task_updated` (rejected: it carries the status but not the summary or the tool_use_id; the notification is the complete statement). Ending the worker on the receipt and *re-opening* it on the notification (rejected: a state that goes done → working is a lie told twice). Keeping stdin open unconditionally and killing on the final result (rejected: D-109 reads the final verdict from the process's own exit; the close is what lets it exit).

**Consequences.** `harness-stream.ts` (task lifecycle, `hasPendingBackgroundTasks`, receipt suppression), `execution.ts` (the close rule), contracts (`usage` on `harness.worker.ended`), `derive-feed.ts` / `worker-inspector.tsx` (stated usage on the facts line), `project-room.tsx` (find root). Tests: the captured lines verbatim in `harness-stream.test.ts`, the stdin hazard through the real spawn path in `execution-endings.test.ts` (fails against the old rule), the stated usage in `trace-presentation.test.ts`.

**Revisit when.** The CLI nests background agents (spawn_depth > 1 arrived in the capture — one level is still what the join records); or `task_progress` earns a place as a worker's live status line, which the forwarded child events already say better while forwarding is on.

## D-203 — The turn as it happened: activity where the words are

**Context.** Owner, reading a real fan-out turn: the harness's interim narration (*"Let me verify the contrast math"*, *"Seven agents running. While they work…"*) arrived as one unbroken wall, and the actions each sentence referred to sat hundreds of steps away in a single *Technical activity · 454 steps* box at the end. "In how systems work those are all parts, and there are other stuff happening between them captured in technical activity." Inside a worker, the inverse: everything inline, twenty-one consecutive *Read ARCHITECTURE.md* rows hiding the steps around them.

**Decision.** The trace renders the turn in stream order — one stream of speech, the activity between speech, and the workers where they were spawned — reversing D-108's one end-of-turn disclosure. Speech is fused only when it was consecutive on the wire; the moment an action intervenes, the wall becomes the separate moments it actually was. Each run of activity is one quiet line on the harness body's own column naming what the run was (*Read ×21 · Bash ×3*), collapsed, expanding to its steps. The harness's identity is said once per turn: speech that resumes after a run is the same speaker continuing, and a mark every few lines is the chat-app clutter signature element 2 refuses. Spawns sit where they were made; consecutive spawns share one row group. Inside a worker, consecutive identical steps fold into one row with a count — only identical ones, because a different file is a different fact.

What does not change: the flat list of steps is still kept, for counting and for tests; the grouping rules of D-065 still hold for consecutive speech; nothing is rendered as a lone centred fragment; a worker's own steps still join the worker, never the turn.

One honest limit, stated to the owner: the *duplicated content* — interim findings and a final report that restates them — is the model's own writing. The interface can put the actions back between the words; it cannot dedupe the words.

**A stray found by the move.** `workspace.declared` — the runner publishing the project's command manifest — had no handler and fell to "unknown kind is technical activity". Arriving just after a direction is submitted, it led every turn's stream as a run called *declared*. Hidden at the end of the turn before, visible at its head now. It is the machine's bookkeeping, read on Overview, and joins the workspace-runtime kinds the trace skips, with `pushed` and `released` beside it.

**Alternatives.** One disclosure per gap between messages (rejected by D-108 at the time as clutter; what is built is one *line* per gap, not one box, and a line that names its run reads as apparatus rather than chrome). Rendering every step inline unfolded (rejected: 897 tool lines in the owner's run; the terminal collapses them too). Re-showing the identity on every resumed paragraph (rejected: the speaker did not change).

**Consequences.** `derive-feed.ts`: `activity` and `workers` segments placed in the stream by `step()` and `spawnRow()`, the skipped kinds widened. `direction-trace.tsx`: `ActivityRun` with a grouped summary, `continued` speech without the identity, workers resolved inline, the end-of-turn disclosure removed. `worker-inspector.tsx`: `collapseRepeats`. DESIGN's signature element 2 and the Technical-activity bullet rewritten. Tests: stream order and spawn placement in `trace-presentation.test.ts`; `slice.spec.ts` expands the run that holds the Write rather than a single end box; `workers.spec.ts` asserts the exact DOM order `msg-agent · technical-activity · worker-rollup · msg-agent` in a real window (evidence `219-`).

**Revisit when.** A run wants its own timestamp or duration on the line; or a turn's checkpoints and checks want to join the stream at their own positions rather than the milestone rows beneath it.

## D-207 — A merge fulfils a decision, not the mission: a lane publishes as many times as its work earns

**Context.** The owner merged PR #1 from inside Novus, kept working in the same mission, asked the agent to "push then MR this to main" — and found nothing. D-099/D-100 had modeled a mission as a feature branch: one decision, one request, merge = shipped = over. The rail still read "Publish · decision recorded" and "PR #1 · merged", the state line still said how publication ended, and the agent, with no second publish to reach for, opened PR #2 on GitHub by hand with `gh` — a request Novus never saw. The owner's verdict on the model: "feel like that's dumb that after one it's almost over — when did that make sense?" It made sense for a feature branch. His missions are rooms: a topic a person returns to, with the transcript, the context and the agent's session memory that leaving for a new mission would throw away.

**Decision.** A decision is about one revision, and its merge **fulfils** it rather than ending anything. Once a checkpoint on the decision's lane postdates the merge, that decision is the mission's history: the mission's state returns to the work's own, the prepared request is gone, the Compare columns offer choosing again, and the next decision — recorded beside the old one, which it supersedes on record — publishes as a new request **on the same branch**. GitHub diffs a request from its merge base, so PR #2 shows exactly the commits since PR #1 merged; a fresh branch per publish would have moved the lane's worktree off the branch it lives on for no cleaner diff. A merged decision with nothing after it still stands, and the room keeps saying how publication ended. The rail lists every request the mission ever opened, oldest first, each its own closable tab; the one-open-per-lane rule is unchanged, so a second request can never race the first.

**Alternatives.** A new mission per follow-up (the prior model; rejected by the owner for the reason above — the room is the unit, branches are what it produces). A fresh branch cut from main per publish (rejected: the lane *is* its branch — worktree, runner, remote-head guarantee all key on it — and the merge base already keeps the diff clean). Reopening or amending the merged request (rejected: GitHub will not, and a merged receipt must stay the receipt that travelled).

**Consequences.** `MissionDetailResponse.pullRequests` (all, oldest first) beside the existing `pullRequest` (the standing decision's own, else the lane's open-or-latest); the projection's `current` decision excludes a fulfilled one that a later checkpoint outran, and that single rule is mirrored in the desktop's `standingDecision` so the columns, the receipt and the state agree. The room holds one tab per request (`::pull:<id>` keys), the rail one row per request, the request page is told which request it is about, and the chosen column says `Chosen — PR #n · state` instead of "not published yet" forever. Proven: `pulls.test.ts` +1 — merge, second checkpoint, state back to the work's, prepared request null, second decision supersedes the first, the stale push refused, PR #2 opened on the same branch, `["merged","draft"]` listed; `pr.spec.ts` extended through a real window — the fake host merges PR #1, a second turn checkpoints, the column offers the choice again, push, PR #2 opens, both rows and both tabs render (evidence `215`), and PR #2 merges before completion's tail. Control-plane 333/333, desktop unit 570/570.

**Revisit when.** A follow-up wants a *different* base than the last merge (a release branch) — that is a base-ref choice on the next decision, not a new mechanism; or the host reports a merged request reopened, which the ingest should treat as a new open request rather than a resurrection.

## D-208 — A request opened outside Novus is adopted, not ignored

**Context.** D-207 let a lane publish again after a merge — and the owner's next message showed the case it still missed. Before D-207 existed, the agent had already opened PR #2 on GitHub by hand with `gh`, and the owner had merged it there. Novus, which tracked only requests it opened itself, showed the mission at "Ready for review" offering Publish for work that was already on main; going through with it would have ended at the host's "no commits between" refusal. PRODUCT.md had promised pull requests "created or adopted by Novus" since D-099; adoption had never been built.

**Decision.** The publication sweep adopts. For every pushed GitHub lane of an open mission it asks the host which requests exist for the lane's branch and inserts any it does not hold — in whatever state the host holds them, merged included — with no decision behind them, no Novus creator, the host's author, and the moment of adoption; recorded as a `pr.adopted` event from the external actor; idempotent by host number per mission. The one-open-per-lane rule stands: an open host request is left alone while Novus holds its own open one. An adopted request takes the same rail row, tab and page as any other, and the page says in words that it was opened outside Novus and by whom; its description is the host's, not a receipt that travelled. When the decided revision already merged through an adopted request, Novus answers `already_merged` by name — which request, which base — instead of forwarding a request the host would refuse, and the publish panel says the same and offers nothing to open.

**Alternatives.** Synthesizing a decision for an adopted request (rejected: a rationale nobody wrote is the fabrication D-075 forbids; the decision column is simply empty). Adopting only open requests (rejected: the merged one is exactly what the owner's case needed, because it is what makes the revision already shipped). Blocking outside opens (impossible, and wrong — the host is the source of truth and the agent was not misbehaving).

**Consequences.** `pull_requests.dec_id` and `created_by` become nullable for adopted rows only; `adopted_at`, `host_author`, and a unique `(mission_id, provider_number)` arrive. Contracts: `PullRequest.decisionId` nullable, `createdBy`/`createdByLogin` nullable, `adopted`, `authorLogin`. `RepositoryProvider.listPullRequestsForHead` on all three providers (the App adapter lists `pulls?head=owner:branch&state=all`; the fake gains `fakeExternalPull` and `fakeBranchTip` for tests). `PR_SELECT` left-joins users — the inner join was silently dropping every creator-less row, which the new test caught first. Proven: `pulls.test.ts` +1 — an outside request adopted with author, state and event; a second sweep adopts nothing twice; the host's merge followed; the decided revision refused as `already_merged` naming the request — control-plane 334/334; desktop unit 570/570; `pr.spec.ts` and `decision.spec.ts` green.

**Revisit when.** A lane's branch carries requests against a different base than the lane's own (a release branch) — adoption records the base as the host states it, and a later decision may want to choose it; or the host reports a merged request reopened, which should arrive as a new open request rather than a resurrection.

## D-209 — The work is followed onward: a request from the branch it merged into is the mission's review

**Context.** The owner's own project: the mission was based on `ppo_branch`, published and merged there (PR #3), and then `ppo_branch` went to `main` on a request a PhD-student reviewer commented on. Novus tracked only requests it opened — D-208 had just widened that to requests opened by hand on the lane's own branch — so the reviewer's comments on the mission's work were invisible in the room. The owner, on "track this PR" as a control: "I have to keep in mind what all I did which needs tracking rather than it automatically showing up." A control that only works if a person remembers is the stop button's failure again.

**Decision.** Adoption follows the work, automatically, by lineage. A request whose head is the branch one of the mission's requests *merged into* carries that mission's commits onward, and the review it draws is review of the mission's work — so the sweep adopts it as **downstream** of the request it carries, open or merged, whoever opened it. One hop per pass; a hop's own merge is the next pass's source; the chain ends where the work reaches the repository's default branch, because everything off the default branch thereafter would *contain* the work without being *about* it. The webhook runs both adoption passes before dropping an unknown request, so the reviewer's first comment arrives on the host's event rather than the sweep's timer.

A downstream request is never the lane's own publication. It blocks no Publish and becomes no decision's receipt (the lane's own-request lookup excludes it), and it is completed — readied, merged, closed, its branch deleted — where it was opened: the stewarding routes refuse every completion verb on it with code `downstream`, server-side, while comment and resolve-thread pass, because answering the reviewer from the room is the point. The mission's state line stays its own request's; Home's open-request column and the close gate count a downstream request as open, which is true — the work is not shipped while its review is.

**Alternatives.** "Track this PR" as a control (rejected above: bookkeeping the person must remember). Matching by commit containment rather than by branch (deferred: exact but costs a commits listing per request per pass; the base-is-head rule is exact for every merge the product itself performs and for the owner's case, and containment can join it when a case needs it). Making the mission state `pull_request_open` for a downstream request (rejected for now: that sentence's verbs — Mark ready, Merge — are exactly the ones refused here; a state that offers nothing is a worse sentence than *Decision recorded — merged as PR #3*). Following past the default branch (rejected: a release cut off `main` is not this mission's review).

**Consequences.** `pull_requests.downstream_of`; `PullRequest.downstreamOf` on the contract; `adoptDownstreamOnce` beside D-208's pass, sharing one `insertAdopted`; `pr.adopted` gains `baseRef` and `downstreamOf`; `PullContext.downstream` and the `reviewOnly` gate; the webhook's adopt-then-retry; the rail row and page head wear the lineage, Completion says where it happens. `pulls.test.ts` carries the owner's case end to end — born on `ppo_branch`, merged there, the reviewer's `ppo_branch → main` adopted with their comment, merge refused from the room while a comment passes, the chain stopping at `main` — failing against the previous code.

**Revisit when.** Commit containment earns its cost (a request carrying the work under a different head branch); or the mission's state wants to say *Under review downstream* as its own sentence, at which point it needs verbs of its own rather than borrowed ones.

## D-210 — The request page takes the host's anatomy

**Context.** On sight of PR #1 and PR #2 beside GitHub's own page for the same request, the owner: "these are not what PR pages look like … so hard to understand anything compared to how GitHub shows it … this weird markdowny format is super weird — using GitHub blocks or something which other planes use is better," naming orca.build as the reference. D-099 had rendered the request as a receipt: a sentence, the raw body in a monospace `pre` with its `##` hashes showing, and lists beneath. Honest as a record; unreadable as a page.

**Decision.** The page is the host's own anatomy in this room's voice. The title is the title. One sentence says the state as a word first, then who wants to merge how many commits into what from what — the sentence every code host has taught — or, once resolved, how it ended; Open on GitHub and Edit sit at its end. The description renders as a document on one surface-1 sheet with an author/when head, captioned as the receipt that travelled or as the host's own description where Novus did not send one (D-208, D-209). A Commits block lists the host's commits up front — fetched once per request, so the sentence's count and the list never wait on a disclosure. Completion keeps its row; the segmented control reads Conversation · Checks · Changes, the open count on Conversation. Every review thread is the same sheet: author, `path:line`, open or resolved, when; the words as a document; Resolve, Send to chat, Open on GitHub on the foot; resolved threads stay, dimmed in words. Monochrome throughout — words carry state, the diff keeps the only hues — and no border inside a sheet (D-137).

**Alternatives.** A state pill on the sentence (rejected: DESIGN's status-is-words rule; the bold word does the work). Reproducing the host's diff hunk inside each thread card (deferred: the Changes tab holds the patches and the anchor names the line; a hunk excerpt per card is a later refinement, not the structure). Keeping the receipt's raw body beside the rendered one (rejected: the rendered document is the same bytes; Open on GitHub shows the host's copy).

**Consequences.** `pull-request.tsx`: `PullRequestPage` fetches files/commits once, renders head, description sheet, commits block, threads as cards; `PullHeadline` gains `opener`/`commitCount` and prints the full sentence on the page, its shorter form on the receipt summary (which keeps the title line the page no longer duplicates). CSS: `.pull-head/.pull-title/.pull-sentence/.pull-state-word`, `.pull-card*`, `.pull-threads`, `.pull-commit*`. `pr.spec.ts` asserts the new sentence by its state word and the rendered description (no `## ` on the page), and captures the conversation as cards (evidence `216`); evidence `108`–`114`/`137` regenerated and reviewed — title, sentence, document, commits, readiness, thread card all checked by eye.

**Revisit when.** A thread wants its diff hunk in the card; or the host's timeline (reviews, approvals, force-pushes) wants listing between description and conversation, which is the next block the host's page has and this one does not.

## D-211 — People on the request page wear their picture

**Context.** On the restructured page (D-210) the owner, immediately: "where my GitHub username is and where the reviewers' GitHub usernames are, there is no icon to represent — makes it so much easier to see frankly." GitHub puts a face beside every name on a request, and a conversation scans by faces before it scans by names.

**Decision.** Every person the page names wears the D-105 mark — their GitHub picture at row size, initials where none resolves — beside their login: the opener on the description sheet's head, each review thread's author, each requested reviewer in the review sentence, each commit's author. The mark is the one identity mark the product already has (a human is a circle, D-105 fetches the host's own `{login}.png`), so reviewers and outside authors resolve exactly as participants do; nothing new is drawn and no colour is introduced.

**Alternatives.** Marks only on threads (rejected: the owner named his own name on the description and the reviewers' line first). A generic person glyph (rejected: the point is recognition, which only the picture gives).

**Consequences.** `Person` in `pull-request.tsx` (`HumanMark` + login), `.pull-person*` styles. Proven: `pr.spec.ts` green; evidence `216` re-reviewed with the reviewer's picture resolved beside her name on the review line and the thread head.

**Revisit when.** A commit's author arrives as a git name rather than a login (initials show; mapping names to logins is the host's knowledge, not Novus's); or the description sheet wants the opener's picture in the sentence as well.

## D-212 — The rail is a ring: ⌃⇥ selects the next project

**Context.** The owner: "using control tab to navigate between different projects open super super useful imo for navigation." First read as walking the *open missions* and shipped that way (ed3eccc); corrected by the owner within the hour: "its to go between projects … we alr have command 1 to 9 if i wanna swap between [missions] … it doesnt have to be open … if its close it opens it." The layers are distinct and both already existed: `⌘1–9` reaches a project's missions by rail position, `⌘T` starts one there — what nothing did was move which *project* those aim at.

**Decision.** Two rebindable chords in the D-204 registry: `⌃⇥` moves you into the next project in the rail, `⌃⇧⇥` the previous, wrapping at both ends; with nothing selected the first press lands on an end. *Into* means the room changes (the owner's third word on it: "actually move me there"): you land on the room you last read in that project if one is open — remembered per project, so moving between projects returns you to where you were in each — else its first mission opens as a room, else, for a project with no missions, the selection alone. The chosen project is **disclosed** if it was folded. (D-077's rule that the project *row's click* never moves the room stands; this chord is a different act.) This required the selected project to become the person's own: it had been derived as the active tab's project outright, which left `⌘1–9` unable to aim anywhere but the open room's project; it is now the rail selection, which follows the room being read whenever *that* changes and otherwise stays where a keystroke or click put it. One pure helper (`cycleKey`) carries the ring; the mission-tab cycler the first reading shipped is removed rather than left beside it. A Tab chord is only ever the Control one — `⌘⇥` is the platform's app switcher and never reaches the window — so the label says `⌃`.

**Alternatives.** Keeping both cyclers on different chords (rejected: the owner named one want, and `⌘1–9` already covers rooms within a project). Walking only the projects with open rooms (rejected by the owner's own words: it need not be open). Selecting without moving (shipped for an hour, rejected by the owner on first use: a navigation chord that leaves you where you were is not navigation).

**Consequences.** `nextProject` / `previousProject` on the Keyboard page; `currentProjectKey` derivation flipped with a following effect; `cycleKey` in its own module with its own test; `cycleTab` and its tests gone. Evidence `63-` and `201-` recaptured; the navigation suite re-run whole against the derivation change.

**Revisit when.** The rail grows groupings (pinned, recent) that make "next" ambiguous.

## D-213 — Changes has a scope: what this prompt did, beside what the mission did

**Context.** The owner: "it shows 22 files changed, which is good — I want that — however if I am doing a bunch of code changes the Changes section shows all changes. I also want somewhere, either in the mission or in Changes, to see what was done in that prompt run." The mission-wide list is the right default and stays; what was missing was the per-turn view of the same facts.

**Decision.** The Changes section takes a scope. Its own tab is the whole mission, as before. A turn's CHECKPOINT row on the trace — which already said *1 file changed +3 −0 8531faae · Changes* — now opens the section narrowed to that checkpoint: the turn's own files with the turn's own diffs, which the record already held (every checkpoint carries its `files`, each `changeId` the diff of that commit and no other), under one sentence naming the prompt that asked for them and offering *Whole mission* as the way back. No new data, no new route: a projection the room already had, finally reachable from the row that names it.

The scope is owned by the shell beside the section itself. Every other way into Changes — the tab, `G C`, the state line's *Review changes* — is the whole mission and clears it; leaving the mission clears it; a scope whose checkpoint the detail no longer holds reads as the whole mission rather than as an empty list. The prompt is the direction that started the turn, bounded to a line, with the full words on hover; a turn with no direction on record is named by its revision instead of by invented words.

**Alternatives.** A per-turn sub-section on the trace (rejected: the diff belongs in the one place diffs are read, and a second diff surface is prohibited pattern territory). A filter chip on Changes listing turns (rejected for now: a chip is furniture the reader must notice; the row on the trace is already the name of the turn, and clicking it is the natural ask — a turn picker can join the scoped header if fan-out turns make "which turn was that" a real question). Grouping the mission-wide list by turn (rejected: the mission-wide answer is "what is on the branch now", and grouping it by history answers a different question worse).

**Consequences.** `derive.ts`: `checkpointFiles`, `checkpointPrompt`. `inspector.tsx`: `changesScope` / `onChangesScope`, the scoped header. `project-shell.tsx`: the scope beside the section, cleared by every other way in and by leaving the mission; `project-room.tsx` and `direction-trace.tsx` carry the checkpoint id from the row. `room-presentation.test.ts` (+3: path order and the prompt, the missing checkpoint and the wordless turn, the bound), `slice.spec.ts` (the tab's way in shows no scope; the row's way in names the prompt and clears; evidence `220-`).

**Revisit when.** A fan-out turn's checkpoint wants its workers' footprints told apart; or the scoped header wants a turn picker, at which point it becomes the Changes section's own navigation rather than a sentence.

## D-214 — The pin sits on the row

**Context.** Owner, on the scoped Changes list: "add to chat gotta be same line, and maybe not this big text — some nice icon." Under D-182 every changed-file row carried a full-width *Add to chat* text button beneath it, so eighteen files were thirty-six rows, and the verb — a quiet one — was the loudest thing in the list.

**Decision.** One line per file. The row stays the toggle for its diff; the pin — a stroke pushpin on the same 16-grid as every other glyph, `--text-3` — sits at the row's end and is quiet until the row is hovered or the pin itself is focused, the rail's `+` idiom, so a long list wears no controls at rest and every row has one at hand. Hover and selection wash the whole line, so the toggle and the pin read as one row. The structure changes with it: a button cannot hold a button, so the line is a flex container of the two, not a text button under a row. Nothing about the verb changes — the pin still adds the file to the next message, and the turn's record still carries it.

**Alternatives.** Always-visible pin (rejected: eighteen rows, eighteen icons — the rail already decided this). The word kept but smaller (rejected: the word at any size is a second line; the ask was the line). Spreading the pin to the verification ledger and the file view now (deferred: the ledger's row already holds its verbs inline beside *Run again*, and the file view's is a header action — neither has the second-line problem; when either is touched the pin is the mark to use).

**Consequences.** `inspector.tsx`: `PinGlyph`, `.change-line` / `.change-pin`; DESIGN's Diff bullet; `slice.spec.ts` captures the hovered row magnified (evidence `221-`); `sessions.spec.ts`'s D-182 case still pins through the same test id.

**Revisit when.** The pin wants a pressed state on the row after pinning (today the composer's chip is the receipt); or the ledger and file view get the mark.

## D-215 — A draft belongs to its conversation

**Context.** Owner: "when the icon adds to chat it adds to chat, but if I go to another chat it still shows it added — it should only be in that chat." True, and wider than the pin: the pinned references were one room-level list, and the composer itself was keyed per mission, so a chat switch carried the box — words, attachments, pins — into whichever conversation came next.

**Decision.** The box belongs to the conversation it was started in. Pinned references are held per chat; the composer is keyed per chat and remembers each chat's words, attached files, and held paths in a scratch while the app runs, so a switch swaps the box for that chat's own and a return finds it as it was left. The new-session draft surface is its own conversation for this purpose, and a lane with no chats yet is its own. Nothing is persisted: a relaunch starts every box empty, as it always did, and an attachment uploaded and never sent is simply unsent.

**Alternatives.** Clearing the box on every switch (rejected: losing a half-written message because you glanced at a sibling chat is the punishment the pin bug merely hinted at). Persisting drafts to disk (deferred: a draft that survives a relaunch is a feature with its own questions — whose, for how long — not a fix for this). Keeping pins room-wide but showing which chat each was made in (rejected: a pin is an intention for *this* next message; labelling it for another chat is explaining a mistake instead of not making it).

**Consequences.** `composer.tsx`: a `scratchKey` and the in-memory scratch; `project-room.tsx`: pins keyed by chat, the composer keyed by chat. `sessions.spec.ts`'s D-182 case now also proves the switch — pin in one chat, nothing in the sibling, a draft typed there stays there, the pin is waiting on return — and fails against the previous code at the first of those.

**Amended the same evening, owner-hit across two missions.** The first fix keyed pins per conversation and still leaked — in both directions — because the room is keyed per *mission* and unmounts on a mission switch. Going out: the inspector hands a pin to the shell as an ask (`contextAsk`), and the shell kept the ask after delivery, so every room that mounted afterwards replayed it — the pin reappeared in whatever mission was opened next. The ask is consumed on delivery now (`onContextConsumed`, findAsk's own pattern). Coming back: pins kept as the room's own state died with the instance, so the pin was gone on return. They live in `pins.ts` now, a store outside any component, keyed by the same conversation key as the composer's scratch. `navigation.spec.ts` proves both legs across two real missions — pinned here, absent there, waiting on return — and fails against the first fix.

**Revisit when.** Drafts want to survive a relaunch, or a pin wants to travel deliberately — a "move to that chat" act, never an accident.

## D-216 — A built-in's answer is a printout, not prose

**Context.** The owner ran `/usage` through a governed turn and got a wall: "is it possible for slash commands like these to be displayed nicer." The pixels said why. Headless, a built-in returns the CLI's own text — line-broken sections, literal underscores in `America/Los_Angeles` — and the room rendered it as markdown (D-145): paragraphs collapsed into run-on sentences, and the underscores opened an italic run across two lines.

**Decision.** A turn whose direction invoked one of the harness's own built-in commands — by name, against the list this machine captured from the session's own announcement (D-188) — renders its speech **preformatted**: the `md-pre` block the renderer already owns, wrapped at the measure rather than scrolled, because a usage report is read, not copied. Everything else stays markdown: a project command is a prompt template whose answer is the model's prose, and a skill invocation is prose too. The judgement lives in the feed projection (`printout` on the trace block), where it is tested without a window.

**Alternatives.** Parsing `/usage` into a panel of its own (rejected: D-188's anti-drift rule — the CLI's report shape is the CLI's to change, and a parser is a mirror that rots). Escaping underscores and preserving breaks inside the markdown path (rejected: it would change how every reply renders to fix one kind). Detecting printouts by content heuristics (rejected: the direction already says what kind of answer to expect; guessing from the text is how false captions get made).

**Consequences.** `TraceBlock.printout`; `SegmentView` takes it and renders `<pre class="md-pre mono harness-printout">`; one wrap rule in the stylesheet. `trace-presentation` pins the judgement (built-in yes, project command no, words no); `skills.spec` drives `/compact` through the menu in a real window and finds the printout.

**Revisit when.** Built-ins start returning structured output (json) headless, at which point a real panel earns itself.

## D-217 — Accounts are lent, not excluded: a person's own claude.ai connectors reach the turns their machine runs

**Context.** The owner, on the connector gap D-198 had stated as a vendor wall: "i defo want to use all of claude mcps in here as well feels stupid not to … as part of onboarding … it also asks maybe in a next page whether i want to onboard all claude existing stuff, gmail drive … i can say yes or no to each one … with the ability to modify in the settings later." D-198 had measured the claude.ai connectors as living in no local file and rejected "carrying account connectors by relaxing strictness" outright, on the ground that "strict config is the guarantee"; D-199's amendment then measured the by-URL road dead (Google's authorization server refuses dynamic client registration) and left Gmail, Calendar, and Drive terminal-only.

**Measured first, against installed `claude 2.1.241`, through the pinned argv.** The guarantee was misattributed. (1) `--setting-sources ""` alone keeps every agent-writable file out: a worktree holding a `.mcp.json`, a `.claude/settings.json`, and a `.claude/settings.local.json` each declaring a server loads **none** of them without `--strict-mcp-config`, while `--setting-sources project` loads the `.mcp.json` at once — the setting-source pin is the wall, and strict was belt over it. (2) With strict omitted under that pin, the CLI injects its own account connectors — `claude.ai Gmail`, `Google Calendar`, `Google Drive` connected, 48 tools — and nothing else. (3) A connector re-declared by its URL in a config Novus writes is `needs-auth`: the CLI applies the account's OAuth only to entries it injects itself, which is D-198's finding, confirmed and now located exactly. (4) `--disallowedTools "mcp__claude_ai_Google_Drive__*"` removes that connector's tools from the model's tool list entirely — the others stay. (5) `claude mcp list` names each connector with its URL and connection state, so a machine can say what it holds without spending a turn.

**Decision.** A person **lends** their own accounts to the turns their machine runs; nothing is excluded by default and nothing is carried without their word.

- **Lending is the person's standing choice, on their own machine.** After GitHub connects, first-run gains **one more page** — *Lend your accounts* — listing the connectors Claude Code on this Mac is signed in to, each with its own On/Off, nothing on until chosen, skippable in one click, with the same rows on Settings → Agents for ever after.
