The choice lives machine-locally (`connectors.json` beside the notification preferences) and crosses the wire only as a fact on each turn's record, never as a preference. There is deliberately **no per-mission gate and no Mission Admin switch**: a project's server is the *mission's* tool surface and an Admin gates it (D-119); a machine server is enabled per mission by owner-plus-Admin (D-198); a lent account is the **person's own** surface and the person's standing choice is its gate. The page appears only when there is something to lend — an empty page would be a fictional control (DESIGN prohibited pattern 11).
- **Spawn.** When nothing is lent the argv is byte-for-byte what D-119 pinned, strict included. When at least one account is lent, `--strict-mcp-config` is omitted — `--setting-sources ""` remains the measured wall — and every connector the CLI would inject that is **not** lent is removed with `--disallowedTools "mcp__{id}__*"`, where the id is the CLI's own sanitization of the connector's name. A read-alongside turn lends nothing, as it carries no capture endpoint (D-095, D-123). `execution.running` names the connectors carried, and the trace states *Accounts carried: Gmail, Google Drive — {owner}'s*. A project or machine server whose name begins `claude.ai` or `claude_ai` is refused at discovery, the `novus` reservation's pattern (D-123), so nothing can wear a connector's tool prefix.
- **A lent account's tool call asks its lender — under every profile, including Don't ask.** The runner's ladder routes such a call to the room whatever the lane's profile (plan's deny-all still stands above it: a plan turn acts on nothing); the control plane refuses any answer but the lender's — the runner row's owner — in words that name them, the baton not required because the account is theirs and not the lane's workspace; and the card says *{owner}'s own Gmail — only they can answer*. This amends D-115's "Don't ask answers everything" by exactly this case: the one answer that can spend somebody's own account is theirs, and an unattended profile set by a Mission Admin does not reach into a participant's inbox.
- **Scope.** Account connectors of the Gmail, Calendar, Drive, Notion kind. The desktop-wide and browser-wide tools (computer use, the Chrome extension) are not account connectors and are **not offered**: a tool that can click Novus's own Allow button ends supervised autonomy, and the room's preview is becoming the agent's own browser instead (the next decision). Native desktop-app projects — a project that *is* a desktop app cannot be previewed in a webview — are parked, named, and not claimed.

**Alternatives.** Keeping strict and waiting for the CLI to name connectors under it (rejected: D-198's revisit condition, now measured unnecessary — the pin already holds). Loading the account wholesale with no choice (rejected: D-186's lesson, one person's laptop silently shaping shared turns). A per-mission Admin gate on top of the person's choice (rejected: the wrong owner, and a second switch for one fact; an org-level ceiling stays the D-115 extension point). The baton holder answering with the account named on the card (rejected: a reviewer deciding about someone else's inbox; D-198's consequence sentence was written for an owner enabling a server *on one mission*, which this standing choice is not). Letting the Chrome and computer-use MCPs in beside the accounts (rejected on sight of what they can reach — everything on screen, the approval card included).

**Consequences.** `ConnectorSchema`/`ConnectorNameSchema` and `MAX_CONNECTORS` in the contracts; `connectors` on the `execution.running` payload and an `executions.connectors` column the respond route consults; `apps/desktop/electron/connectors.ts` (discover by `claude mcp list`, the preference file, the tool id, the argv it yields); the spawn flags and the router rule in `execution.ts`; the two name reservations in `mcp.ts` and `machine-mcp.ts`; the `/approvals/:id/respond` lender rule; the first-run page and its Settings card; the card's lender note; the trace line. `NOVUS_FAKE_CONNECTORS` feeds a fake list under the fake harness so a real window can be driven without this machine's account. PRODUCT's "uncarryable" sentence, ARCHITECTURE's strict-config paragraph, and DESIGN's first-run and Needs-approval entries change in the same commit. D-198's machine-server road is untouched — `notion` at user scope still rides it.

**Revisit when.** Cloud runners exist (no machine, no account to lend — the question returns as brokered credentials); an organization asks for a ceiling on what its members may lend; or the CLI names connectors under strictness, at which point strict returns to the argv with nothing else changing.

## D-218 — The agent gets hands: a fenced browser and raw computer use, with a guardrail that is structure first and words second

**Context.** Owner-directed, over a concern the author raised twice and the owner overrode twice. The author's position: raw computer use (the agent driving the real mouse and keyboard across the whole Mac) cannot be perfectly fenced, because the OS delivers clicks by screen position — there is no reliable "click anywhere except Novus," so a system prompt telling the agent to leave Novus alone is a suggestion, not a boundary, and the one thing it must never do (approve its own actions by clicking Novus's own Allow button) is exactly the thing words cannot prevent. The owner's decision: build it anyway. "I do want a system prompt to be used as a guardrail and have the ability to raw computer use." Also asked for: a **visible cursor** — the agent's pointer moving on screen the way Codex and Anthropic's own computer use show it — and, restated as the load-bearing rule, that the agent must **never be able to turn its own permission on when it is off**.

**Decision.** Two hands, one guardrail built in layers — structure carrying the weight the prompt cannot.

- **Two surfaces, not one capability.** The **fenced browser** (D-217's next step) is a webview inside Novus the agent may navigate, click, type, and read — sealed by construction: it cannot reach the desktop, other windows, or Novus's own chrome, so no guardrail is *needed* there, only the grant model below. **Raw computer use** is the new, dangerous one: a Novus-authored tool that captures the screen and synthesizes real input across the whole machine. It is off by default and per person, exactly as a lent account is (D-217): a standing machine-local choice, never a mission setting, never something a role or a lease can switch on.

- **The guardrail is four layers, and the prompt is the weakest one, stated as such.**
  1. **Novus injects its own system prompt** (owner-directed): the composed turn carries a Novus-authored directive — not the project's `CLAUDE.md`, a separate pinned block the agent cannot edit — stating that it may operate the Mac but must never act on Novus itself or any process Novus is running, and must never attempt to change its own permissions. This is **instructions, and instructions are not authority** (the standing principle, D-062): it can be ignored or led past by something on screen, and it is the *last* line, not the first.
  2. **A structural refusal is the real fence.** Every computer-use action is checked in the main process before it reaches the OS: if Novus's own window is frontmost, or the target coordinates fall inside a Novus window's bounds, the action is refused and the turn is told why. This is enforcement, not a sentence — the agent cannot click what it is refused. **Honest gap, recorded rather than hidden**: a determined agent could send Novus to the background first, and occlusion and multi-display make bounds imperfect; this narrows the exposure sharply but does not close it, which is the residue raw computer use carries by nature.
  3. **The permission is un-self-renewable.** The agent cannot turn its own capability on. The grant is minted by a person, server-side, and pinned; the agent reaches no switch, writes no settings (`--setting-sources ""` stands), and a computer-use tool call is refused unless an unspent, person-minted grant stands — the same shape as the capture endpoint's grant (D-123). This is the owner's "never turn its own permissions back on," and it is already how every Novus permission works.
  4. **Mid-turn cutoff.** The grant is turn-scoped **and** revocable mid-turn: a stop control on the acting surface ends computer-use access at once, without ending the turn, and the next action is refused. One approval covers the turn's actions (clicking is not per-click-approved, which would be unusable); the cutoff is the person's hand on the plug.

- **The cursor is visible.** While the agent operates the machine, an on-screen overlay shows its pointer and what it is doing — the Codex/computer-use affordance the owner named — so a person watching sees every move and knows where the stop control's plug is going in. A visible actor is part of the guardrail, not decoration.

**Alternatives.** Prompt-only, as first asked (rejected by the author and, on reflection, by the owner's own rule — words cannot stop the one action that matters; the structural layer is why this is buildable at all). Browser-only, no raw computer use (the author's recommendation; rejected by the owner). Per-click approval (rejected: unusable, and the mid-turn cutoff plus visible cursor give control without it). Detecting Novus by pixels or window title alone (rejected as sole mechanism: spoofable; the frontmost-app and bounds check in the main process is the trustworthy signal, imperfect as noted).

**Consequences.** Raw computer use is a **Novus-authored native tool** — the headless `claude` Novus spawns has no computer-use MCP, so Novus builds one (screen capture plus input synthesis via the OS, behind the `novus` endpoint like the screenshot and push tools), with the four-layer guardrail, the turn grant, the mid-turn stop, the visible-cursor overlay, and the injected system prompt. It is a large native build and is **not yet implemented** — this entry records the agreed shape; the fenced browser is its smaller, safer sibling and lands first. PRODUCT's capability table gains the two acting capabilities; ARCHITECTURE's harness-protocol section gains the tool, the structural refusal, and the grant; DESIGN gains the acting overlay and the stop control. Every one names the honest residue in words.

**Amended 2026-08-23, both surfaces built to the boundary the entry named.** The **fenced browser** landed whole: five tools on the `novus` endpoint driving the isolated webview, a per-turn session (one approval, revocable mid-turn), the banner and cut-off, the cursor dot — proven in a real window (the page itself the witness). The **raw computer-use governed core** landed too: the machine-local opt-in (off by default, unreachable by the agent, its own settings switch), the structural fence (refuses any action on a Novus window, a keypress while Novus is frontmost), the per-turn session and mid-turn cut-off, the injected system-prompt directive, and the visible always-on-top cursor overlay — all proven against a `DesktopDriver` fake and, for the opt-in gate, end to end in a real window. The one part deliberately **not** built, exactly as this entry said, is the **native input backend**: synthesizing the real mouse and keyboard needs a native dependency and macOS Accessibility permission Novus neither adds nor grants, and it cannot be tested headlessly. Screen capture is real (Electron's `desktopCapturer`, needing Screen Recording permission). **Amended again the same day, owner-approved ("due to dependency and grant accessibility"): the native input backend is now wired** — `@nut-tree-fork/nut-js` as an **optional** dependency (a machine without it, or without a prebuild for its Electron, degrades to eyes-only rather than failing), adapted behind the `DesktopDriver` seam, gated on macOS Accessibility permission the settings card reads and can request. Proven to load under Electron 37.10.3 with Accessibility granted (throwaway probe). What is still not done, and deliberately: proving the acting path by moving the real cursor in an automated test, because that seizes the owner's own mouse — the fence, opt-in, session, and backend-load are all proven; the real-input look is the one left to the person at their own screen. **Amended 2026-08-24, owner-hit on the first live test:** the fence refused *every* key while Novus was frontmost, which trapped the agent — a person watching keeps Novus in front, so the agent could not take its first step, and it could not move Novus aside because moving Novus is operating Novus, which the fence also refuses (the circular trap). Resolved by letting exactly two shortcuts through even while Novus is front — ⌘-space (Spotlight) and ⌘-tab (app switch) — because they only move focus *away* from Novus and can operate nothing of it; typing and every other key while Novus is front stay refused. The directive gained a line telling the agent to use them to leave Novus first. **Also fixed the same day, owner-hit live:** the first real combo (⌘-space) failed with libnut's "Invalid key flag specified" — the real root cause, found only by testing the library **under Electron** (two earlier source-level guesses were wrong): nut-js's modifier-**flag** mechanism is broken under Electron — `keyToggle(key, down, [flag])` rejects the Command modifier with "Invalid key flag specified" under Electron even though the identical call works under plain node (proven: a benign flag probe passed under node, threw under Electron). The fix is to stop using nut-js for keyboard input and drive it through **macOS's own AppleScript** `System Events` — `key code 49 using {command down}` for ⌘-space, `keystroke` for text — which sets the modifier the way the OS expects and actually triggers system shortcuts; proven end-to-end by a screenshot of Spotlight opening from the exact script the code emits. Mouse input stays on nut-js, which has no flag involvement. Only Accessibility permission is needed, already held. And screen capture surfaced a second macOS permission — **Screen Recording**, separate from Accessibility and needed only by `computer_screenshot` — so the settings card now reads its status and opens the pane, since a rebuilt dev binary starts without it.

**Amended 2026-08-24, owner-hit on the first turn that actually worked — the agent was blind, and a person could not see what it saw.** Two things, both about the eye. First, a real bug: `computer_screenshot` captured the screen but returned only a *sentence* about it ("Screenshot of the 1440x900 screen") and threw the pixels away, so the model was asked to click and type against a screen it could not see — it was sending blind keystrokes. Fixed by returning the frame to the agent as an MCP **image block** (image first so a model reading the first block sees the screen, the sentence second), which is what makes the hands usable at all. Second, the owner's ask — "when it takes a screenshot it should show the image here as well": the frames the agent looks at now appear in the **room**. The runner pushes each screenshot over a host-local IPC channel (`novus:agent-screenshot`, main → renderer, never the control plane), and the room renders a live **Agent's view** card at the tail of the feed — the newest frame large with a breathing live dot, the earlier ones a thin strip, each opening full-size in its own lightbox (a live data URL, so nothing to reveal in Finder, unlike the artifact lightbox). The frames are **ephemeral**: kept only in renderer state, capped at a dozen, never persisted and never in the transcript, and dropped when the mission on screen changes — a person watching sees what the agent sees, and the record still holds only what the turn itself wrote. Proven by typecheck, the desktop build, and the full suite (603 pass, `artifact-mcp.test.ts` gaining the image-block assertion); the end-to-end look — a frame landing in the room during a live opt-in computer-use turn — is left to the person at their own screen, like the real-cursor proof this entry already deferred.

**Revisit when.** The OS grows a real per-app input fence (then layer 2 becomes trustworthy rather than best-effort); cloud runners need an actor that is not this Mac; or the residue in layer 2's gap proves reachable in practice and raw computer use has to be narrowed or pulled.

## D-219 — The controller can hand the baton back: `control.release`, the deliberate twin of expiry

**Context.** The lease machine has always defined a `released` state — no recipient; workstream unheld until claimed — but no verb reached it. A controller who was done had only bad options: offer control to a specific person (a different act — a handoff needs a named recipient who accepts), walk away and let the lease *expire* (the machine reading silence, minutes later, as what should have been a sentence), or hold authority they no longer wanted. The collaboration loop had a way to take and a way to give-to, but no way to give *back*.

**Decision.** A `control.release` capability and route (`POST /missions/:id/control/release`), lease-granted only, like `control.offer`. Semantics are expiry's, chosen instead of suffered: the lease moves `held → released` directly, the lane is unheld until claimed, work already authorized continues (D-034), and the event records whether the harness was left waiting on an approval — the same honesty `control.expired` carries. Release is refused while a handoff offer is live (`offer_live`, withdraw first): naming a recipient and walking away are different acts, and failing someone's live offer as a side effect of leaving would be silent. Open requests stay open; the existing claim path (a request against an unheld lease fulfills immediately, first accepted wins) is deliberately reused rather than auto-granting the oldest request — who gets an abandoned baton stays a visible, first-come claim, never a queue nobody agreed to. In the room, the holder's state line gains a quiet **Release control** text action beside "You have the baton", mirroring Request control's seat (DESIGN.md#signature-elements).

**Alternatives.** Auto-fulfilling the oldest open request on release (rejected: it turns release into a transfer with an implicit recipient, and the requester never accepted receiving it at that moment). Waiting for a safe boundary before releasing (rejected: expiry does not, and D-034 already separates authority from work — release removes the ability to issue privileged commands, it interrupts nothing). Overloading `control.revoke` on self (rejected: revoke is the Mission Admin's force-take and always ends with the admin holding the lease; release ends with nobody holding it).

**Consequences.** PRODUCT.md's capability table and #control gain the verb; the contracts package gains the capability and the bridge method; `authz.ts` adds it to the lease grants; `authority.ts` owns the route; the feed already knew how to say "released control". The claim path is now load-bearing for hand-back: release then re-request is the two-step return of a baton, each step attributed.

**Revisit when.** Multi-lane missions make per-lane release ambiguous in the interface, or a real team asks for release-to-requester as one act often enough that the two-step return proves to be friction rather than clarity.

## D-220 — The receipt leaves the room: a markdown export of the stored snapshot

**Context.** The receipt was durable but captive: snapshotted at close, re-renderable in the app, and reachable by nobody outside it. The record's whole purpose is to be handed to whoever was not in the room — a postmortem appendix, a reviewer, a future self without Novus open — and there was no way to hand it.

**Decision.** An **Export** action on the receipt document, rendering the stored `ReceiptSnapshot` to markdown and saving it through the OS save dialog. The projection (`receipt-export.ts`) is deterministic, like the transcript (D-173): rendered from the snapshot alone — no clock, no recomputation, no model — so the file and the screen cannot disagree; rationale and reasons verbatim; the "remains uncertain" section never omitted, stating its emptiness instead; visual evidence carried as references and digests, never bytes and never signed URLs (the artifact boundary D-122 already draws). The desktop main process owns only the dialog and the write (`novus:missions:export-receipt`); a cancelled dialog resolves null and the document says nothing, because a cancel is an answer. Markdown because the audience is people and diffs; the JSON snapshot stays durable server-side and re-derivable, so a second format waits for a real consumer.

**Alternatives.** PDF (rejected: a rendering pipeline for no named reader; markdown pastes into the places postmortems actually live). Exporting the JSON snapshot (rejected as the default: it is the storage shape, not the handoff shape; nothing prevents adding it later). Posting the receipt to the pull request automatically (rejected for now: publishing the record is a deliberate act with an audience question attached, not a side effect of closing).

**Consequences.** The bridge gains `missions.exportReceipt`; PRODUCT.md's Receipt definition gains "exportable"; DESIGN.md's receipt section gains the head's one action. The e2e proves the written file against the rendered room via a declared export directory standing in for the dialog's answer — a test seam, not a product path.

**Revisit when.** A consumer wants the JSON or a host wants the receipt posted to the PR — both are one projection away from the same snapshot.

## D-221 — The control plane becomes deployable: a chosen bind, a container, TLS in front

**Context.** ARCHITECTURE.md has promised "a Novus-hosted control plane" since D-018, but the process could only ever serve the machine it ran on: `listen` hardcoded `127.0.0.1`, there was no image, and no recorded position on TLS. Everything else was already deployment-shaped — `NOVUS_CP_PUBLIC_URL` derives the OAuth callback and the App webhook, sessions are bearer tokens with no cookie to mis-flag, the desktop honors `NOVUS_CP_URL`, migration runs on boot, and the webhook receiver and S3 artifact adapter exist precisely for a public deployment.

**Decision.** Three pieces, each the smallest honest one. **The bind is configuration**: `NOVUS_CP_HOST`, loopback by default — local-first must not become network-reachable by accident; a deployment widens it deliberately. **The container is the unit of deployment**: `apps/control-plane/deploy/Dockerfile` (Node 24, contracts built, TypeScript run directly exactly as `pnpm dev` runs it) with a compose file pairing it with PostgreSQL and a volume-backed artifact store. **TLS terminates in front, never inside**: the process speaks plain HTTP to a reverse proxy or load balancer; `NOVUS_CP_PUBLIC_URL` carries the https identity. No in-process TLS, no cert plumbing in config.

**Alternatives.** In-process TLS (rejected: certificate lifecycle inside the app for no gain over the proxy every real deployment already has). Building contracts to JS and bundling (rejected: a second build pipeline for the same code; strip-types is how the process already runs everywhere). A root-level Dockerfile (rejected: the image is the control plane's, and the root belongs to the canon).

**Consequences.** ARCHITECTURE.md#deployment-modes states the deployed shape. Proven so far: the image builds, compose brings up PostgreSQL and the control plane, migration runs, and `/health` answers on loopback **and on the machine's LAN address** — a second machine on the network can reach it via `NOVUS_CP_URL`. **Not yet proven**: a run on a box that is not this Mac, behind real TLS, with a real GitHub OAuth App pointed at the public callback — that is the live proof this row still owes (AGENTS.md rule 11).

**Revisit when.** Cloud runners land (D-023) — the workspace provider may want the control plane co-located — or a real deployment's proxy needs websocket/stream affordances the mission stream's long-poll does not already survive.

## D-222 — The desktop app becomes installable: an unsigned dmg, and an address a Finder launch can hold

**Context.** `pnpm dev` was the only way to run the client — fine for this repository's owner, a dead end for anyone handed the product. Two things stood between a checkout and an installable app: no packaging at all, and a control-plane address that lived only in an environment variable no Finder launch carries.

**Decision.** **electron-builder** packages the app (`pnpm package` → `release/Novus-{version}-arm64.dmg`): the exact `dist-electron`/`dist-renderer` bytes development runs, production `node_modules`, the pty's prebuilt binary unpacked from the asar, and `npmRebuild` off because the prebuilt is the point. **Unsigned deliberately** (`identity: null`, owner-decided 2026-08-26): Gatekeeper's warning and the right-click-Open ritual are accepted for now — the audience is developers, and a Developer ID is a later purchase, not a launch blocker. **The address is a file**: a packaged app reads `{userData}/control-plane.url` (one http(s) URL, trailing slashes trimmed) when `NOVUS_CP_URL` is absent, loopback remaining the default — so pointing an installed app at a deployed control plane (D-221) is writing one line, not crafting an environment. The existing D-027 rule carries the safety: every test hook refuses packaged builds, so the dmg is only ever the real product.

**Alternatives.** Signing + notarization now (rejected by the owner: $99/yr and enrollment latency for a warning developers can click through). electron-forge (rejected: electron-builder is the boring, dominant choice and handles the pnpm workspace). A first-run settings screen for the address (deferred: a file is honest and scriptable; a surface for it is DESIGN work that deserves its own slice when a non-developer audience exists).

**Consequences.** ARCHITECTURE.md#deployment-modes records the packaged shape and the address file. `release/` is git-ignored. `e2e/packaged.spec.ts` launches the packaged bundle itself — skipped when none exists, and its skip means unpackaged, never passing — asserting `isPackaged` and a real landing surface, with evidence captured only for the signed-out landing because a signed-in shell is somebody's real missions. Proven on this machine: the dmg builds, and the packaged app launches into a real signed-in session against the local control plane. **Not proven**: an install on a second Mac from the dmg alone — that is the row's live proof.

**Amended 2026-08-27, owner-hit on the first real Finder launch.** Two findings. First, a real bug: a Finder launch inherits launchd's bare PATH, so the setup card said Codex was *not found on this Mac* while a terminal `pnpm dev` found it fine — `codex` lives under nvm (`~/.nvm/versions/node/v24.18.0/bin`), which neither the probe's fallback list nor `novusPath`'s covered, and the same hole would have made a packaged turn unable to spawn any version-manager-installed CLI (`claude` escaped only because `~/.local/bin` is on the fallback list). Fixed structurally: at startup the app resolves the **interactive login shell's** PATH once — interactive because nvm initializes in `.zshrc`, which a plain login shell never reads — marker-fenced against rc-file noise, 4-second timeout, silent fallback on failure, and folds the missing entries into its own `process.env.PATH`, so the probe, `novusPath`, and every spawn simply inherit a complete one (`amendPathFromLoginShell`, `workspace-env.ts`; extraction and merge unit-tested; the owner's shell verified to surface the nvm bin). Second, not a bug in the app: the packaged app's GitHub card said sign-in was unconfigured because the **D-221 compose stack was still up from the day before**, squatting the default port 4460 with empty OAuth values — the exact `--env-file` misuse its own header documents — and with its *own* PostgreSQL, so the packaged app had landed in a different, empty world without saying so. Resolved operationally (stop the stack, or restart it with `--env-file .env`); the compose file also dropped its dead `NOVUS_GHAPP_*` rows (D-223 retired them). The honest residue: nothing yet tells a person *which* control plane a window is talking to — two worlds on one port number is a footgun the surface does not name; recorded here rather than papered over.

**Amended again the same day, second owner-hit round.** The fresh dmg still reported Codex missing: the login-shell fold-in runs at `whenReady`, but `harness-probe.ts` and `connectors.ts` had each frozen `process.env.PATH` into a module-scope constant at import — before the fold-in ever ran — so the probe kept the bare Finder PATH it was built to escape. Both compute per call now; the lesson is the constant, not the fold-in: nothing may snapshot PATH at module load. The repos refusal was the container again — the port-clash recovery's `up -d` (no `--build`) silently reused the previous day's pre-D-223 image and compose recreated nothing; `--force-recreate` landed the rebuilt image, verified by the running container holding `github-user-provider.ts`.

**Revisit when.** A non-developer audience arrives (signing, notarization, auto-update, an address surface in Settings), or a second platform is asked for.

## D-223 — Repository access is the person's own: user tokens replace the GitHub App everywhere a person acts

**Context.** Owner-hit on the Add-project dialog: repositories the owner had been invited to as a collaborator never appeared, while Conductor on the same machine shows them all. The cause is structural, not a bug in the listing: the live provider (D-031/D-032) saw GitHub through a **GitHub App installation** — `/installation/repositories` under an installation token — so it could only ever see repositories where the App itself is installed, and its V0 shortcut bound to the App's *first* installation besides. A repository someone else owns, where the owner is merely a collaborator, is invisible to that credential no matter what Novus does, because the credential models *the App's* reach, not *the person's*. The owner's direction, after the difference was explained: "i want it to be conductor like — even stuff im added to i can open. that makes so so so much more sense."

**Decision.** Repository access becomes the acting person's own GitHub access. D-031's provider boundary stays; its credential model reverses.

- **Every provider call names whose credential performs it.** `RepositoryProvider` methods gain a leading `actor: RepoActor` (`{ token: string | null }`). The live adapter is now `GithubUserRepositoryProvider`: plain REST under `Bearer {actor.token}`, no App JWT, no installation tokens, no server-held GitHub credential at all. A null token, or a 401 from GitHub, refuses with a named error (`repo_token_missing` → "sign in to GitHub again") rather than falling back to anything shared. The fake and unconfigured providers keep the interface; the fake ignores the actor, which is honest — deterministic tests fabricate identity wholesale.
- **Listing is the person's whole reach.** `listRepositories` reads `/user/repos?affiliation=owner,collaborator,organization_member`, newest push first — owned, collaborator, and org-member repositories alike, exactly what the person's own `git` could clone. The token is the `repo`-scoped OAuth token sign-in has stored since D-101 widened the scope; nothing new is asked of the person.
- **Whose token, where.** A user route spends the caller's token, read at the moment of the call (D-101's own pattern, now the rule rather than the exception). The runner's clone and push credentials become the **runner owner's** token — `runners.owner_user_id` already records who enrolled the machine, and the machine already runs that person's harness login, so the git credential now matches the machine's standing identity. Background sweeps (poll ingestion, adoption) spend the **pull creator's** token, falling back to the mission creator's for adoption; a row whose person has no stored token is skipped that pass and stays visibly unsynced rather than being read with someone else's credential.
- **The App retires from repository access.** `GithubAppRepositoryProvider`, the manifest setup flow (`/setup/github-app`), and the `NOVUS_GHAPP_ID`/`NOVUS_GHAPP_PEM_B64` config leave the tree. The webhook receiver stays — it verifies an HMAC secret and re-reads through the provider, so it never needed App credentials — and OAuth sign-in is untouched (it was always a separate classic OAuth app; verified live: the stored token is `gho_`, the client `Ov23`, so `repo` scope genuinely reaches collaborator repositories).
- **Authorship stops being the App's.** A pull request, comment, merge, or branch push now reaches GitHub as the person whose token acted — reversing D-100's "authored by the App, attributed in the body" and deleting D-101's app-authored fallback: where no token stands, the act refuses instead of wearing a bot's name.

**Alternatives.** Keeping the App and aggregating all installations (rejected: fixes multi-org, still cannot see a collaborator repository on an account that never installed the App — the model, not the loop bound, was wrong for a person-centered product). A hybrid — App where installed, user token elsewhere (rejected: two credential paths to keep honest, for no capability the token alone lacks). D-031's original rejection of user tokens said "identity is not authorization, and it collapses the org-level trust model" — that argument was right for a shared cloud control plane and is deliberately traded away here: in the local-first deployment the control plane serves the person whose tokens it holds, and *GitHub itself* enforces per-person authorization on every call, which is stricter than an App acting org-wide regardless of who asked.

**Consequences.** The trades, named. The control plane's database now holds standing `repo`-scoped user tokens whose *use* is broad, not just their storage (storage began at D-101); the runner's handout is the owner's own token rather than a ~1h repository-scoped mint — the same credential the owner's own `git` uses, but no longer narrowable per repository (ARCHITECTURE#secret-placement updated to say so). Organizations enforcing SAML SSO refuse classic OAuth tokens until the person authorizes them for that org — surfaced as GitHub's own error, recorded as a known limit. Rate limits become per-user (5,000/h), which local-first traffic does not approach. `sendComment`'s "via Novus" body prefix and `authoredAs: "app"` die with the fallback; the deterministic test that asserted them now asserts the refusal. The live suites (`live-ingest`, `live-webhook`, `live-pr-adapter`) construct the user provider from `NOVUS_LIVE_GITHUB_TOKEN` instead of App credentials. The Add-project dialog's "GitHub App not installed" state becomes "GitHub sign-in lacks repository access", and a fresh `repo_token_missing` state asks for a re-sign-in by name. PRODUCT's Repository definition, ARCHITECTURE's provider/secret/ingestion sections, and DESIGN's dialog-state list update in this change. **Proven deterministically** (provider unit tests against a local stub asserting the affiliation query, the bearer header, the null-token and 401 refusals; the full suites green). **Not yet proven live**: the owner's next real listing showing a collaborator repository, and a real turn cloning one — the same one-machine live proof every GitHub row already owes.

**Revisit when.** A deployed multi-tenant control plane or a cloud runner arrives (a server acting with no person present wants its own credential again — that is the moment a GitHub App returns, as the *server's* identity rather than the person's); GitHub's fine-grained user tokens mature enough to narrow the runner handout per repository; or SSO-bound organizations become a real audience.

## D-224 — The preview grows a browser's hands for the person: free navigation, an address bar, history — while the agent and evidence stay fenced

**Context.** Owner-directed, on sight of the confinement: "we shoul dhave a url bar and all that … insid ebranchwork i have this personal portfolio server running and not being able to open my github hyperlink is jus stupid so full browser capaiblit needs to be tehre." Offered the narrower fixes — external links popping to the system browser, with or without an origin-bound address bar — and the owner chose the full in-app browser explicitly. This reverses D-098's "navigation is confined to the approved origin … this is a view of one running application, not a browser" and DESIGN's "the surface never grows … an address bar, or any navigation chrome."

**Decision.** The person may browse the embedded surface anywhere on the web; the surface's *identity* does not move an inch.

- **Opening is unchanged.** A preview still opens only on a loopback address a live run process of the workstream reports right now (D-098's admission, whole). The tab is still one lane's app; the address bar is where the person may wander from it.
- **The person's navigation is `http`/`https`, credential-free, and nothing else.** `will-navigate`, `will-redirect`, and the window-open handler now admit any such address (`browserNavigationAllowed`); `file:`, `javascript:`, `about:`, smuggled credentials, and control characters stay refused in the main process. The address bar resolves a schemeless typing as `https://` — never a search. `window.open` and `target="_blank"` still open no second window: the element carries `allowpopups` so the guest's new-window requests actually reach the main process's handler (without it they are blocked outright — hit live on the first real portfolio link, which was `target="_blank"` and clicked dead), and the handler denies every window, loading a navigable address in place; the preview stays one page deep.
- **Every sandbox clamp stands.** No preload, no Node, no `window.novus`, sandboxed, an isolated **in-memory** partition per workstream (so a web login does not survive the app; accepted by the owner in the choosing), every OS permission denied, downloads refused.
- **The agent's hands stay the fenced browser D-218 promised.** `browser_click`, `browser_type`, `browser_press`, and `browser_read` refuse in words while the page is off the approved origin; `browser_navigate` still admits only same-origin destinations and is the agent's one way home from wherever the person went. Honest residue, named: a link the agent *clicks inside its own app* can now lead the top frame off-origin (navigation no longer blocks it) — but the next act there refuses, so the agent can follow a link out and read nothing.
- **Evidence stays bound to the app.** A capture or recording while the page is off the approved origin is refused in words — pixels of wherever the person browsed, attributed to the lane's process and origin, would be the provenance lie D-122 exists to prevent.
- **The chrome is Novus's own.** Back, forward, and an address field that is the origin chip grown editable — the file-chip anatomy, mono, no favicon, no padlock, no progress theatre. `PreviewStatus` gains `currentUrl`/`canGoBack`/`canGoForward`; the bridge gains `preview.navigate`/`back`/`forward`, each decided in the main process.

**Alternatives.** External links via `shell.openExternal` with the webview confined (the author's recommendation — smallest change, keeps every recorded guarantee, real browser session outside; rejected by the owner in an explicit choice). An origin-bound editable address only (offered, not chosen). A persistent (on-disk) partition so web logins survive restarts (deliberately not done here: widening what a compromised dev-server page can reach into is a separate decision with its own residue; Revisit names it).

**Consequences.** `preview-policy.ts` gains `browserNavigationAllowed`/`resolveBrowseAddress` beside the untouched `previewNavigationAllowed`, which now fences only the agent's acts and evidence capture; `workspace-preview.ts` splits `drivable` from `drivableOnOrigin`, tracks location and history; the e2e's old refused-navigation proof becomes a browsing proof against a second loopback server, with the off-origin capture refusal and the bar's `file:` refusal asserted. PRODUCT's D-218 paragraph, ARCHITECTURE's embedded-preview and fenced-browser sections, and DESIGN's Preview-surface entry all update in this change.

**Revisit when.** Web logins in the preview matter enough to want a persistent partition (its own decision); the person browsing while an agent's browser session is live proves confusing in practice (the two hands share one page); or the preview wants tabs, at which point it has become a browser product and deserves its own design pass.

## D-225 — The preview grows tabs: several pages of one surface, each alive off screen

**Context.** Owner-directed, minutes after D-224 landed: "what about opeing several tabs that is no thtre." D-224 made the surface browsable but one page deep — a `_blank` link navigated in place, so reading GitHub meant leaving the portfolio page. D-098's Revisit clause had already named "more than one preview at a time" as the moment the one-view model would be revisited; this is that moment, arriving as tabs inside the one surface rather than sibling surfaces.

**Decision.** The preview holds several pages — tabs — under the one standing approval; nothing about the surface's identity or governance moves.

- **A tab exists only through the main process's own verb.** `preview.newTab` registers the tab (on the app itself with no address, or anywhere the D-224 browse policy allows) before any element exists; `will-attach-webview` admits an element only for a registered tab's exact address and partition, so the renderer — or a page — can never conjure one. `window.open` still opens no window: a navigable address loads in place, and becoming a tab is a person's act. At most 8 tabs, refused in words.
- **Every tab is the full D-163/D-224 clamp.** Its own guest process; the lane's one in-memory partition (so the tabs share the lane's web logins); no preload, no Node, sandboxed; permissions denied, downloads refused; navigation http(s)-only. Off screen a tab stays painted at opacity 0 — the D-170 rule applied per tab — so switching back is instant and nothing captures an empty guest.
- **The active tab is the surface.** The address bar, back/forward, reload, the agent's drive verbs, and evidence capture all act on the active tab, under their existing gates — the agent still refuses off the approved origin, and a capture away from the app still refuses in words. `PreviewStatus` carries `tabs` (id, registered `src`, title, `currentUrl`) and `activeTabId`, with the standing fields describing the active tab, so every existing reader keeps its meaning.
- **The strip appears only past one tab.** Quiet mono text chips — the page's title, or its host while untitled — with the selection wash on the active one and a plain ×; the `+` lives in the head. No favicons, no dragging, no overflow theatre. One tab needs no furniture, and closing the last tab is the preview tab's own close, refused in words from the strip's verb.
- **The process ending still takes the whole surface down.** `stopped` overrides every tab's page state: the surface's identity is the lane's app, and a surface whose app died does not keep browsing.

**Alternatives.** Sibling preview surfaces in the room's own tab row (rejected: the room's tabs are canvases — files, sessions, Compare — and N browser pages are one canvas's content, not N canvases). Unbounded tabs (rejected: 8 is generous for a preview; past that it is a browser product). Destroying inactive tabs' pages to save memory (rejected: D-170 already proved a destroyed guest costs state, reload flashes, and empty captures).

**Consequences.** `workspace-preview.ts` reworked from one `contents` to a tab list with per-tab page state (location, title, history, phase) and per-tab adoption; the attach gate matches pending tabs; `preview.newTab`/`selectTab`/`closeTab` cross the bridge; `preview-surface.tsx` renders one element per tab and the strip; the e2e proves the second tab is its own live page (browsed away while the first does not move), switching, and closing. `PreviewStatusSchema` widens additively.

**Revisit when.** Tabs want to survive the preview tab's close or the app's restart (a session-restore decision); the agent wants to see or drive a non-active tab; or two lanes' previews want to be open at once, which is D-098's original one-view rule resurfacing a level up.

## D-226 — The person gets hands on the workspace's ignored files: type in the `.env`, declare the secret, from inside Novus

**Context.** Owner-hit, live, on a friend's downloaded repository: the agent set everything up, then dead-ended at "paste your Anthropic API key into referral-intake/.env" — a file that existed nowhere on the machine — and the owner's honest reading of the product was "i dont think inside novus i have ability to make any file changes … so i have to then go to cursor". The mechanism was closer than it looked: secrets (D-044) and ignored-file copying (D-041) both existed, but the dialog could only *supply values* for names the TOML already declared and *copy files* that already existed in the checkout. A fresh machine with neither had no move that did not leave the product. Asked whether this should grow into full file editing, the owner chose the recommended boundary: ignored files only.

**Decision.** A person's own hands reach exactly the files the mission record never carries — and no further.

- **Type in a local file.** The setup dialog's files section grows a composer: a path and contents, written into the lane's worktree by the main process — only after git *confirms* it ignores the path (a tracked or un-ignored path is refused in words: it would land in the next diff as nobody's change), under the same shape/containment/never-copy rules as the copy path, always 0600 (person-typed contents are presumed sensitive), overwrite allowed (it is the person's own supplied file). The path is remembered in the machine-local settings' `localFiles`, so the dialog lists it; contents cross the bridge one way and are never read back. **Remove** is the same boundary in reverse: only an ignored file, refused in words otherwise.
- **Declare a secret name from the dialog.** `Add variable` writes the name into `settings.local.toml`'s `secretNames` — machine-local, gitignored, no commit to a friend's repository — and the existing supply flow (D-044) takes the value into OS-encrypted storage from there. The shared file is never touched by this verb; committing a name for the team stays a deliberate settings save.
- **Tracked files stay out of reach, restated as the rule.** The person's hands in Novus touch ignored files only. Changing tracked source is the mission's own attributed work — or the person's own editor on their own checkout (D-159) — never a silent write under the agent.

**Alternatives.** Full in-room file editing (offered, declined by the owner on the recommendation: unattributed human edits inside the mission worktree would make the record lie). An agent-side `declare_secret_name` tool (deferred: the person-side declaration closes the loop, and the agent can already edit `.novus/settings.toml` as ordinary work). Supplying the file into the source checkout as well, so future lanes can copy it (deferred: writing into the person's own checkout is a different consent; named here so it is a decision, not an accident).

**Consequences.** `workspace-files.ts` gains `writeLocalFile`/`deleteLocalFile` beside the copy path, tested against a real git repository (`workspace-files.test.ts`, 17); `workspace.ts` wraps them with the machine-local `localFiles` bookkeeping and adds `addLocalSecretName`; three IPC verbs and bridge methods; the dialog grows the composer, per-row Remove, and the secret-name adder. Proven in a real window (`e2e/local-files.spec.ts`, 1/1): a bare repository with only `.gitignore` and `.env.example`, the secret declared and supplied with its value appearing nowhere, the `.env` written and listed, a tracked path refused in words, and the file removed — evidence `110-own-hands-on-the-workspace.png`.

**Amended 2026-08-28, owner-asked on the first refusal they hit:** "shouldn't it read the .gitignore and tell you the exact path it ignores?" It should — the refusal was withholding an answer the worktree's own `.gitignore` files already held. A refused write now scans them (bounded: depth 3, 20 files, concrete non-glob lines naming the file), verifies each candidate with `git check-ignore` itself, and says "this repository ignores `referral-intake/.env`; write it there" — up to three, alphabetical, or the original plain refusal when nothing matches. Suggestions are only ever git-confirmed paths, so the message can never point somewhere the write would also refuse. Same day, found by the paired-e2e flake: `git check-ignore` failing to run (exit 128 — a held lock, a racing operation) was read as "not ignored", refusing a person over a path git never judged; `ignoredByGit` is tri-state now, retried once, and a git that cannot answer refuses as exactly that (`workspace-files.test.ts` 22, +5 across both).

**Revisit when.** Writing the supplied file through to the person's source checkout earns its own consent flow; the agent should be able to *ask* for a local file or secret by name (a governed request card, not a write); or a second machine joining a mission wants to know which names it still owes values for.

## D-227 — The open file answers: it wears the mission's change in place, and a click lights a name's uses

**Context.** Owner-asked, both halves in one breath: "if i open up the file change right i don't see the code change there — only in the change section; the green changes should be there as well … also i should be able to click stuff in code like methods and the light grey hover over where the methods are used and the method itself for easy clarity." The file view showed the file as bytes and nothing else; what the mission did to it lived only in the Changes surface, and finding a method's uses meant ⌘F.

**Decision.** Two enrichments to the open file, both quiet, neither a gate.

- **The wash (change-in-place).** A non-markdown file open in the room shows the mission's own delta on its lines: lines the mission added or rewrote take the Changes surface's `--diff-add` wash with its inset edge; a removed run leaves a thin `--diff-del` seam above the line that follows. Computed on the machine holding the checkout (`workspace.fileDiff` → `git diff --unified=0` against the lane's **pinned base**, so the wash is "what this mission did", not "what is uncommitted"), with three honesties: a base the checkout no longer holds falls back to HEAD rather than erroring; a gitignored file never wears the wash (a person-supplied `.env` is not the mission's work); and any git failure answers `changed: false` — the wash is enrichment, so its absence is a plain file, never a refusal. A dirty editor drops the wash: edited text shifts the lines it was computed for.
- **The click (a name's uses).** Clicking an identifier in the source view lights every whole-word occurrence of that name in the file with the hover wash — the method and its uses readable at a glance — via the CSS Custom Highlight registry the room's find already uses (`novus-ident`, styled from `--text-3` at wash opacity: orientation, never the find's alarm amber). Clicking it again, clicking a non-name, or Escape clears it. **Lexical on purpose**: same-name-same-file is what a reader scans for, `run` never lights inside `running`, and cross-file resolution is a language server's job — a later decision if it earns one, not a regex pretending.

**Alternatives.** Showing the full diff (old lines interleaved) in the file view (rejected: that is the Changes surface's job; the file view shows the file as it stands, annotated). Hover-driven highlighting (deferred: highlight-on-every-mousemove is churn; click is deliberate). A language server for real references (deferred as above, named in the revisit).

**Consequences.** `workspace-diff.ts` (new: the zero-context hunk parser and the git runner, both tested — parser on git's grammar, whole answer against a real repository with a real pinned base, including the ignored-file and dead-base cases); `FileLineDiffSchema` and `workspace.fileDiff` in the contracts; `WorkspaceTarget` carries the lane's `baseSha`; `Source` in `file-view.tsx` gains the wash classes and the identifier click; DESIGN's file-view entry gains both behaviors. Evidence: `workspace-diff.test.ts` (4), and `e2e/navigation.spec.ts` grown one proof in a real window — a fake turn writes a code file, every line wears the wash, a clicked name registers exactly its two occurrences, Escape clears (`230-file-wears-its-change.png`).

**Revisit when.** A language server arrives for real cross-file references; the wash wants live refresh as turns land (today it is fetched with the read); or the diff base should follow a chosen comparison (against HEAD, against a checkpoint) rather than the lane's birth base.
